# v and h: all the ideas in one place

*Sep 24 2026; brought up to date Sep 27 (new §8h, the open list and the names table) and Sep 28 (new §8i, from building Horizon Defender, Two Standpoints, Blind, Two Strips, Spotter, Landmarks, The Plane, Two Lines and The g Question, Own Time, Common Beat, The Unseen, The Back, Near Order, The Ladder, The Set, The Even Sky, Near Order Tilted and The Ladder in 3d, the rulings R83–R84, and five open items; earlier items are left as
they were, with ⚠ where Sep 27 changes them); and Oct 1 (new §8j: the five situations, the corner and the working horizon, arctan as the shortcut; ⚠ **Oct 1** where they change items above); and Oct 4 (new §8k: every octave a sweep, the level of detail, the ruling check, signs by situation, the three horizons, every octave its own corner, and the priority item on grain; ⚠ **Oct 4** where they change items above); and Oct 5 (new §8l: the lay and the retirement of "grain", breadth and depth as the gift of geometry, depth as the recursion, g as the expression of breadth, and the central assumption that h is the reader; ⚠ **Oct 5** where they change items above). Every idea in the corpus that is about v and h, gathered in one file, from the continuous observer
outward. One idea per item; the source follows each. Nothing here is new. Items marked (record) are quoted from the
session records of papers not in this folder; check them against the papers before citing. The Relation Space items
were checked against the current paper (Part One rewritten Sep 1), which uses **reader (H)** and **writer (V)**, and
**fulcrum** for v = h. *The Unit Rule* (dated Aug 21) and *The Six Things* were checked against the uploaded copies.
Where the papers disagree, the item says so.*

**Rulings, Sep 24 afternoon** (Tom): **the order is v/h** (h underneath, so the horizon is at h = 0); **readers don't
build arches; building is the view from everywhere** (later: **the builder is the writer**; one writer, two types of reader); **depth is z**; the reader's limits are **chosen**; **one facing
per freedom**. Later: **breadth means extent, not quantity**. Also agreed: dividing is what removes the scale, and choosing what goes underneath is what gives a reader
its horizon, so readers divide.

---

## 1. What v and h are

1. **One push, two parts.** v is the part going up, h the part going across. They arrive together, and neither is
   privileged (Tom, Sep 20; *One Reader* §1.1).
2. **Scale-freedom.** Double the push and its direction does not change. The reader keeps the relation of v to h and
   drops their common size (*One Reader* §1.1).
3. **The push is only a way in.** After it, v and h are plain magnitudes; no thrust lines, statics or equilibrium (Tom,
   Sep 20).
4. **Why two, exactly.** At least two, because a lone reading is not an observation; at most two, because a third must be
   stored or manufactured. Relations among three or more are sequences of two (*The Unit Observer*, record). *The
   Relation Space* §26 forces the count under stated conditions: the reading must keep order, vary continuously and
   come from the one direct alignment; a multiplexed stream is a composite, not a primitive.
5. **The relation is not symmetric.** h is the reader and v the writer; the only sentence the pair can build is the
   writer in terms of the reader, "what you write is a third of what I can read". Reader and writer are roles, not
   kinds (*The Relation Space* §2, §16).
6. **Neither part leaves a bounded range; their relation does.** v and h each stay within [0, 1] for the whole run, and
   0 and 1 are each part's own extremes (its own empty and full), not a shared unit. Only v/h runs off (*The Relation
   Space* §16, §18).
6a. **The ray, not the point.** (v, h) and (λv, λh) are the same arrangement; the intrinsic object is the ray, and its
   whole content is v/h (*The Relation Space* §16).
6b. **An amount needs a unit; a ratio does not.** "You are three times me" needs no unit; "three more than me" does.
   So the space is multiplicative (*The Relation Space* §19).
7. **Two senses of the pair.** The relational pair (the bar's attitude) and the register pair (the held slot and the
   arriving slot) are two objects, not two readings of one. Conflating them is the corpus's most frequent error (*The
   Unit Observer*, record). The register pair: "Call the level in hand **the hold**, and the sample being read **the
   arrival**"; the hold is the reader's stand-in, the arrival the writer; the reading is arrival/hold (*The Unit Rule*
   §4). "The first arrival is not a frame; it is the founding of the hold" (§4).
7a. **Two denominators.** Divide by the reader and the result is the pair's reading; divide by the ground and it is an
   amount, which needs a unit (*The Six Things* §1, §6).
7b. **The reversed relation is a second statement.** "The reader describes the writer"; h/v is a second statement,
   "which the first does not contain" (*The Six Things* §18).

## 2. The hold and the sweep

8. **The hold.** Held against their own size, the pair lies on v² + h² = 1 (*One Reader* §1.1).
   ⚠ In *Degrees of Freedom* "the hold" is **h**, the denominator: "h the hold, v the one read against it" (§1); the
   unit circle is "two freedoms sharing one denominator" (§4.2), and "the locus of arrivals that read the same as the
   hold" (§4.3).
9. **An equation is a third party.** v² + h² = 1 lends its terms to both parts; the direct relation is v/h, and it is
   the one with home, the horizon and infinity. In the equation v and h are not relating to each other; each is
   relating to the equation (*The Relation Space* §17). It "admits a third thing at the equals sign" (*The Six
   Things* §6).
10. **The sweep.** A horizontal bar of 1 sweeping to a vertical bar of 1. The angle 0 to π/2 is the full set of relations
    between a horizontal of 1 and a vertical of 1 (Tom, Sep 22; *One Reader* §1.3).
11. **Closure is four sweeps.** Horizontal, vertical, horizontal, vertical, back to horizontal (Tom, Sep 22).
11a. **Facings are rotations between v and h** (Tom, Sep 24). A facing is one quarter rotation of the bar; each swaps
    which of v and h the bar moves away from; closure is four (*One Reader* §1.3):

    ⚠ **Checked against *The Boundary* (Sep 19–22 working log): most of what follows was already tested there, and two of
    the five consequences were killed.** *The Boundary* §9.4 B57 found that "the corpus has one of each under one word":
    **the quarters** (a quarter turn applied repeatedly) form the cyclic group of order four, while **the facings** (which
    term is underneath, a flip) form the Klein group, because "a facing cannot have order 4; it is a choice between two".
    The cyclic four "is about **signs** … an **outside** object"; the Klein four has "no plane, no signs, no page". So Tom's
    statement here, four quarter turns to closure, describes **the quarters**, and the table below is the quarters' table.
    The kill log (B57, B58) then settles two of the consequences listed under it:
    - "the facing does the work a sign would do": **killed as worded.** The four quarters need signs; the two facings do
      not, and "rerun … with every magnitude positive … all four [facing configurations] stay distinct" (B57). The sign
      ruling itself is still unmade: "the corpus currently holds both, in different files" (B324–B325);
    - "a rotation count tells facing 1 from facing 3": **killed.** Recovering the facing from order "imports a walker, and
      nobody is walking a path" (B33 withdrawn, B58); "u and u + π give identical addresses", and a lap "costs an unbroken
      watch — a completed quantifier a serial reader cannot supply" (B75).

    What survives: every other quarter reads as the swap, "measuring the turn from the other end **is** the swap" (B69), and
    the facing is "a selection among freedoms", not a count (B55).

    | facing | the bar | v and h | the reading v/h |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | 1 | horizontal → vertical | h falls to 0, v rises to 1 | 0 → ∞ |
    | 2 | vertical → horizontal | v falls to 0, h rises to 1 | ∞ → 0 (h/v runs 0 → ∞) |
    | 3 | horizontal → vertical | as facing 1 | 0 → ∞ |
    | 4 | vertical → horizontal | as facing 2 | ∞ → 0 |

    - **As magnitudes, facings 1 and 3 are identical, and so are 2 and 4.** What tells them apart is the count of rotations.
      So the facing does the work a sign would do: signed v and h mark the four quarters with ±; magnitudes with facings mark
      the same four with a rotation count. This bears on the open **sign** ruling: a third answer is magnitudes, with the
      facing carrying what the sign would have carried. Unruled.
    - **Every other facing is the swap.** Facing 2 is facing 1 read from the other seat, the reading running as h/v. v/h and
      h/v are consecutive facings, not rival readings. This bears on the open **order** ruling. Unruled.
    - **It fits the sorting by sweeps** (*One Reader* §5.1): an array is one facing; a ring two, meeting itself at the seam; a shape four,
      closed.
    - **It fits the two readers** (item 36a): one rotation swaps, for the outside reader, what it sees across its view and what
      lies in its depth; for the inside reader, v/h and h/v. Both meet a facing as a swap of the two parts' roles.
    - **Ruled, Sep 24: one facing per freedom.** A freedom's facing is its choice of what goes underneath, fixed
      along the freedom; the four quarters are the view from everywhere's drawing of four freedoms joined.
    - **No walker is needed.** A rotation is the bar turning a quarter; this answers Tom's Sep 19 concern that "the facing is
      confusing because no one is actually walking a path."
    - **The Relation Space already has it** (item 13b): four passages from two choices, whose terms and which way; four
      quadrants only on a page; "fullnesses have no sign" (§18). The facing reading of the sign is its reading.
    - ⚠ **Where *The Six Things* differs** (§4, §13, §15): it agrees that only the facing tells two identical traversals
      apart, but has the seats exchange on the **second out-and-back** ("on the doubled run the seats exchange"), not on
      every other quarter, and keeps the sign as a declared extension (item 21). Unruled.
    - ⚠ ***Degrees of Freedom*** keeps explicit signs for closure (§5.1) and has the facing as "a choice between V/H and
      H/V" (§5.2), which agrees with "every other facing is the swap"; but its scripts record "the four facings are Klein
      and the four signed copies cyclic — not the same four" and "order does not recover which term is underneath; the
      claim imported a walker". The second cuts against the rotation count. Unruled.
12. **The curve is a chart.** Every p-ball gives v/h = tan t identically; only the order is the space's own (*The Unit
    Observer*, record). "The circle is a choice": any curve crossing the same values of v/h in the same order is the same
    passage (*The Relation Space* §23).
13. **Symmetric and asymmetric.** Drawn on a page, the run is the quarter turn, the symmetric space: both directions
    open, both ends ordinary. Undrawn, it is [0, ∞), the asymmetric space: one direction, a near end contained and a
    far end approached (*The Relation Space* §17, §18, §24). "Symmetry is purchased with a third thing that leaves its
    terms in the answer. Asymmetry is what reading costs when the only third thing is one that cancels" (§6). Every
    relating has a third party; the spaces differ in what it leaves behind (Corrections).
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** "symmetric" and "asymmetric" now name situations 1 and 2, both views from nowhere. The relation this item calls asymmetric (one direction, [0, ∞), landmarks) is situation 3, the fisheye, which is **neither** (R137; item 351).
13a. **A list of magnitudes is the symmetric account; a serial reading is the asymmetric one.** The list holds the
    figure and cannot state a rate; the serial reading states the rate and cannot hold the figure. "The serial account
    has landmarks, the parallel account has none" (*The Relation Space* §6). Measured: dividing the two components
    cancels the figure outright (0.0000 % spread); keeping the magnitudes keeps the figure and loses the rate (87.6 %)
    (*The Six Things* §8). ⚠ Written before Tom's Sep 22–24 rulings
    that the parallel reader is not the view from everywhere and meets a horizon too; see item 36.
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** a list of magnitudes of a known shape is situation 1 or 2, a view from nowhere (item 351).
13b. **Four passages.** Two choices, whose terms are used and which way the passage runs, give four passages, and they
    exhaust the ways two things can be related. On a page, and only there, they are the four quadrants of one turn. Each
    holds its near end and hands its far end to the next; the meetings alternate home, horizon, home, horizon (*The
    Relation Space* §14, §21, §22). This is item 11a reached without drawing.
14. **The unit circle is demoted.** "Just something the everywhere view can infer." Semicircles are for builders,
    horizons for readers (Tom, Sep 24; *One Reader* §1.2).
    Anticipated in *Degrees of Freedom* ruling 10: "the turn and the circle belong to whoever is describing the reader"
    and ruling 4, "only the unit circle remains — as an idea, not as a drawing"; the quarter turn's angle "is borrowed"
    (§4.3). ⚠ But the same paper also says "the circle is where the reader starts" (§7.9a).
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** settled: **the reader is not the unit circle**; the uniform unit circle is the view from nowhere, situation 1 (R126–R127; item 352). *Degrees of Freedom*'s "the circle is where the reader starts" does not stand.
    ⚠ **Oct 4:** the coarse record of a level-of-detail reader draws as the *chart's* unit curve (a circle in the circle's chart, a square in the square's), not the reader's (SPN Proposition 3.10, corrected; item 365).

## 3. Dividing

15. **The reading.** s = v/h, on the freedom [0, ∞) (*One Reader* §1.2).
16. **The relation before division.** The disagreement, v_A·h_B − v_B·h_A. Sameness is zero disagreement, order is its
    sign; nothing divides until the report, once, at the end. "The reading is the relation served; the disagreement is
    the relation raw". Only the faces of the disagreement, its zero and its sign, are the reader's; its size is not
    (*The Relation Space* §25; *The Six Things* §5). The disagreement is division-free but not scale-blind; compared
    with its own exchange, it vanishes exactly at the fulcrum (*The Six Things* §5).
17. **The divergence is in the division.** A pair turning through h = 0 steps as evenly there as anywhere; only the
    quotient runs away. "The natural world does not divide by zero" (*Keeping the Horizon* §0, §5, record).
18. **An unmatched division is where an account shows through.** What a pair owns are quantities in which every division
    is matched; an unmatched one moves when the figure is restated, or runs off the edge of its chart (*Keeping the
    Horizon* §0, record).
19. **The projection removes the scale.** Dividing cancels every magnitude and leaves a uniform array of addresses; the
    world is not uniform (Tom, Sep 24; *One Reader* §1.6).
20. **The swap.** h/v is the same relation from the other seat. **The order is ruled: v/h** (Tom, Sep 24): h goes
    underneath, and the horizon is where h runs out. Dividing removes the scale; choosing what goes underneath is what
    gives the reader its horizon.
21. **The sign.** *The Relation Space* §18: "V/H is never negative. Fullnesses have no sign." The four quadrants appear
    only on a page, as four oriented passages (§21). Whether the corpus keeps this, or takes signed v and h (with an
    anti-corner at v = −h, *The Anchor*), is the open sign ruling. *The Six Things* §15 takes the sign on as a declared
    extension of the reading: the anti-fulcrum "is equality met in the reversed facing. It requires the signed reading
    and is not an arrangement in the positive part of the space". The sign of the disagreement is the order (§5).
    ⚠ **Oct 4 (R173):** in situation 3, v and h are magnitudes and the facing carries direction (R37); in situations 1 and 2, the views from nowhere, the pair may be signed, its signs being the quarters of the full turn. The anti-corner and the harmonic lock belong there (item 366).

## 4. The landmarks

22. **Home**, (v, h) = (0, 1): the writer empty, the reader entire; "home is where nothing is written" (*The Relation
    Space* §10, §16). Where the reader lays its unit down (Tom, Sep 7). The writer at rest (*The Unit Rule* §7, record).
    ⚠ *Degrees of Freedom* §5.6: "home and the horizon are one place with two names"; past its own horizon a reader
    holds "one address containing everything".
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** home is no h's, v/h = 0: where the sweep starts. It bounds nothing; the corner is what bounds the front side (R149; item 359). Situations 1 and 2 have no home (R131, R139).
23. **The corner, or fulcrum**, v = h: the one reading that is its own reverse, v/h = h/v = 1. Equality alone names a
    ray; intrinsically the fulcrum is the locus of swap-invariance, and on the page's census it is equality **at half
    fullness**, v² = h² = ½, so v = h = 1/√2, never ½. "The reader only knows the match; the page records the half"
    (*The Relation Space* §5, §20, Corrections). Part One's share chart, s = v/(v + h), puts it at s = ½: a different ½,
    the share of the sum (§16). "The equality is the relation's; the 1/√2 is the account's" (*The Six Things* §7). The
    value is "1/√2 in each party, never ½. The half is in the squared share" (§14).
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** "fulcrum" is the older name for the corner. The 1/√2 address is on the circle, and the circle (situation 1) has no corner (R139). In situation 3 the corner is one h, known though v's breadth is not (R149; item 359).
    ⚠ **Oct 4 (R174):** the corner is **the near horizon** (item 367).
23a. **The fulcrum is approached, never reached, as a reading.** Every share and every count goes trivial there, so no
    reading rests on it: an interior horizon. The amount v = h is reached and passed through; the reader certifies the
    crossing as an event, not a position (*The Relation Space* §10, §20). ⚠ **The papers disagree here.** *The Six
    Things* has the fulcrum received as a reading: "the single arrangement at which the reading is 1, received once,
    self-certified" (§12); "the reading is 1, the count of steps is 0" (§14). *The Unit Rule* §7 has the parties "at the
    corner, not near it". Only the event of crossing is common to all three.
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** R149 takes the reached reading: the corner is reached when v reaches one h, and it is the edge of the working horizon (item 359). *The Relation Space*'s "interior horizon" now collides with "working horizon".
23b. **The symmetric space has no fulcrum.** On the drawing, v = h is an unremarkable midpoint with congruent arcs either
    side. The fulcrum needs the unequal arms, [0, 1] against [1, ∞), which only the reading has (*The Relation Space*
    §20, Corrections).
24. **The corner is the one mark.** "A scale with no marks can order; a scale with one mark can locate." It is the only
    mark a two-party relation can draw for itself, anchored by reciprocal agreement (*The Relation Space* §9).
25. **The only sentence both parties can speak.** At the fulcrum the two seats' accounts are identical, so agreement costs
    nothing (*The Anchor* §5, record). "The only place that calibrates itself"; "the fulcrum names nothing" (*The
    Relation Space* §20).
26. **The unit changes hands, and the reader cannot hand it over.** Below the fulcrum the reading is a share of the
    reader (proportional, [0, 1]); above it the reader keeps itself as the unit, stops dividing and counts (a tally,
    then a logarithm, [1, ∞)). "Before the fulcrum, the relation space is proportional in terms of the reader. After
    the fulcrum, it is logarithmic in terms of the writer." The relation forces the logarithm; the base belongs to
    whatever does the stepping (*The Relation Space* §9, §19). The bucket and the water (§9). ⚠ *The Six Things* §14
    counts in the reader's own unit, "how many of the reader's own reaches the writer exceeds", not "in terms of the
    writer". *The Unit Rule* §7 puts it as the angle: below 45° "the angle IS the proportion"; above it the angle from
    the horizon is the reciprocal proportion; "the denominator changes hands at 45°".
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** **ruled** (R149–R150; items 359–360). Proportional before the corner, because the front side has two known ends (home and the corner) and is swept from home in plain proportion, n/C; logarithmic after, counted by doublings, because the back side has no end. *The Unit Rule*'s register is logarithmic on both sides (item 350).
27. **The agreement point is the relation at rest**: the two coming and going at the same rate. A pair locked there is
    symmetric by its own act, and has no landmark: "the corner is a landmark only of a relation that can move". The
    reader seeks agreement and the writer is oblivious (*The Relation Space* §6; *The Unit Rule* §7). ⚠ *The Unit Rule*
    calls this resting point **the corner** ("it is the corner that rests, not home"), meaning arrival = hold; its
    fulcrum is the relation's equality, which "the register's corner sits on … only when the held value does" (§7).
27a. **Three rests.** "**Home** is where the writer is at rest; the **horizon** is where the reader is at rest; the
    **corner** is where the reader and the writer do not move away from each other." "A reader that is reading lives
    at the corner, not at home" (*The Unit Rule* §7).
28. **The horizon**, (v, h) = (1, 0): the writer entire, the reader's eyes closed; "the horizon is where nothing is
    read". Approached, never reached, because a statement cannot survive the extinguishing of its own unit. Infinity is
    not an arrangement but "the name for the growing" (*The Relation Space* §4, §10, §11; *Keeping the Horizon* §2,
    record).
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** with h the known side by definition (R146), the horizon is v beyond any count of h's: the same ray, v/h → ∞, with the failure on v's side, not the reader's eyes closing. Not the working horizon, which is the front side (R149; item 359).
    ⚠ **Oct 4 (R174):** this is **the mathematical horizon**, never reached. The **far horizon** is where the reader's addresses run out, and there it carries into the next octave. The **near horizon** is the corner. Unqualified, "horizon" is the common word; name the one meant (item 367).
28b. **The horizon is where addresses become indistinguishable** (*Degrees of Freedom* §5.6); "an address no arrival
    reaches" (ruling 6). In serial it is where no further hanger can be hung, about 2N/π out (§5.16).
28c. ⚠ **Two landmarks, not three?** *The Boundary* kill log (§9.3 B52): "Home, the corner and the horizon are three
    landmarks on the line" → "two. Home and the horizon are one place exchanged by the facing." With *Degrees of
    Freedom* §5.6, "one place with two names". The corpus, this list included, still says three. **Ruled (R88, Sep 29):
    three, of two kinds** — the corner, and home with the horizon as one pair exchanged by the facing. ⚠ **Oct 4:** R88 confirmed; the horizon it pairs with home is the mathematical horizon (items 367, 370).
28a. **Finer steps do not recover the sizes.** A reader stepping more finely learns more exactly where and when the pair
    stands, never how much there is of either (*The Relation Space* §15).
29. **The harmonic lock.** Home, corner, horizon and anti-corner have cross ratio −1 at every size. Equality alone gives
    the lock; half-fullness gives the origin and the measure (*The Anchor* §3, record).
30. **The rung distance is a classical metric.** Its log is the cross ratio against home and horizon: the Cayley–Klein
    metric with the ends as the absolute (Aug 22, record).

## 5. The reader's moves

31. **Two registers.** The angle register (θ = arctan(v/h), the compose move) and the log register (z = ln(v/h), the
    ratio move) (*One Reader* §1.4). *The Unit Rule* §7 names three coordinates, related by the tangent and not to be
    conflated: log r, the register's; θ, the reader's screen; rapidity, the dial's needle.
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** θ = arctan(v/h) is the shortcut, not the sweep (R148; item 358).
31a. **The ladder is one corner repeated.** "Each rung is the corner again, at another scale"; "counting corners is
    taking a logarithm". Any discrete company of rescalings is the powers of one generator, so the geometric ladder is
    the only discrete design (*The Unit Rule* §5). The base is the reader's grain (*The Six Things* §16).
    ⚠ **Oct 4 (R172, R175):** every octave with two facings (×4, ½c to 2c) is itself a sweep, with its own corner at 4ⁿh. "Grain" is a priority open item (item 371).
31b. **2/π at the fulcrum.** The lens's log–log slope at the fulcrum is exactly 2/π (*The Unit Rule* §7).
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** the 2/π divides by the whole quarter turn, out to a horizon a situated reader never reaches (R148). The ψ₁ paper is archived.
32. **Together they make the group.** The compose and ratio moves generate PSL(2,ℝ), and the moves are exactly the maps
    that keep cross ratios (*One Reader* §2.1a).
33. **The Cauchy is forced.** It is the only law the reader's moves cannot leave (*One Reader* §2.2; Knight, Letac,
    McCullagh).
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** forced in situation 3 (R138). In situation 2, laid on the circle's even turn, a shape of breadth ratio b reads as a Cauchy of scale b (R147; item 355).

## 6. The two readers

34. **The inside reader holds v/h; the outside reader holds v.** Same sweep; one divides, the other does not (*One
    Reader* §6.4).
35. **Inside = serial = Cauchy = fisheye = the unit rule** (Tom, Sep 24). A serial reader has no unit, so it must divide,
    and its clock meets the turn evenly.
    ⚠ *Degrees of Freedom* §2.1: "A freedom has a unit; its magnitude list has none. The corner is that unit",
    correcting "the reader has no unit". And §5.18: the serial reader "receives neither" v nor h, only the motion;
    the pair arrives at O4 (item 37).
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** the fisheye is situation 3, the only view from somewhere (R137; item 351).
36. **Outside = parallel = arcsine = reverse fisheye (provisional) = the kept frame.** It sees the whole shape against one
    frame, so it keeps the size (*One Reader* §6.4).
    ⚠ Not in *Degrees of Freedom*: there parallel hangers are "equal and uniform" in the turn, the same hangers the
    serial reader sees Cauchy-arranged, and the parallel account has "no home, no fulcrum and no horizon" (§5.15,
    §5.16). The arcsine outside reader with a rim is from Sep 24.
36a. **v and h are different things to the two readers** (Tom, Sep 24). To the inside reader they are two parts of one
    arrival, even-handed, size dropped. To the outside reader v is a position across its view and h is the foreshortening,
    the depth along its line of sight, which a side-on view discards; it recovers h only as foreshortening, the hΔ spacing
    behind its 1/h crowding. So the outside reader has nothing to divide by, and the inside reader no size to keep
    (*One Reader* §6.4):

    | | **inside** (at the centre) | **outside** (far away, looking side-on) |
    |---|---|---|
    | **v** | one part of the arrival: how far across from the focus | a position across its view, which it sees |
    | **h** | the other part of the arrival: how far toward the focus | the foreshortening: the depth along its line of sight, which it cannot see |
    | their size | dropped; only the relation is kept | kept, against its frame |
    | what it can hold | v/h | v directly; h only indirectly |
    | as h → 0 | the reading runs off to infinity | the surface turns edge-on: the rim |

    ~~*Clue for 3D, not a claim:* to the outside reader h is depth, and "the 1d signal is where depth comes from, it is
    the z" (Tom, Sep 20); each reader supplies what the other cannot see.~~ **Withdrawn by ruling (Tom, Sep 24): depth is z.** What
    the outside reader recovers from h is its tilt, not its depth. ⚠ *Degrees of Freedom* §5.19 rules depth as z, the log
    radius, with x and y carrying direction only; h sits on the direction side. The foreshortening here is a third
    sense of depth. *The Boundary* B276, B319 make v the depth ("a point sits at height v with offset
    h"), and B69 calls the labels "one choice wearing three names". *The Centring* §1a supports the clue: the outside reader's gaps "give how squarely
    the surface faces it, and that gives depth".
37. **The serial ladder.** The floor observer holds only the move, one magnitude over the last; v and h together arrive at
    the fourth rung, O4, and with them the reading and the corner test (`serial-watch.xlsx`).
38. **Two horizons from one point.** Both meet h = 0. The outside reader meets it as a rim at v = 1, where everything
    crowds and saturates; the inside reader as an infinity it never reaches. Near it, the outside reader's distance to
    its rim is about 1/(2s²) (*One Reader* §6.4; this session).
39. **Readers need horizons.** A picture with no horizon, such as the Roman arch, has no readers in it, only the math
    (Tom, Sep 24; *One Reader* §5.4).
40. **The stitch.** Two readers, neither holding N, join at the horizon they share (*One Reader* §5.3).
41. **Where the pair runs out.** The outside reader is blind where h·ds < ε, the inside reader where Δ/(vh) < d; the pair
    first loses a hanger where h²v/κ = ε/d (*One Reader* §6.1).
42. **Reading is seeking.** A ratio-only reader reads every writer if and only if its hold tracks the writer within its
    register's reach, which the paper glosses as returning to the corner; a predicting rule is a seeker too (*The Unit
    Rule* §7).
42a. **The side bit.** A hold stepping toward the short side settles on the median, not the mean; "the fulcrum is what
    the drift is pointing at" (*The Unit Rule* §7, §18).
42b. **The turn.** "When the magnitudes change direction, a quarter turn has occurred": a landmark that costs no unit.
    One seen turn with the corner fixes the phase and the count, not the schedule or the magnitudes. "The system's turns
    beyond the horizon cannot be known" (*The Unit Rule* §7).
42c. **Every rule is quiet on the shape it believes in, and its corner is the landmark of that shape** (*The Unit Rule*
    §14a).
42d. **A lens does not widen the view.** "The back side is invisible to the reader, and a fisheye does not change that"
    (*The Unit Rule* §7).

## 7. Density

*⚠ Sep 30 (R124): the word "density" is dropped as a framework term. Here it means the lens's spacing of addresses; the Cauchy's "probability density" keeps its name.*
*⚠ Oct 4: octaves within octaves are called **nesting** (R172); "density" stays only in Tom's quotes (item 364).*

43. **The lens is the density.** Its slope: h² inside (the Cauchy), vh in the log register, 1/h outside (the arcsine),
    1/h² for the dial (*One Reader* §6.4). Found earlier through the register: a rung's cell on the screen is the unit
    times cos²θ, which "is the Cauchy" (*The Unit Rule* §7).
44. **Focus and unit are the Cauchy's location and scale; dimension gives h^(n+1) inside and leaves 1/h outside**
    (*One Reader* §6.4).
45. **ε is the pixel, not a shift;** how coarse is set by εN. Noise is what bends the curve (*One Reader* §6.4).
46. **The corner rule.** A serial reader with no N normalises at the corner (v = h holds half the turn) and mirrors by the
    swap; it forecasts its lap, gN, half a lap before closure confirms it (`cornerrule.mjs`; *One Reader* §6.4).
    ⚠ Already in *Degrees of Freedom* §5.19a: "the corner is crossed at arrival N/2, so N = 2m exactly — half the cost
    of closure", **but only where the corner is unique**: a wandering signal crossing 1 twenty-five times gives "25
    answers between 4 and 122". `cornerrule.mjs` tested steady turns only.
    ⚠ **Oct 1:** "v = h holds half the turn" holds only for b = 1 (R142, R147). R149 reaches the same corner by sweeping to it: the corner rule anticipated the working horizon (item 359).

## 8. The same pair under other names

47. **An array on the freedom.** v is the count, h the unit; the reading is i/u (*One Reader* §6.2).
45a. **The writer draws the shape on the page, and that creates both the array of v/h and the scale** (Tom, Sep 24).
    One act, two products: the readings, one per index, and the size the drawing was made at. The inside reader keeps
    the array of v/h and drops the scale; the outside reader keeps the scale. A reader without the scale has the shape
    "one number short" (*The Centring* §5c): the missing number is the writer's scale.
45b. **The writer knows N and holds the blueprint; the reader never knows N and uses the Cauchy, the pure mathematical
    blueprint** (Tom, Sep 24: "This is just the writer situation. It is the blueprint. Reader never knows N. They use
    cauchy." "The cauchy is a type of blueprint, but it's a pure mathematical blueprint."). The writer's blueprint is an
    instance of the Cauchy. What of the writer's figure reaches a reader is the residue, the shape one number short
    (*The Centring* §5a, §5c); the circle leaves none.
45c. **The arch metaphor is demoted** (Tom, Sep 24: "Demote the arch metaphor. It does not hold up to scrutiny."). The
    Roman arch, its centring, stones and striking (*The Centring*; items 71–73; *One Reader* §5.4) are no longer used
    to explain the writer's situation. (Earlier the same afternoon: "the scaffold is not scale"; "normally the frame
    remains; the Roman arch is unusual in that there scaffolding can be removed"; "Roman arch is just a variation of the
    writer situation". Superseded by the demotion.)
46a. **The array** (Tom, Sep 24: "List implies lifo fifo"; "an array is indexed by definition"). **The magnitude list is anything that can be indexed** (Tom, Sep 24): an index in, a magnitude out. "These are all
    the same things at their core, just a collection of things that can be indexed." The reader turns the index into
    an address; the list turns it into a magnitude; they meet only at i. So anything plugs in: an array, a shape,
    physics, a system, a lazy list, addresses to another list. In the rigorous paper those examples go at the end, to
    show it works universally.
46a′. **The array never comes back to the reader: proof by contradiction** (Tom, Sep 24: "If the magnitude list was
    returned to the reader, its signal would be interleaved with the motion signal"; "the signal would not be uniform
    and we know that it is"). On one register, a size and a step look the same. If the reader can sort them, it holds the
    scale that dividing removed; if it can't, its signal loses uniformity, depends on the writer's scale and stops
    closing. Premise: one register (a tag bit is a second freedom, the outside reader's). Tested on seven shapes
    (`interleave.mjs`, all five predictions held): pure streams are scale-free, uniform (D = 2/N), balanced at the
    corner and closed; interleaved streams fail all four. Uniformity is the leg that catches every case (the unit
    circle at scale 1 still closes). Also found: laid by share, **the reading stream is the same for every shape**
    (tan θᵢ); the shape reaches the reader as **motion** rᵢ₊₁/rᵢ. Readings are addresses, motions are the shape,
    magnitudes are the writer's. The rigorous paper's Proposition 3.
46a″. **No indexes: the freedom is a sweep; the array is a metaphor** (Tom, Sep 24: "Indexes are a computer science
    concept, they are not needed. The freedom is a sweep, not an index."; "Arrays are fine, but it requires a floor
    operation, it is lossy, removing the decimal. Its a metaphor only."). The reader has a place on the sweep, which is
    continuous. An index is that place divided by the pixel ε with the decimal dropped: a floor. The dropped part is
    what the resolution horizon is made of, and N is what the floor makes of the sweep. So Proposition 3 becomes **the
    sweep is the only contact** (the proof never used the index). Supersedes the "index" wording in 46a and 46a′.
46a‴. **One sweep; the writer is free** (Tom, Sep 24: "It's the same sweep, the writer does the math though. He is
    free to arrange the signal however he chooses, nothing forced"). The semicircle (known length) and the freedom
    (unknown length) are one sweep divided two ways: evenly in the turn or evenly in the reading. The division is the
    writer's math and is not forced. This answers the review's "layouts defined, not derived" on the writer's side.
    Open 0 moves to the reader's side: the reader never sees the writer's arrangement, so why the Cauchy? Refines 47a.
46a⁗. **Dividing leaves only the Cauchy** (Tom, Sep 24: "The cauchy emerges when you divide away the scale. Its the
    only thing that emerges. We can show that easily."). h = R cos θ, v = R sin θ, so v/h = tan θ: the size R is gone,
    whatever it was, and a bar swept evenly gives the Cauchy. A linear move of the pair keeps the Cauchy and only shifts
    its focus and spread. Tested (`divide.mjs`, predictions registered first, all held): five size laws × three scales
    all standard Cauchy with identical readings; three moves each on its predicted member; the square spread by length
    is not Cauchy (the writer's arrangement), the circle is. **The readings are the Cauchy plus the writer's residue.**
    Closes Open 0 on the reader's side: the reader uses the Cauchy because it is what dividing leaves, not by choice.
    The rigorous paper's Proposition 7a.
46a⁵. **How the projective theory is forced** (discussed Sep 24; Tom: "Yes"). (1) Pushes up to size *are* the
    projective line, and v/h is its coordinate: forced by dividing. (2) A linear move of the pair is a Möbius move of the
    reading, and swap, compose and ratio are the parts of any linear move (turn, stretch, turn): not a menu. (3) One
    premise left: a move acts on the reading as a linear move of the pair does. The Cauchy tells them apart
    (`moves.mjs`, `moves2.mjs`): linear moves keep it on the predicted member; warps outside the Möbius motions miss at
    first order, warps along them only at second order. First-run kills logged (the sin 2θ warps are first-order
    Möbius; a sin θ warp is not a move of the reading). Not claimed: that only Möbius maps keep the Cauchy (false in
    general: Boole's ½(x − 1/x)); whether it holds for one-to-one moves is to be checked in Letac 1977. The rigorous
    paper's §3.0.
46a⁶. **Three even sweeps** (from the third review, translated onto the sweep; Tom: "ok"). An even sweep is one of the
    reader's moves run continuously, and there are exactly three kinds (standard: elliptic, parabolic, hyperbolic),
    told apart by fixed places on the reading line: **the turn** (the share, the semicircle): none, so closure and no
    horizon; **the shift** (the fixed step, the dial): one, the horizon; **the stretch** (the ladder gⁱ): two, home and
    the horizon. Swept evenly they give the Cauchy, a uniform reading, a uniform log reading (`sweeps.mjs`, all held).
    The kind survives any change of standpoint; the fixed places move with it. Random moves are turns or stretches,
    never exactly a shift (it has to be chosen). So the writer is free, among exactly three kinds; the freedom-vs-ladder
    disagreement in the corpus is two of the three kinds, not a conflict. The rigorous paper's §2.2.
46a⁷. **The geometry is the addressing scheme, not the payload** (Tom, Sep 25: "the interesting thing is that the
    geometry is not the payload, it is just the addressing scheme"; "geometry provides the continuous sweep over the
    payload. discrete consumption is possible with indexing."). A reading v/h is an address; the magnitude drawn there
    is the payload; dividing keeps the address and drops the size. Supported by: the reading stream is the same for
    every shape (`interleave.mjs`); the Cauchy is what dividing leaves whatever the sizes (Prop 7a); share, step and
    ladder are the three even ways to lay addresses (three even sweeps). The payload reaches the reader only as motion.
    A shape = closure (the addressing's) + residue (the payload's). Indexing = discrete consumption, a lossy floor.
    The rigorous paper's opening thesis; it is retitled *Geometry as Addressing* (provisional).
    **Indexing is at the reader's ε** (Tom, Sep 25: "the indexing can be done at the users epsilon"): each reader
    floors the same sweep at its own chosen grain; addresses and payload are unchanged, only the fineness of
    consumption, and with it the count and the limb count (εN), are the reader's.
46a⁸. **You sweep; you can't point** (Tom, Sep 25: "geometry is a sweep, you start at home and sweep to where you
    want to go. you can't actually just point at where you want to go."; "there is no way to just point at something
    on the sweep."). Worked out Sep 25: the bar's state is (v, h); geometry gives a place, an order (the sign of the
    disagreement) and the landmarks, never a distance along the sweep. **An address is where the sweep has got to.**
    Walker test (*The Boundary* B33, B57, B58, B75): where the bar is is **state**, no tally; how many times round is
    **history**, and the walker returns there. So the ruling holds up to the lap. Pointing (random access) is the
    writer's. The stretch fixes home, so a ladder starts at the corner (reading, to check). On the turn the bar reaches
    h = 0; only the reading never arrives. **Ruled (Tom, Sep 25): "No, you can't point, the boundary is wrong… you
    can algorithimically point, but that is not an O(1) operation."** *The Boundary* §2's "inside is pointing" is
    withdrawn as worded. Pointing is an O(1) jump; algorithmic pointing (e.g. CORDIC, a fast sweep one digit per step)
    costs log2(1/ε) + 0 to 2 steps, measured (`pointing*.mjs`); the corner is the first CORDIC turn, passed in one
    step, but the loop cannot tell it has arrived. The rigorous paper's §1.5 and rulings 17–19.
46a⁹. **Arctan is engineering, not geometry** (Tom, Sep 25: "arctan is not geometric it is algoritmic."; "arctan is the
    point of contact between the continuous sweep and a discrete point on that sweep. but it is engineering and must be
    calculated, its not geometric."). Arctan turns a place into a number of turn, which a floor needs to name a discrete
    point; it is computed, not drawn. What survives without it: compose as (s + t)/(1 − s·t) (*The Boundary* B16);
    order; the corner; halving, v/(1 + h) on the hold, which puts the Cauchy's quartiles 0.4142 and 2.4142 at the
    bisections of the corner. So the dyadic places are geometric and other even divisions are the calculation. The
    square root (Tom, Sep 25: "is it needed for the sweep or just the reading of the sweep?"): **the reading's, not the
    sweep's.** In the sweep, halving is adding two bars of 1, (1, 0) + (h, v), a rhombus; from the reading alone the
    size must be computed back, s/(1 + √(1 + s²)), and at the corner the answer √2 − 1 is irrational. Arctan recovers a
    count of turn; the root recovers the bar's size. `halving.mjs`, all held. Marks the angle
    register (item 31) and the turn densities (item 43) as the describer's calculation.
46a¹⁰. **Access to the stop makes v and h symmetric** (Tom, Sep 25, 05:03 "the moment she stops, N is defined";
    05:06 "the moment the reader has access to the stoping point, its a semi-circle not a freedom"; 05:10 "its not so
    much as the writer stopping puts you on a semi-circle, its that V and H are now symetric, where they were
    previously asymetric."). The writer may stop; what matters is the reader's access to the stop. Access closes the far
    end, and a space with both ends contained is the symmetric (resolved) space (items 13, 13a; *The Six Things* §7:
    "resolved when its range contains both of its ends"; *The Relation Space* §6). The semicircle is its drawing, not
    its cause. So known/unknown length (47a) is symmetric/asymmetric, decided by access. Fits *The Boundary* B183–B188
    (handed the magnitudes, the anchor and N arrive together) and *The Relation Space* §1 (a completed system is taken
    in whole). Caution: *The Six Things* §7 says the far end "is reached by a change of account, never by further
    sweeping", which agrees with ruling 19 (no pointing).
46a¹¹. **Finished is discrete; unfinished is the sweep** (Tom, Sep 25, 05:15: "a reader can know a finished array
    [0,1,2,3]. they can know its finished. its still a type of reading. maybe, when you know the complete array its no
    longer a continuous sweep, but a discrete array."; confirmed 05:17). Unfinished (asymmetric): continuous sweep, no
    N, no pointing, floor needed, horizon, rate stated. Finished (symmetric): discrete array, N known, O(1) pointing, no
    floor, no horizon, no rate. Still reading in both. Scopes 46a″ ("the array is a metaphor") to the sweep. Open: the
    Sep 22 ruling (no reader knows N) holds for readers of the sweep; who reads a finished array? The rigorous paper's
    D8a and ruling 20c.
46a¹². **The writer builds discrete things** (Tom, Sep 25, 05:28: "the writer is the builder of discrete things. yes,
    she may do that continuously. but the things themselves are discrete."). And, the same message, "is any of this
    phillosophy necessary for this paper": no. The rigorous paper was cut to the reader (A3: complete or not; D8a);
    the writer material now lives in `writer-notes.md`.
46a¹³. **Reader geometry is addressing; the rest of geometry is addressing plus a unit** (Tom, Sep 25: "Is all of
    geometry addresses or just reader geometry"; agreed "Yes" to the scope). Test: multiply v and h by one factor.
    Unchanged (direction, order, landmarks, cross ratios, the scale-free Cayley–Klein metric) is address; changed
    (length, area, radius) needs a unit and is payload. ⚠ Corrected the same morning (Tom, 07:42: "The unit circle by
    definition has no scale."): the resolved account closes the far end but its 1 is each party's own whole, not a
    scale, so the unit circle is still address. Sizes come only with an anchor (the "one number short"). Two
    additions: completeness (still address) and an anchor (scale, payload). The rigorous paper is retitled *Reader Geometry as Addressing*.
46a¹⁴. **The view from nowhere; one, two, four facings** (Tom, Sep 25: 08:55 "the unit circle is a view from
    everywhere. Readers have a view from somewhere, and that is cauchy."; 09:03 "Both unit circles and cauchy are
    unitless and scale free, they contain no values, they sweep systems with values. If you can see all four facings,
    you have a view from nowhere. If you have a view from somewhere, you have two facings."; 09:10 "Nowhere seem better.
    An array has only one facing"). **Renamed: the view from everywhere → the view from nowhere.** An array has one
    facing, a reader from somewhere two (the ring; the standard Cauchy is an even half turn), the view from nowhere four
    (the shape closed). Answers the rigorous paper's §7.1 "why four": from the reader's side it is two. Bears on the
    sign ruling: the second facing needs the signed reading or the swap.
46a¹⁵. **A facing is a direction of looking, one per degree of freedom** (Tom, Sep 25, 09:17: "the second facing is the
    second degree of freedom, you can left or right. The fourth freedom, is just four degrees of freedom, you can look
    left right forwards and backwards. A shere just adds up an down."). The second facing is a second freedom facing the
    other way, not a sign on a magnitude (fits *The Boundary* B55). Points toward magnitudes on the sign ruling. The sphere is
    LEFT OPEN (Tom, 09:22: "Someone just needs to map all this out. Leave the sphere open."): directions 6, sweeps 12,
    sign-corners 8 (2n, 2n(n−1), 2ⁿ); all three are 4 in the plane.
46b. **A shape is an array with four facings and the complete set of h relative to v** (Tom, Sep 24). The same indexed
    list, laid over four facings (four freedoms joined, one facing each) until it holds every relation of h to v: closure
    (Tom, Sep 20, *Degrees of Freedom* §5.18a). Array, ring and shape are one object at one, two and four facings
    (*One Reader* §5.1).
47a. **Known length: the semicircle; unknown length: the freedom** (Tom, Sep 24). A known length lays each entry by share,
    θ = (π/2)(i/N): both ends contained, no horizon, relative addresses. An unknown length lays each by a unit, v/h = i/u:
    a horizon, absolute addresses. Length is the count N, not the breadth. A reader never knows N, so a reader is on the
    freedom; the semicircle is the builder's. The magnitude list's length decides which geometry it plugs into
    (*One Reader* §6.2).
48. **Special relativity.** v is β, h is 1/γ, the reading is celerity βγ, and the hold β² + 1/γ² = 1 is the definition
    of γ (*One Reader* §4.2).
48a. **The arrangement as a body.** The party supplying the terms drawn as width, the party described as height; the
    fulcrum is "a square with no measurements"; the extents "owe each other nothing" (*The Six Things* §12).
49. **Two settings.** The folding census (squared shares summing to one, the circle) meets v = h once, at the corner; the
    saturating census (the unit hyperbola) never meets it, so there the corner becomes the horizon (*The Two Settings*
    §3, record). Also *The Six Things* §9: on a census that conserves a difference, "the fulcrum is displaced to the far
    end".
50. **Time.** The present as the corner, where h and v are always equal: the diagonal (Tom, Sep 10). Corrected by Tom: if
    the arriving value never changes, "it's not a diagonal, it's a straight line" (*Riding the Corner*, record).

## 8a. From *Degrees of Freedom*

*Checked Sep 24 against the uploaded copy. Items not already above.*

51. **A degree of freedom is v in terms of h, at expression g**: an address on [0, ∞), scale-invariant, "an address to
    a magnitude rather than the magnitude itself" (header, §1). The address is an equivalence class with no size;
    magnitude comes back only with the hold, v = s·h (ruling 1). arctan s is "a parametrisation … not a second address".
52. ⚠ *(Sep 27: Tom proposes g is the depth; see §8h, item 131.)* **g, the expression, is a density** (Tom, Sep 18): "the share of the freedom's hangers that are placed … at g = 1
    all N of them, at g = 0 none" (§1). Purely the reader's: "there is no g error anywhere, because there could not be"
    (§5.15g). "Closure quantises g to multiples of 2/N"; "gN is a subset, not a count" (§5.15b). In serial "g does not
    punch holes in the signal, it multiplies it" (§5.18). g was called the unit radius until Sep 18.
53. **The marks.** 0: the numerator has gone absent; 1: "the two stand equal — the corner"; ∞: the denominator has gone
    absent. "Finite in extent and infinite in density" (§1).
54. **A freedom has a unit, and it is the corner.** "A freedom has a unit; its magnitude list has none" (§2.1).
55. **Composition.** Freedoms sharing a denominator compose by dividing, c = s₁/s₂, and the hold cancels,
    "constitutive but non-retentive"; the identity is the corner, the inverse is the parties swapped (§3). Dividing
    cancels what is shared; multiplying cancels what is private (§4.1). Composition stops where one address is 0 and
    the other ∞ (§6).
56. **The semicircle is two freedoms joined at their horizons**: v non-negative, h either sign; "the seam is the
    middle"; the swap "has determinant −1 … not reachable by doing" (§5.2).
57. **A freedom's own measure is ln(b/a)**, essentially unique; "the angle measures a freedom's extent … its own
    measure counts its addresses"; "it is the grain" (§5.7).
58. **Dividing, then counting.** "A freedom is not counted; it is swept" (§5.5).
59. **The Cauchy is an address to a centre of gravity**: where the thing sits relative to the reader's own hold; its
    median is the corner, its quartiles 0.4142 and 2.4142 an inverse pair; "undistorted at the hold and nowhere else"
    (§5.1). The fisheye is logarithmic "at one point only, and that point is the corner" (§7.4).
60. **Closure by counting sign flips**: four copies, "the same event with the roles swapped" at each seam; it
    generalises to 2ⁿ (§5.1). "The turn closes by counting … never closes by distance" (§5.13).
61. **The breadth is the world, the density is the reader** (Tom, Sep 21, §5.15g), measured with a control reader.
    ⚠ There breadth is the world's **range** and "N belongs to neither … it is the place they meet"; on Sep 24 breadth is
    **N**. **Ruled (Tom, Sep 24): breadth means extent, not quantity.** The Sep 21 sense stands; N is the count.
62. **The lens is what density means once the breadth is fixed**: du/dx = d/(x² + d²), "It **is** the fisheye, written
    out" (§5.15i). The kernel belongs to **the setup**, fixed by a breadth and where the facing is; g only decides how
    many places go on "a lens that was already shaped" (§5.15n).
63. **The even frame is not forced; it is what poverty can build**: "the only one a reader can build from N alone"
    (§5.15k). "A frame cannot lift itself by its own readings" (§5.15l).
64. ⚠ *(Sep 27: depth reassigned to the forward sweep, the parallel reader's, R79; see §8h.)* **Serial and parallel are two signals.** "We project serial and count parallel" (Tom, Sep 20). In serial "the values
    never arrive — the motion does" (§5.18). 2d is closure, "a symmetry about the corner" (§5.18a). Depth is z, the log
    radius; "3d is just the motion of 2d"; "3d is consumed as 2d images of 1d slices" (§5.19).

## 8b. From *The Centring*

*Checked Sep 24 against the uploaded copy (Sep 17, revised Sep 18 and Sep 22). Items not already above.*

65. **The own-size reading.** A reader that brought nothing reads each arrival against the arrival's own size:
    (v, h)/size = (cos u, sin u). The magnitude divides out and a direction remains (§1, §6, Theorem A).
66. **v² + h² = 1 is a receipt.** "Not a fact about right triangles met in the world: a receipt that two readings were
    taken against the same denominator, and that the denominator was the pair itself." Composites never know which
    hold was used; only the receipt does (§4). *(A third reading of the equation, beside "the hold" and "a third
    party", items 8 and 9.)*
67. **The circle is the fixed point of the own-size reading**: the one figure that is its own image, "the signature of
    no unit brought" (§6, Theorems B and C). A stick laid down at home has no distinguished figure.
68. **Every figure is a centring; the circle is the one with no residue.** Across directions a figure leaves its own
    ratio r(t₂)/r(t₁): the residue is the shape (§5a). The residues compose, and they give the whole shape "and one
    number short", the size (§5c, Propositions 1–3).
69. **A lap multiplies to one, and that checks the reader, not the figure.** Keep residues to 16, 12 or 8 bits and the
    lap misses by 1.1×10⁻⁴, 7.5×10⁻⁴, 3.3×10⁻³: the grain's amount, not the shape's (§5c, Proposition 4).
70. **Half or whole.** The ratio v/h repeats every half turn, so a reader holding one ratio covers a half turn. Both
    components, read against the arrival's own size, keep the sign of h, the side, and cover the whole circle. "What
    neither holds is a tally of how many horizons it has crossed. So neither knows when it has closed" (§6, Sep 22).
71. **The centring is the circle, not a metaphor** (Tom, Sep 18): g erects the frame (the places), the magnitudes are
    the stones, dividing strikes the frame. "A freedom is a centring with g still free. A figure is a centring with g
    spent" (§0, §5b).
71a. ⚠ **(Arch metaphor demoted, item 45c.)** **Ruled Sep 24: readers don't build arches; building is the view from everywhere.** Item 71's "g erects the
    frame" and item 73's "the inside reader holds the frame" are the view from everywhere's, not a reader's.
72. **The keystone is the corner**: "the stone that needs no unit" (§0). ⚠ The Sep 22 check in the same paper: the
    corner is the middle of a single sweep, not of a half turn, so which span is the arch is unruled.
73. **Which reader this is** (§1a, Sep 22): the inside reader holds the frame and none of the stones; the outside
    reader holds half the stones ("the outside reader is the magnitude list", Tom, Sep 22); every stone at once is the
    view from everywhere's (there called god's eye). "No single reader builds the whole arch" (§7).
74. **The everywhere equation, π/2 · 2/π = 1**, over one sweep: (arc ÷ width) × (mean facing) = 1 on every convex side
    tried; the circle is the one figure whose two factors stay fixed from every direction; on other figures "the uneven
    spread is the shape" (§6, `godseye.mjs`).

**Where *The Centring* bears on today's items:**
- **Supports 36a's depth clue.** The outside reader turns stones back into magnitudes "only if the stones are evenly hung
  … its gaps then give how squarely the surface faces it, and that gives depth" (§1a). That is h as foreshortening,
  giving depth, as in 36a, and against *Degrees of Freedom*'s depth-as-z.
- **Two inside readings, not one.** *One Reader* §6.4 has the inside reader hold v/h; here it holds both components
  against the arrival's own size, which keeps the side and covers the whole circle (item 70). The fisheye is the ratio
  reading; the own-size reading is the circle.
- **Cuts against the facing count** (item 11a): "what neither holds is a tally of how many horizons it has crossed", like
  *Degrees of Freedom*'s "no walker" result.
- ⚠ **Conflicts with Tom's Sep 24 ruling "readers don't build roman arches, they read them."** Here the reader
  "builds against" the circle and the inside reader erects the frame (§0, §1a). One of the two needs revising.

## 8c. From *The Boundary*

*Checked Sep 24 against the uploaded working log (Sep 19–22; 332 checks, 185-row kill log). Items not already above.*

75. **Two faculties.** Adding goes Gaussian, dividing goes Cauchy, "and nothing carries it back"; "pointing divides —
    and the ratio of two Gaussians is Cauchy" (§4). Isotropy, not Gaussianity, makes the Cauchy (B6; sources table).
    ⚠ The Sep 19 table's "parallel / counting / Gaussian" is superseded by Tom's Sep 24 ruling, "gaussian is not
    correct here".
76. **The lens is h².** "h is how present the hold is; h² is how much measure is kept there" (§9.6 B64). v² is the
    swapped address's lens, and h² + v² = 1: "the two facings partition the measure exactly" (B67). ⚠ B154 later finds
    the tan spacing "a tautology" unless Möbius rigidity forces it.
77. **Every landmark is a fixed point of a move**: rescale fixes 0 and ∞, swap fixes ±1, compose fixes ±i (the clamp)
    (B19–B20). The corner is the only address at rest under an additive common mode (B28–B29). In rungs the swap is
    negation (B30).
78. **vh is the rescale speed**: s/(1 + s²) per rung of zoom, "maximal at the corner, exactly 1/2" (B31).
79. **The relation lives at twice the turn**: R = 2vh and D = v² − h², with R² + D² = 1 at 2u (B70–B72). D = v² − h²
    is the one signed quantity built from fullnesses (B78). The corner is the maximum-entropy point of (v², h²) (B85).
80. **Amplitudes and measures.** v, h, R are amplitudes; v², h², g are measures: "g should be compared to h², not to h"
    (B79).
81. **The swap is free within one arrival and impossible between arrivals**; the choice is made "once, before the arrival
    came" (B126). The cost of holding the wrong term is lowest at the corner (B127).
82. **The address drops the radius; the residue drops the direction** (B104, B112). The lost sign is one global bit,
    "not in what the reader holds" (B112–B115).
83. **"Unit circle" does three jobs**: radius, circle-ness and completeness (B236).
84. **Serial is level 3 without level 2**: magnitudes with no frame; "it receives no values. It gets the MOVE" (B241, kill
    log). ⚠ In tension with the log's own §9.7o: "the corpus's serial reader receives v **and** h (B116)".
85. **A turn is communicable between readers; an index is not.** The corner is the shared landmark (B298).
86. **The rulings in it, for the record.** "Outside is counting, inside is pointing" (Tom, Sep 19; ⚠ **withdrawn Sep 25**:
    "No, you can't point, the boundary is wrong", item 46a⁸); "notice, no inside or
    outside in this framing. we project serial, and count parallel" (Tom, Sep 20, B139), later qualified by the Sep 22
    three scenarios; "A freedom is mathematical, it has an infinity, it is asymmetric, it is projective, N is unknown.
    Quarter turns are symmetric, have no infinity, are angular, can be counted, N is known. The Roman arch connects two
    quarter turns" (Tom, Sep 21); "the quarter turn is proportional on both sides of the corner. V over h before and h
    over v after" (Tom, B153); "the facing is confusing because no one is actually walking a path" (Tom; the word still
    "needs a ruling").

**Where *The Boundary* bears on today's items:**
- **Facings (11a):** the quarters are cyclic and need signs; the facings are Klein and sign-free; the rotation count
  imports a walker. Recorded under 11a.
- **The Serial Cauchy's "observer's signature"** (§6a there): killed (B300); the kernel is the setup's.
- **Breadth (61):** B273 has breadth as the world's range and N as "the place where they meet"; B275 then corrects Tom's
  Sep 21 binning: "Each side has both; what differs is finiteness … Ownership, not possession." Sep 24's "just breadth"
  (breadth = N) is later, and by Tom's precedence ruling ("today takes precedence") would govern; the conflict is
  unwritten-out.
- **The corner rule (46):** B210–B213: "the corner does not identify itself"; "the prior is not corrected, it is
  confirmed"; on a monotone list "it is not a loop at all".
- **Readers build or read:** B87, "the builder puts it up"; B291, "the only one a reader can build from N alone".
- **The outside reader's arcsine, 1/h and rim do not appear anywhere in the log.** They are this session's.

## 8d. From *The Cauchy*

*Checked Sep 24 against the uploaded copy (Aug 22). Items not already above. In that paper "outside" means the outside
account, the page; it has no outside reader, and "corner" never means v = h.*

87. **The Cauchy is the circle as it arrives**: "what the circle looks like when it can only arrive one reading at a
    time"; the heavy tail is the horizon (opening, §2).
88. **The measure is the resolved object's, and the reader inherits it** (§4, §5). A density has nonzero degree, so it is
    an account's; the cross ratio of four arrivals is the pair's, and out of the reader's reach (§5).
89. **The missing mean is a coordinate artifact.** In rungs the mean is exactly 0 and the variance π²/4; the divergence
    lives past the fulcrum and grows logarithmically (§6, §7). "The mean is the wrong question… the median is the
    right one"; the median's spread is about π/(2√n) (§6, §10a).
90. **The fulcrum is the median for any exchange-symmetric law**, not only the Cauchy (§7); it is the Cauchy's scale
    parameter (§11a).
91. **Half or a quartile.** On the unsigned quarter turn the fulcrum halves the arc (§4). On the signed half turn "home is
    the median of the arrivals; the fulcrum is their upper quartile" (§11a).
92. **The reader can find the regime but not the law**: *heavy* is an order fact, the law a metric one (§8).
93. **Where π lives**: "the reader inside never emits a π"; π is the outside account's bookkeeping (§9). π/2 · 2/π = 1
    already appears as normalisation, density × extent: "the transcendental cancels and the exact unit remains… a
    theorem, not a choice" (§4).
94. **The reach cancels in every single reading**; readings distinguish rays, and the census returns the unit circle from
    any figure: "the unit circle is not what was there. It is what the census costs nothing to say" (§11).
95. **A single list divided by its own increment** cancels the gain but not the shape, and comes out Cauchy (§11a).
96. **The arrivals recover the frame**: the Möbius group, then the projective line, then the disagreement up to scale,
    up to a re-facing (§12). With an uneven schedule the arrival density is the arc element, which gives the figure up to
    size (§12).
97. **A departure from the Cauchy does not name its cause**: schedule or figure (§13). "The turn belongs to the relation;
    the schedule belongs to the reader" (§13).
98. **"The circle is nobody's"** (§14); homogeneity is unoccupiable: the reading warps, the page cuts (§14).
99. **The cancellation is the mechanism, not the price**: serial reading is blind to form and sensitive to dynamics; the
    reader tracks a centre of gravity in motion (§14b, §14c).

**Where *The Cauchy* bears on today's items:** it already has the lens as the density, the fulcrum as the scale, and the
median; it sides with *Degrees of Freedom* and *The Boundary* that the measure is not the reader's but inherited; its
"quartile" is the signed case of the corner rule's "half"; and its opening signpost, "the Gaussian is the study of *N*,
and the Cauchy is the study of *n*", should be reworded or retired in light of Tom's Sep 24 "gaussian is not correct
here" if parallel is read as N.

## 8e. From *Perfect Fidelity at Zero*

*Checked Sep 24 against the uploaded copy. ⚠ This paper was ARCHIVED on Tom's ruling of Sep 5 ("fisheye investigation
is over. archive the fidelity paper, it's not needed"); its items are recorded, not current doctrine.*

100. **Fidelity is h = cos θ, the observer's share of the relation.** At home the reading is all unit, so fidelity is
     perfect "because a unit is exact by construction"; at the horizon the observer is absent from its own report
     (§4, Steps 3–4). The magnification sec θ = 1/h is "how much of the report is not the observer".
101. **The circle is the unique curve on which an atom's two extents are equal.** Over |v|ᵖ + |h|ᵖ = 1 the radial extent
     is π/2 throughout and the linear extent crosses π/2 once, at p = 2 (§4, Step 5; proved within the p-norm family
     only).
102. **The observer's coordinate is the rapidity, asinh(v/h)**: proportional below the corner, logarithmic above, joined
     at 1; derived from the observer's units with nothing borrowed (§6).
103. **The fidelity is a rate**: dθ = h·dζ, the conversion between the observer's step and the drawn arc (§6).
104. **"The Cauchy, 1/(1 + r²), is not the observer's law. It is the shadow an observer uniform in its own coordinate
     casts when its arrivals are laid on the drawn arc"** (§6). An observer uniform in ζ has density 1/√(1 + r²) = h in
     the reading, with no total.
105. **Three coordinates, three owners**: θ the drawn arc's (ours), ln r the register's, asinh r the atom's (§6).
106. **The two lower ladders, priced**: the mirror ladder keeps the closure certificate and cannot state home; the
     proportional ladder states home, cannot leave it, and loses the certificate (§6, `ladder-test.mjs`).
107. **"The fisheye is just the semicircle."** "The observer does not act on it, does not apply a lens to it, does not
     distort it; it stands somewhere inside the circle and looks, and *fisheye* is the adjective for what that looks
     like. From the centre a circle looks like a circle. From anywhere else it looks fisheye" (plain-language close).

**Where it bears on today's items (with the archive caveat):**
- **A fourth voice against "the Cauchy is the reader's density"** (items 43–44; *One Reader* §6.4): here the observer's
  own measure is uniform in rapidity, density h in the reading, and the Cauchy (h²) is the drawn arc's shadow. On that
  account the reader's own packing is h, one power lower than §6.4's h².
- **Against "the lens"**: item 107 says the observer applies no lens; "fisheye" is an adjective for a standpoint.
  That fits *Degrees of Freedom* §5.15n (the kernel is fixed by where the facing is), and cuts against speaking of the
  reader's lens as something it brings.
- **Against "the inside reader at the centre sees the fisheye"**: "from the centre a circle looks like a circle". The
  paper's own opening picture (the reader at the centre, reading onto a flat sheet) says the opposite; the closing
  paragraph reads as the later correction.

## 8f. From *The Observation Atom*

*Checked Sep 24 against the uploaded copy. Its observer is not called a reader; v is "the magnitude currently arriving"
and h the observer's own capacity, so its pair is the register pair (item 7), not two even-handed parts of a push.*

108. **v/h is a name, never an act.** "Written with a slash, v/h names an executed quotient — a value the observer does
     not compute … held as the pair (v, h)." Dividing is "a **division** and a **conditional**, and the observer has
     neither": it would have to decide which quantity goes underneath (§1.5).
109. **The state is the direction of the pair**, (h, v): 0 at home, the corner, π/2 at the far end, "an ordinary value";
     no fold and no exception at h = 0 (§1.5). "Order is observation's; spacing is the instrument's" (§1.5).
110. **h is possessed and unquotable**: "h = 1 is the statement that the observer's own extent is what '1' means" (§1.2).
111. **The third party supplies the occurrence, never the value**: "a settling, never a division"; it must cancel from the
     read and power the settling (§1.4, §1.4a).
112. **"The fisheye is not something the observer applies.** It is what an even layout looks like when described in the
     slope chart." The fisheye shape is "the **chart's**, applied to a device's layout"; the Cauchy is "the **chart's** —
     and only for the angle device"; π is "the **describer's** — radians" (§1.6a–b).
113. **Even spacing is not forced**; aperture-invariance forces log spacing; which layout is "a choice between two criteria
     neither of which the observer can apply" (§1.6b–d).
114. **Two clarities**: "Absolute precision is best at home; relative precision is best at the corner" (§1.6a).
115. **No running total**: "No running total can be kept: it needs a register, and the register is the observer" (§2.1).
116. **The corner is v = h, four characterisations, one state**; the landmarks are home, the corner and the **far end**,
     an ordinary state, not an unreachable horizon (§1.6). "Past the corner the world is measuring the observer."

## 8g. From *The Balance*

*Checked Sep 24 against the uploaded copy. Its primitive read is b = (A − B)/(A + B), the balance, not v/h; it has no
inside or outside readers and no facings; its "outside" is the describer.*

117. **The balance read**: b = (A − B)/(A + B) = tanh φ, with the inner coordinate φ = ½ ln(A/B); readings chain as
     (b₁ + b₂)/(1 + b₁b₂) (§0, §1). The balance is hyperbolic; the slope v/h elliptic (§31.1).
118. **Division is never executed.** "Executing v/h is what is forbidden; *holding* (v, h) and reading their standing is
     the primitive act itself" (§20.2). "The centre of gravity is a division — and the balance never performs it"
     (§32.1); the steelyard settles, it does not divide (§2).
119. **The centre is free**: "the one point a system defines without being given an origin" (§4).
120. **Taut is the corner**, "not the horizon … you cannot be mistaken about it" (§17.1).
121. **The read closes at π, the hold at 2π**: "the **line** has come home; the **ball** has not" (§17.4): closure as a
     double cover walked, two halves, not four sweeps.
122. **The divergence lives in the chart**: "the dial gap is constant. The read gap … diverges … the chart is precisely the
     object the observer declines to compute" (§27.6). The infinity is "something an outside describer introduces"; π is
     "the outside describer's constant" (§27.6, §28.5).
123. **Three verbs, and no fourth**: project (a·b), compare (det), count; det² + (a·b)² = |a|²|b|² (§29). "Dividing throws
     away the one bit that distinguishes a configuration from its mirror" (§29.7). The atom is stated with v, h ≥ 0
     (§31.2).
124. **The budget is the measure**: Σvᵢ² + h² = 1 read as vᵢ/h gives the Cauchy (§23–24).

**Where these two bear on today's items.** Both refuse the one act today's list gives the inside reader. *The
Observation Atom*: the observer "cannot execute a quotient". *The Balance*: "executing v/h is what is forbidden"; the
evaluated quotient belongs to "the outside describer, and nobody else". So "the inside reader holds v/h and divides"
(items 34–35) and "the projection divides v by h" (item 19) conflict with both. The corpus's own reconciliation is
already in *The Relation Space* §25: the reader holds the pair and its disagreement; "only the final report divides".
Both papers also put the fisheye and the Cauchy on the describer's side (the chart's, not the observer's), a fifth and
sixth voice with item 104. *The Observation Atom* forbids the running count the corner rule needs (item 115), and keeps
three landmarks with an ordinary far end, against *The Boundary*'s two.

## 8h. Sep 27: the view from somewhere, three sweeps, and g

*Rulings are numbered as in* Reader Geometry as Addressing *(RGA), Appendix A. Quotes are Tom's, with the time.*

125. **Serial and parallel, not inside and outside** (R45, Sep 25). The names *inside* and *outside* in items 34–38 are
     dropped: "inside what, outside what". Items above keep them as written.
126. **The view from nowhere and the view from somewhere** (Sep 25–27). The unit circle, all four facings, no
     standpoint, is the view from nowhere; a reader has the view from somewhere, two facings, the Cauchy. "Classical
     sources are almost all views from nowhere. That is what we are exploring" (Sep 27). Replaces "the view from
     everywhere" throughout.
127. **The two infinities are named apart** (R77, 09:19). **The horizon** is the reading's infinity, v/h → ∞ as h → 0,
     at the reader's **sides**. **The vanishing point** is the far ahead, where the reading goes to 0, at **home**.
     Neither word is used for the other. *(Items 28 and 38 are about the horizon in this sense.)*
128. **The serial reader divides, the parallel reader does not; the corner connects them** (R78, 15:02). "One divides one
     doesn't and they are connected by the corner." The corner is the one place where dividing and not dividing say the
     same thing: v = h, read as 1 or as two equal magnitudes (*The Corner Between the Readers*).
129. **Three sweeps: left, right and forward** (R79, 18:17). **Left** and **right** each run from home to the horizon on
     their side: the front half, the serial reader's two facings, each with its own corner. **Forward** runs along each
     address to the first border: the parallel reader's sweep, toward the vanishing point. The parallel reader rides the
     serial sweeps; it sweeps **along**, not across.
130. **Depth is the forward sweep, the parallel reader's** (R79, 18:21: "I don't think that old ruling is correct. This
     one is cleaner."). Supersedes "the 1d signal is where depth comes from, it is the z" (Sep 20). It agrees with
     *Degrees of Freedom* §5.19's "depth is z, the log radius, with x and y carrying direction only" (item 36a, item 64):
     direction across is the serial reader's, the radius forward is the parallel reader's. RGA §7.4 rewritten: the
     image (x/z, y/z) is the divided reading, depth the kept one; addresses followed to their forward magnitudes give the
     object in the parallel reader's unit (3.4 × 10⁻¹⁶, `toy3d-r79.mjs`); if depth is divided too, up to one scale, and
     the unit is the corner.
131. **Three [0, 1]s and one [0, ∞)** (20:36: "h = [0,1], v = [0,1]. relation = [0,inf) if you divide, [0,1] if you
     don't. [unit circle=0, shape=1]"; 20:41: "i think g is the depth, it is the third dimesion.").

     | | range | 0 | 1 |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | h | [0, 1] | none of its own all | all of it |
     | v | [0, 1] | none of its own all | all of it |
     | the relation, divided (serial) | [0, ∞) | home | the corner (∞ is the horizon) |
     | the relation, not divided (parallel) | [0, 1] | | |
     | g, the depth | [0, 1] | the unit circle: addresses only, N = 0 | the shape: every magnitude, N placed |

     The addresses, and so the Cauchy f = h²/π per facing, are the same at every g (R52, the breathing). g says how much
     of the magnitude list is present: at g = 0 every magnitude sits on the unit circle, reading 1 against the
     reader's own size, ~~so depth starts at the corner~~ *(withdrawn, Tom 21:34: "a corner is only for things with a horizon. g is just
     [0,1]. no horizon.")*; at g = 1 every magnitude is the shape's. Fits Sep 20: "2d is closure… 3d is the depth of that
     representation" and "3d is consumed as 2d images of 1d slices", and the balloon (define ratios, inflate).
132. **Ruled: g counts the hangers placed** (R80, 20:46: "yes, g =1 means all the hangers are placed. g=0 no hangers
     placed."). The first reading below. The other two are kept for the record. Asked before the ruling, with three
     readings on the table:
     - **How many** (*Degrees of Freedom* §1, Sep 18): gN hangers carry their magnitude, the rest stay at 1. A slice is
       jagged. "gN is a subset, not a count"; "closure quantises g to multiples of 2/N".
     - **How far, additively**: every address moves out together, 1 + g(r − 1). A slice is smooth. *But it adds, and an
       amount needs a unit (item 6b: "three more than me" needs one); so it is not available to a reader without one.*
     - **How far, multiplicatively**: every address moves out together, r^g, so the log radius is g · ln r. A slice is
       smooth, and nothing but ratios is used. This is *Degrees of Freedom* §5.18's "in serial g does not punch holes in
       the signal, it multiplies it": the motion ln(rₖ₊₁/rₖ) is multiplied by g. It also fits §5.19's depth as the log
       radius, and item 80, "g should be compared to h², not to h" (g a measure). *[Reading, not ruled.]*
     Tom, 20:31, set g by the count ("at density = 0 unit circle, N = 0… at density = 1 full shape, N = 1000"), which
     is the first reading at its two ends; all three agree at g = 0 and g = 1 and differ only between.
133. **The Cauchy in 1d, 2d and 3d** (checked Sep 27 against 4 × 10⁵ evenly met directions). With n readings,
     f(x) = Γ((n+1)/2) / (π^{(n+1)/2} √|Σ|) · [1 + (x − μ)ᵀΣ⁻¹(x − μ)]^{−(n+1)/2}. On the bar of length 1 it is h²/π
     (1d), h³/2π (2d), h⁴/π² (3d): 1/π at home and 1/2π at the corner in 1d. Under R79 a reader in 3d space has two
     readings and one magnitude, so its Cauchy is the 2d one; a 3d Cauchy is a reader dividing four parts. Only on the
     line is the corner also the median: the median ring is at 45°, 60° and 66° in 1d, 2d and 3d, and within 45° lie
     50%, 29% and 18%.
134. **Composing readings and adding magnitudes** (*Observing from Somewhere* §4.5; McCullagh 1996 after Wold 1934;
     the "two limits" lab). Composing n readings by (s + t)/(1 − st) goes to the standard Cauchy; adding n magnitudes to
     the Gaussian. On one shape the serial side settled by n ≈ 5, the parallel side by n ≈ 50 *[one shape, post hoc]*.
135. **Lines, not rays** (*Observing from Somewhere* §1; RGA §5.4). A reading names a line: carried along chords through
     a standpoint, the even circle lands exactly on the Cauchy there; cast as rays, exactly half even and half Cauchy.

136. **The game** (Sep 28, 04:08: "this feels right"). *Vanishing Point* (artifact): the front half laid evenly in
     turn, horizons at the sides, the vanishing point at home; shapes of 400 hangers come **ahead** out of the
