     vanishing point or **across** from horizon to horizon; only the hangers the reader can tell apart are drawn (a fixed
     grain in turn), so g rises as 1/distance (B.43); beneath, the Cauchy with g kept in, gN · h²/π, with the placed
     hangers ticked at their readings. The player names each shape at the smallest g they can.

137. **g is the reader's; the shapes move through it** (R81, Sep 28, 04:47). Not the world's: laid out from the reader,
     1 near and falling as 1/distance toward the vanishing point (B.43). The address law with g kept in, gN · h²/π, is
     the reader's and fixed; what the world contributes is only where each shape is in it. In the game the payload's
     median and mean (R47) drift as pieces come and go while the addresses and their corners stay put.

138. **Missiles are the reverse g** (R82, Sep 28, 05:16). A missile runs out along its address from g = 1 toward the
     vanishing point; a piece runs in. On the same address the whole way, they meet only when their g's are equal. A hit
     is two conditions: the same address (serial, left and right) and the same g (forward, parallel).

## 8i. Sep 28: Horizon Defender, what a working reader taught

*From building* Horizon Defender *(artifact, versions 17–29; game files and backups in* game/*). Quotes are Tom's, with
the time. These are findings from use, not proofs: the game shows the framing is sufficient and self-consistent when
run, not that vision works this way (a claim about perception needs a planned test).*

139. **The picture is read, not projected** (v17–20; Tom 05:57: "looks better than the view from nowhere version").
     Each address sweeps forward and keeps its first border. Four rules do all the reader's work: **lay the addresses
     evenly in turn; read the first border along each; carry the jump between neighbours as a primitive; make contact
     where the g's are equal on the same address.** Occlusion, clipping, the horizon and the vanishing point are never
     handled; they follow from the reading. The view-from-nowhere version needed about seven mechanisms, two of them
     tolerances set by hand (a hit width of 0.52/distance scaled by the yaw, a height of 0.55) to keep what can be hit in
     step with what is drawn. In the reading version what you see and what you can hit are one reading.

140. **Edges are the reader's** (v20, Tangent Bridge §15.1; Tom 06:05: "performance and visually its is superb"). Where
     two neighbouring addresses read different pieces, the edge is placed between them from what the nearer one read,
     followed toward its neighbour until it leaves the shape: finer than the grain, with no further addresses and no
     smoothing. The earlier pixelation came from treating a jump as a sample.

141. **Address and payload, shown** (Tom 07:27: "cauchy address and payloads are new and they make the experience very
     different"). The addresses are laid before anything is out there, the same whatever the world holds; the payload
     is only what each finds (which piece, at what g). Laid evenly in turn, the whole front half fits from home to both
     horizons; the Cauchy, 1/(π(1 + s²)), is the exact map to the ratio chart, where a flat screen lives and never
     reaches the horizon. Pieces turning in place (battleships going bow-on, the red bar turning end over end) change
     the payload while the addresses stay put.

142. **R81 made operational** (v25; Tom 06:47 asked whether the battleships could shoot "from somewhere"). With each
     battleship a reader of its own (its own addresses across its own front half, its own g, a turret-like facing
     sweeping a row), one contact gets as many g's as there are readers, none of them the event's: "theirs g 0.13 ·
     seen g 0.26", "theirs g 1.00 · seen g 0.08". Contact is decided in the shooter's reading; every other reader sees
     it at its own g. Nothing in the world carries a g.

143. **R82 generalises** (v25–28). "Same address, same g" served every shooter and missile against missile, with no
     special case. Every missile, yours or a ship's, leaves its shooter along one of the shooter's addresses carrying
     the shooter's g; a hit needs the address (the sweep in turn, left and right) and the g (the forward sweep) to agree.
     R79's sweeps each do work: neither alone decides a contact.

144. **A second reader finds the view from nowhere's leftovers** (v26–27). With one reader nothing looked wrong. Once the
     ships read, two residues showed themselves: the pieces were billboards turned toward the one reader, and their
     motion is laid out around that reader. The first was converted (each piece now keeps its own facing in the world,
     and every reader sees whichever side faces it). *Method:* a claim that something is from somewhere is checked with
     at least two readers; one reader cannot tell its standpoint from the world's.

145. **The world and the reader separate; the view from nowhere stays, as the world.** The world still holds positions
     and moves the pieces; working out what an address reads meets the world's geometry. What the view from somewhere
     removed is the reader's need for any of it. The old version fused the two in one projection; now the world moves
     things and each reader only reads addresses. (With the three scenarios of Sep 22: the view from nowhere is where the
     world is kept; the view from somewhere is what can be read of it.)

146. **Standpoints are interchangeable** (v28–29; Tom 07:30: "the user is a purple battle ship, twice as big as the
     others"). The player is a ship among ships, read and struck by the others; the view switches to any ship's
     addresses and the player steers and fires from whichever ship is being looked through. The world does not change
     with the switch; only whose reading the picture, the strips and the disc are.

147. **Grain rescales; standpoint reorders** (*Two Standpoints*, Sep 28; Tom 08:25: "use the previous demo as the
     starting point"). Two readers side by side on one world, each read wholly from its own standpoint; the red ships'
     g stays 1 out to D1 = 10, yours out to 3.5. Past D1, g = D1/distance, so two readers in the same place with different
     grain differ by a fixed factor on every event (10/3.5 ≈ 2.9), and **agree on every relation between g's**: an event
     at half another's g for one is at half for the other. Dividing removes the grain as it removes the scale. Readers
     in different places do not agree on those relations. Inside a reader's D1 everything reads 1 and the relations
     are lost. (One kill in the test run: shooter 0.18, you 0.35, the red ship 0.24; each gap mixes both causes.)

148. **The world was being updated inside the reader's drawing.** In *Horizon Defender* contact, firing and the
     on-screen effects all ran inside the drawing of your picture, invisibly with one reader. With two, the world has to
     be updated once and read twice, and every on-screen effect had to go: a spark drawn on a screen belongs to one
     reader. **An event is a point in the world; each reader reads it at its own address and its own g** (the "seen g"
     is worked out once, at the moment, from that reader's own addresses). Item 145 made structural.

149. **The "world's frame" in the demo is a phantom standpoint.** The switch between frames changes only the point the
     patterns are laid out around: you, or a fixed point where no reader stands. The motion law itself (sweep in turn,
     close in along distance, "through the g") is written from a standpoint; moved to the fixed point it is written from
     a standpoint nobody occupies. So motion can belong to a reader, to a point no one stands at, or to no point at all
     (pieces carrying their own velocities). See the open list.

150. **Nothing marks a reader as "you" but the controls.** The two sides run identical code; either can steer and fire.
     What still singles you out is that the pieces move around you (in your frame), the same leftover as item 149 seen
     from the other side. Control is not standpoint: one can act from either reader without being either.

151. **Acting needs only the address and the g** (*Blind*, Sep 28; Tom 09:09 chose it). The picture is switched off;
     the view holds only the payload: a bar per slot of addresses, laid evenly in turn, its colour the piece read and
     its height the g. Every hit is decided on the strip: tap a slot, the missile's g falls, contact where it equals the
     slot's g. R82 uses nothing the picture has. **The picture is for recognition, not for action.** (A scripted player
     aiming at the highest-g slots landed 23 of 40; not comparable with the earlier 8 of 40, which fired at random
     points on the pieces. It shows the strip is enough to act on, not how hard it is.)

152. **Facing is read between neighbouring addresses.** A slanted piece shows as bars sloping in height, its near end
     at a higher g than its far end. No single slot holds the slant; it is the relation of one slot's g to the next,
     the same kind of thing as the jump (item 140): read between addresses, not at one. Being a ratio of g's, it
     survives a change of grain (item 147); only standpoint changes it.

153. **The strip is one sweep in turn; what it loses is the up-and-down sweep.** The picture is addresses across (in
     turn) and up and down; the strip keeps only the first. What vanished is what the up-and-down sweep carries:
     outlines, anything stacked (a grid of nine reads as one bar), a wheel rolling in the picture's plane. Evidence for
     the open 3d item: R79's sweeps in 3d as **left and right, up and down, and forward**, each dimension of the
     picture belonging to one sweep.

154. **Dropping a sweep hides more.** Each slot keeps only the nearest reading across its whole column, so anything
     behind that reading in the same turn is gone, even what the picture would show above or below it. With both across
     sweeps a far piece can show past the edge of a near one; with one, it cannot. Fewer sweeps, more hidden.

155. **Two sweeps side by side lose most of the picture** (*Two Strips*, Sep 28; Tom 09:17: "Proceed"). No picture; two
     strips, each one across sweep on its own: along the bottom, each slot in turn shows the nearest reading anywhere up
     or down that turn; down the side, each slot in height shows the nearest reading at that height anywhere across. To
     fire, the player pairs a turn with a height. Measured over two runs (about 60 samples each): per moment **15–18
     pieces in the picture, about 11 in the turn strip, 2–3 in the height strip, about 2 in both**. The height strip is
     taken over by the nearest one or two pieces (most things sit near one height, and a near piece spans every height
     band it covers). Of every pairing of an occupied turn slot with an occupied height slot, **only 30–38% point at an
     address that reads anything, and 13–14% at the piece both strips named.** (Scripted firing, 6–18 hits in 40 either
     way, was swamped by motion and settles nothing.)

156. **The picture is the two across sweeps crossed at every address.** Each address carries one turn and one height
     together; that pairing is what separate strips throw away, and it cannot be recovered from them. For the 3d item:
     left and right, up and down, and forward, *with the across sweeps crossed at each address*, not laid side by side.
     The same point as R78 (two readings connected at the corner) in another form: two readings are not a picture until
     they meet.

157. **What one reader can pass to another: the address carries over only between readers who share a standpoint**
     (*Spotter*, Sep 28; Tom 09:45: "Proceed"). Your side is dark; a friendly green ship reads for you and passes only
     what it has: the address a target sits on in its reading and the g it reads there (every 1.2 s, the nearest thing
     it reads). **Beside you** (standing where you stand, facing where you face, grain D1 = 10 against your 3.5): at the
     reported address your own address read the same piece in 15 and 19 of 20 reports (two runs; the misses are the one
     frame between its reading and yours). **Away** (45 units off, facing the fixed point): 2 and 0 of 21. Your address
     read *something* there about 13 times in 21 by chance, but almost never the piece reported.

158. **Item 147 confirmed by the readers themselves.** Beside you, where both read the same piece past both D1s, the ratio
     of its g to yours had median **2.86 and 2.87** (ranges 2.80–2.97, 2.82–3.01) against the predicted 10/3.5 = 2.857.
     A difference of grain is one fixed factor, so a report from a finer or coarser reader in the same place is usable
     as it stands: the address directly, the g once divided by the factor. A difference of standpoint breaks the address
     itself, and nothing in the report repairs it: the report names a turn and a height in the spotter's layout, which is
     not yours. (Hits from firing at reports were few either way, 1–5 of 14–18, swamped by motion during the missile's
     flight; the address match is the result.)

159. **What survives a change of standpoint: the cross ratio of four readings** (*Landmarks*, Sep 28; Tom 10:10:
     "Proceed"). Three posts stand in a row (a rail 55 units ahead of you) and runners travel along it. The spotter,
     standing 45 units off, reads the three posts and a runner and passes **one number**: the cross ratio of the four
     turns it reads them at, taken as readings v/h (the slope from its own home). You read the same three posts from
     where you stand; the one turn giving the same cross ratio is where the runner is on your addresses. Measured over
     two runs: **away, the cross ratio landed on the runner 22 of 22 times both runs; the spotter's raw address, 4 of 22.**
     Column error: median 0.5 of an address (max 3.8, then 1.4). Beside you both work (16 of 16). No g, no distance and
     no unit passes between the readers; four readings each and one relation among them.

160. **Why it works, and what it needs.** Four lines through a standpoint that meet one straight row hold the same
     cross ratio from every standpoint (the classical projective invariant, reached here from two readers; cf. *The
     Route*). In the corpus's terms: the cross ratio of four readings v/h is unchanged when the facing turns (a change of
     home is a Möbius map of the readings), and unchanged between standpoints for points on one row. What it needs: the
     target on the landmarks' row (off the row it fails; not yet shown), all four read by both readers, and landmarks
     wider than the grain. The first posts, about one unit wide at 85 units and seen at a slant, fell between the
     spotter's addresses and were **not read at all**: a landmark thinner than the grain does not exist for that
     reader. Widened to about two units, all were read.

161. **Off the row: two numbers, and both sweeps crossed** (*The Plane*, Sep 28; Tom 10:31: "Proceed"). Four posts stand
     on the ground at the corners of a patch; runners roam it. Each reader reads where things *stand*: the foot of what
     its addresses read, as two readings, v/h in turn and v/h up and down (the two across sweeps crossed at the
     address, item 156). The spotter, 60 units off, passes **two numbers**: where the runner's foot sits in the frame of
     the four posts' feet (its projective coordinates). You place them in your own four posts' frame. Measured over two
     runs: **away, the two numbers landed on the runner 17 of 20 both runs; the one-number row method (item 159) 0 of
     20, the raw address 0–1.** Beside you: 13 of 13. A plane needs four landmarks and two numbers, as a row needs three
     and one; and it needs both sweeps, since the foot is placed by the up-and-down sweep as much as by the turn.

162. **What the plane needs, learned by failing.** (a) **Posts must read from every side.** A flat post faced one way was
     edge-on to the spotter and not read at all; two panels crossed at right angles fixed it (the edge-on empty reading
     of the open list, again). (b) **The readers must look down on the plane.** With the ground 3 below the eyes and the
     patch 50–100 away, the posts' feet differed by about six addresses up and down, and the two numbers landed **0 of
     20**; with the readers on high ground, 12 above it, **17 of 20**. A plane seen at a graze gives the up-and-down
     sweep almost nothing to work with; the transfer is only as good as the reading of both sweeps at the feet.

163. **Off every plane: one reader passes a line, two readers pass a point** (*Two Lines*, Sep 28; Tom 10:41:
     "Proceed"). Four tall masts stand on low ground; flyers roam between them at changing heights. From one standpoint a
     flyer is a line of sight, not a point: no reader can say how far along its address the flyer is (g would, but g
     does not cross standpoints, item 158). Each spotter passes **four numbers**: where its line of sight meets the ground
     (two numbers, in the frame of the masts' feet) and where it meets the height of the masts' tops (two numbers, in the
     frame of their tops). Placed in your own frames, each is a line on your addresses, and **it passes through the
     spotter as you read it** (in the screenshot each line runs out to its ship). Two spotters, two lines; the flyer is
     where they cross. With both spotters on towers (25 and 40 up): **26 of 26 landed on the flyer, both runs; miss median
     0.7–0.8 of an address, max 1.5.** Nothing passed but eight numbers; no g, no distance, no unit.

164. **Readers in one level plane cannot cross well.** With both spotters at your own eye height, all three standpoints
     in one level plane, both lines lay almost along your horizon and nearly parallel: the crossing landed **1 of 26**
     (median miss 37 addresses). One spotter raised: 16 of 26 (median 6). Both raised: 26 of 26. The same lesson as the
     graze (item 162), now for standpoints: the up-and-down sweep only helps when the readers are spread up and down, as
     well as across.

165. **Does g cross? Not across standpoints** (*The g Question*, Sep 28; Tom 10:51: "Proceed"). Every piece both
     readers read, paired: the spotter's g against yours, raw and as a ratio to the g of a landmark both read (the blue
     plate). Measured over 30 s each (g < 1 on both sides only):

     | | theirs / yours, median | spread (IQR / median) | log correlation |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | **Beside you**, raw g | 2.85 | 1% | 1.00 |
     | **Beside you**, g ÷ the plate's g | 1.00 | 1% | 1.00 |
     | **Away**, raw g | 1.86 | 47% | 0.75 |
     | **Away**, g ÷ the plate's g | 1.37 | 51% | 0.64 |

     Beside you, dividing by a shared landmark's g removes the grain completely (2.85 → 1.00): **a landmark both read is
     a shared unit for g, between readers in one place.** Away, it removes nothing: the spread stays near 50%. On the
     scatter each piece traces its own curve as it moves; one "theirs" goes with many "yours". The correlation that
     remains (0.75) comes from the world's layout (things near the arena are near to both), not from any rule.

166. **So the division stands: the shared world is projective; g is private.** Between readers, what crosses is
     relations among readings of shared landmarks (items 159–164): cross ratios, frames, lines of sight and where they
     cross. g crosses only between readers who share a standpoint, and then divided by a shared landmark's g it agrees
     exactly. A reader elsewhere that needs g for a thing must first find the thing on its own addresses (by the shared
     relations) and then read its own g there. R81 from the other side: the g is the readers', and each reader's alone.

167. **Ruled: the game loop is the world** (R83, Sep 28, 11:07): "Computers have a game loop, that is the world. Each
     reader does not get their own loop. So we should be flexible the here, but realize reality does not have game
     loops." The frame the motion is laid out in is a free choice in demos; no finding rests on it. What the loop
     supplies does not carry over to reality: one global update, one "now" shared by every reader, an event at a single
     tick for all (item 148's "worked out once, at the moment" is the loop's moment, not a fact about readers).

168. **Without the shared now: still things cross exactly; moving things smear** (*Own Time*, Sep 28; Tom 11:09:
     "Proceed", after R83). Two Lines with each spotter reading at its own rhythm and holding its last reading between;
     its line on your side is from then, not now. One marker stands still among the moving flyers. Measured (reports
     landing within 3 addresses of the flyer; miss median in addresses):

     | rhythm | moving flyers | the still marker |
     |---|---|---|
     | every frame (the loop) | 15 / 15, 0.9 | 4 / 4, 0.4 |
     | own (every 0.25 s and 0.4 s) | 16 / 16, 1.3 | 5 / 5, 0.4 |
     | slow (every 0.6 s and 1 s) | 13 / 17, 2.6 | 4 / 4, 0.4 |

     The still marker does not care when anyone reads it. For moving flyers the miss grows with how stale the readings
     are and how far the flyer moves meanwhile (here 3–7 units a second, about 100 units off, so a quarter second is
     one or two addresses). **What the loop supplied was simultaneity, and only moving things need it.** The shared
     projective world of items 159–166 is exact for what holds still; for what moves, it is exact only to within how far
     things move between readings.

169. **A shared "when" is read off a shared moving landmark** (*Common Beat*, Sep 28; Tom 11:13: "Proceed"). With no
     game-loop now (R83), readers still share something of *when*: a gold star runs along the near row of three masts,
     and where it is, as the cross ratio of its turn with those masts, is **the same number for every reader at the same
     moment**, whatever its standpoint, grain or rhythm (item 159 applied to a moving thing). Each spotter stamps its
     readings with that beat; you read the beat now and carry each spotter's line forward from its last two readings to
     it. At the slow rhythm (readings every 0.6 s and 1 s), flyers moving in straight lines: **carried, miss median 1.4
     addresses, 15 of 19 within 3; not carried, 3.7, 10 of 24.** At the own rhythm (0.25 s, 0.4 s) both about 1.

170. **What the beat can and cannot do.** It orders and spaces readings for every reader without a clock of anyone's;
     it does not supply motion. Carrying a line forward from two readings assumes the flyer moves straight between them:
     with flyers also rising and falling, the carry helped less (median 2.4 against 3.4). And carrying adds its own error
     to what holds still (the still marker, 0.4 not carried, 1.2 carried), since it extends two noisy readings. So: a
     shared *when* is available from somewhere, as a relation among shared landmarks, exactly like a shared *where*; what
     it buys depends on how well each reader's last readings predict the thing's motion.

171. **A second reader places what the first cannot read** (*The Unseen*, Sep 28; Tom 11:24: "Proceed"). The Plane's
     two-number report, for runners you cannot read. **Cloud:** a grey panel hangs between you and the middle of the
     patch, clear of every post and of the spotter's view. **Edge-on:** every runner is a flat plate kept edge-on to you
     (on purpose), so no address of yours finds it. The spotter reads them all. Scored against where each runner's foot
     truly falls on your addresses (the view from nowhere, used only to score):

     | | unread by you | your address there reads | placed within (median, addresses) |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | cloud | 12 of 33 | the cloud, 12 of 12 | 2.5 (read ones: 2.5) |
     | edge-on | 25 of 25 | nothing, 25 of 25 | 2.6 |

     Hidden or unread runners are placed as well as ones you read. **An empty reading is not an empty place, and a
     second reader's relations can say so:** where your own address reads nothing, or the cloud, the report puts a
     runner.

172. **Knowing where is not reaching.** Contact is a reading too (R82): a missile makes contact with whatever its
     address reads first. Fired along the pointed address, it meets the cloud; at an edge-on plate it finds nothing to
     meet at all. (This follows from the game's contact rule; it was not separately measured.) So company gives a reader *where* a thing is that it cannot read, but not a way to touch it from where
     it stands: to touch it, the reader has to move until its own addresses read it.

173. **The back is read by turning, and turning is paid in time** (*The Back*, Sep 28; Tom 11:33: "Proceed"). Pieces come
     in from every side, evenly all the way round, taking about ten seconds to arrive; the reader reads only its front
     half. For each piece that touches it (touch scored in the world, since a front half cannot read it): was it read
     first, and how long before? 45 s of each:

     | turning | touched | read first | warning, median (min) |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | still | 14 | 50% | 9.7 s (0.9) |
     | steady sweep, 0.4 a second | 18 | 100% | 8.6 s (4.9) |
     | steady sweep, 1.2 a second | 21 | 100% | 6.9 s (4.2) |
     | about-face every 2 s | 24 | 100% | 6.9 s (5.6) |

     Still, the reader reads exactly its half: half of what touches it comes unread from behind. Any turning that brings
     the front half round sooner than a piece takes to arrive reads everything first, and pays for the back in warning:
     what the still reader saw at once, a turning reader sees only when its turn comes round.

174. **Touch from behind, answered.** A front-half reader holds nothing of its back at any one moment; turning spreads
     its reading over time, so the back is read *serially*, one half after the other, and the price is the wait between
     them. Nothing in the reader has to change: no second facing, no new kind of reading, only the turn. What stays
     unread is anything that arrives faster than the turn comes round.

175. **Near order: g falls silent inside D1; first border decides only where things meet; the sway decides all** (*Near
     Order*, Sep 28; Tom 11:40: "Proceed"). Eight still pieces in front of the reader, five inside D1 (every g reads 1)
     and three beyond; the reader sways gently side to side. Which of each pair is nearer, read three ways from the
     reader's addresses and scored against the world's order (for the score only). After 25 s, of the 28 pairs:

     | way | decides | right |
     |---|---|---|
     | by g | 18 | 18 |
     | by first border (which one is read where the two meet) | 8 | 8 |
     | by the sway (how fast each slides across the addresses) | 28 | 26 |

     g decides exactly the pairs with at least one piece beyond D1 (18 = 28 − the 10 pairs inside), and never wrongly.
     The first border is always right but only where two pieces meet on neighbouring addresses. The sway decides every
     pair, near or far, and missed two.

176. **Two kinds of depth, one per reader** (R79). g is the forward sweep's, the parallel reader's magnitude, and it
     saturates: inside D1 it says only "all here". The sway is the serial reader's: motion, read as a ratio (how much
     faster one piece slides than another as the reader moves, a ratio that needs no unit), and it does not saturate. So
     near the reader, order comes from the serial reader's motion, not from g; the first border is the two meeting at
     one address. Answers the open question: "first on the address" is not a second kind of depth, it is where the
     forward sweep stops; the second kind is the serial reader's, and it needs the reader to move.

177. **The ladder from somewhere to nowhere, measured** (*The Ladder*, Sep 28; Tom 12:10 chose it). Readers stand round a
     patch of ground with sixteen posts, four of which are a square, and pool **only relations**: each reader's readings
     of where posts stand are carried into one shared frame through four or more posts already placed, and every post's
     place is the average over the readers that read it. Each rung adds one shared fact. (Readings here are computed and
     rounded to the grain, not rendered; 20 worlds per setting; errors on a patch 100 across.) Coarse grain (1/300):

     | readers | reads per post | relations only: right up to a projective map | as a shape | + "a parallelogram": up to affine | as a shape | + "a square": up to a scale | + "one length": as it stands |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | 1 | 1.0 | 0.45 | 13.7 | 1.45 | 10.2 | 1.45 | 1.49 |
     | 2 | 2.0 | 0.31 | 15.1 | 0.83 | 9.7 | 1.05 | 1.16 |
     | 4 | 3.9 | 0.23 | 17.2 | 0.56 | 9.8 | 0.60 | 0.74 |
     | 8 | 7.8 | 0.17 | 17.1 | 0.51 | 10.0 | 0.53 | 0.54 |

     Fine grain (1/1000): projective 0.13 → 0.05, square 0.50 → 0.20, length 0.50 → 0.22, as readers go 1 → 8.

178. **What the rungs say.** Relations alone give the whole patch right up to a projective map, and more readers only
     sharpen it (0.45 → 0.17); as a shape it stays wrong by about 15 on 100, whatever the number of readers. **No number
     of readers climbs a rung; only a shared fact does.** "These four are a parallelogram" leaves an affine map (a
     shear, still wrong by about 10 as a shape); "they are a square" leaves only a scale; "its side is 20" leaves nothing.
     So the view from nowhere is not assembled from views from somewhere by adding readers: readers give the projective
     world, and each metric rung needs a fact about the world that no reading carries (parallel, right angle, a length),
     shared by all. For a plane, one reader already has the whole projective map; readers add precision (and coverage
     where views are partial). The rungs are Klein's classical ladder of geometries (projective, affine, similarity,
     Euclidean); what is ours is that each rung above the first is a shared fact the readers must be given, not a reading.

179. **The Set: a painted world is exact from one standpoint** (*The Set*, Sep 28; Tom 12:58 raised *The Truman Show*,
     13:03: "this might offer a new way to explore the same ideas"). A harbour with far mountains, a lighthouse, a pier of
     four posts and the sun. Either real (230–420 off) or painted on a dome's wall 60 from the middle: each thing shrunk
     toward the dome's middle until it sits on the wall, which leaves every reading from the middle exactly as it would
     be. **From the painter's one standpoint no reading can tell them apart**: a reader holds the first border along each
     address and its g, not what lies behind. What tells, measured with the reader swayed and a friend 30 to one side
     (values in thousandths):

     | | real | painted |
     |---|---|---|
     | g of the far things | 8–15, each its own | 58–62, all alike |
     | slide per unit of sway | 2.3–4.4, each its own (1/distance) | 14.5–16.7, all alike (the wall's) |
     | angle between the outer mountains, you / friend | 1.002 / 0.981 (2% apart) | 1.002 / 0.756 (25% apart) |
     | sailing north | no wall (64 out) | stopped at 57: the bow touches the sky |

180. **What gives a set away, and what does not.** Every tell is a change of standpoint or a reading the painter could not
     fake for all standpoints at once: the parallel reader's g (the wall is at one distance, so everything painted reads
     one g); the serial reader's sway (everything painted slides together, at the wall's rate); a second reader (relations
     among far things that should hold from both standpoints do not); and contact. **What did not tell:** the cross ratio
     of the pier (you and your friend differed by 4–6% in the real world and 1–2% in the painted one, over two runs: noise
     at this grain, and if anything the wrong way round): painted on a smooth
     wall, four posts in a row stay nearly in a row, so relations among painted things alone are almost those of real
     ones. So a set is exposed by what relates painted things to *where the readers stand* (g, sway, a second
     standpoint), not by relations among the painted things themselves. The world, from somewhere, is what holds up when
     the standpoint changes.

181. **Christof: a painter with the view from nowhere defeats the sway, and nothing else** (*The Set*, v2; Tom 13:19:
     "proceed"). Sometimes the director repaints the wall every moment so that, from wherever you now stand, each painted
     thing lies exactly on your line of sight to where the real one would be (scaled about your eye, so every turn you
     read is the real one's). Measured with the same sway and friend (thousandths):

     | | real | painted | repainted for you |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | g of the far things | 8–15 | 58–62 | 58–62 |
     | slide per unit of sway | 2.3–4.4 | 14.6–16.7 | **2.3–4.4** |
     | outer mountains, you / friend | 1.003 / 0.981 | 1.002 / 0.756 | 1.002 / 0.756 |
     | sailing north | no wall | the bow touches the sky | the bow touches the sky |

182. **What a director who sees everything cannot fake.** The sway (the serial reader's depth) is a relation between
     *one* reader's readings over its own motion, and a painter who knows where that reader stands can match it
     exactly. Three things stay out of reach. **g**: the painted thing is where it is, on the wall, and the forward sweep
     meets it there; the painter moves where it is seen, not how far it is. **A second reader**: one painting cannot lie
     on two readers' lines of sight at once (the world is one; R83), so relations between standpoints break. **Contact**.
     So the parallel reader's g and the company of readers are what the view from nowhere cannot counterfeit to a view from
     somewhere; the serial reader alone can be fooled by a painter who follows it.

183. **Two paintings on one wall: each reader sees the other's as a ghost** (*The Set*, v3; Tom 13:38: "proceed").
     Christof paints a second copy of everything for your friend, repainted for where they stand. Your line of sight and
     your friend's to a real mountain meet only at the mountain, never on the wall, so the two copies are two panels in
     two places; one world (R83), and each reader reads both. You read **18 far things where the other worlds give 9**:
     two suns, doubled mountains (screenshot). g still reads the wall (58–63), the slide of your own copies still matches
     the real (2.2–4.1), the bow still touches the sky.

184. **The last loophole, and what would survive it (a prediction, not measured).** Christof's copies here are the real
     things scaled about each eye, so their facing is fixed by the picture they must make. A painter free to paint
     *anamorphically* (the picture for one eye laid on any surface, distorted so that from that eye it is exact) could
     turn each copy edge-on to the other reader, and each would then read only its own (edge-on reads as nothing, item
     171). Then every relation between readers would also agree with the real world, since each reads exactly the real
     picture. Prediction: **only g and contact would remain**: the parallel reader's forward sweep meets the wall at the
     wall's distance, and the bow meets the wall. The renderer cannot yet draw an anamorphic panel, so this is untested.

185. **The anamorphic Christof: only g and contact survive** (*The Set*, v4; Tom 13:55: "proceed on the next build").
     The renderer now draws anamorphic panels: a panel carries a picture painted for one eye (a point on it is painted
     if the line from that eye through it meets the real thing), exact from that eye whatever way the panel faces. The
     painter puts each reader's copy where that reader's line of sight meets the wall, turned edge-on to the other
     reader, and repaints every moment. Measured (thousandths), against the real world:

     | | real | two paintings (item 183) | anamorphic |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | far things you read | 9 | 16–18 | **9** |
     | outer mountains, you / friend | 1.002 / 0.981 | ghosts | **1.002 / 0.981** |
     | slide per unit of sway | 2.3–4.4 | 2.2–4.1 | 2.4–5.4 |
     | g of the far things | 8–15 | 58–63 | **58–87** |
     | sailing north | no wall | the bow touches the sky | the bow touches the sky |

     Item 184's prediction held: with each copy hidden edge-on from the other reader, each reads exactly the real picture,
     so every relation between readers agrees with the real world (the friend's angle is the real one to three places),
     and the ghosts are gone. **What stays out of reach is g** (the forward sweep meets the painted panel, 60–90 off,
     where the real thing would be 230–420) **and contact.** (One of the pier's slides read 5.4, off the real 4.2; the
     anamorphic panels are large and turned, and a picture near their edge is read less cleanly.)

186. **The end of the Truman line.** The view from nowhere, with a painter who knows every standpoint, can counterfeit
     everything the serial reader reads (the turn, the sway) and everything readers share (relations between standpoints),
     for any number of readers, by giving each its own exact picture hidden from the rest. It cannot counterfeit the
     parallel reader's g, because g is where the forward sweep actually stops, and it cannot counterfeit contact. In the
     corpus's terms: **the serial reader and the company of readers can be deceived; the forward sweep cannot.** R79's
     three sweeps split here: left and right carry the picture, which can be painted; forward carries g, which cannot.

187. **The even sky** (*The Even Sky*; Tom 14:18: "what about 3d cauchy", 14:19: "ok"). Every demo so far laid its
     addresses as **crossed turns**: evenly left to right, then evenly up and down at each. Laid on the whole front half,
     that has two poles, straight up and straight down, where every left-to-right address bunches into one point, so it
     has a built-in up. The demo lays 1,600 addresses three ways: crossed turns; the **even sky**, evenly over the whole
     front half (the 2d Cauchy of item 133, h³/2π on the bar); and **crossed Cauchys**, evenly in each reading on its own
     (a flat chart). Eight equal balls stand on a ring at the same v/h from home (1.19) and distance; the reader tilts its
     head. Addresses on a ball, fewest to most:

     | | crossed turns | even sky | crossed Cauchys |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | level | 32–52 (1.63) | 47–53 (1.13) | 42–50 (1.19) |
     | tilted ⅛ turn | 32–52 (1.63) | 47–53 (1.13) | 42–50 (1.19) |
     | which balls get most | the two at the poles | none in particular | the four on the axes |
     | g of the up ball ×1000 | 885–887 | 887–889 | 886–889 |
     | share within the corner (v/h < 1) | 20% | **29%** | 22% |
     | median v/h | 2.69 | **1.73** | 2.19 |

     Under crossed turns the balls at the poles get 1.6 times the addresses of the balls at the sides (the layout's
     1/cos of the up-and-down turn: 1.56 at this ring), and when the reader tilts, the surplus stays with the poles and
     passes to whichever balls now sit there. It belongs to the layout, not the world. Crossed Cauchys favour the four
     axes over the diagonals (1.19), fourfold. The even sky favours no direction: its 47–53 is the grain of the lay
     (the spiral that lays it), not a direction, and tilting changes nothing. **g is the same in all three and at every
     tilt** (to the grain), as it must be: g is read along one address, and no layout changes where the forward sweep
     stops; nor then does contact (R82), which is two g's on one address. The even sky's corner and median are item 133's
     exactly: 29% within the corner, median v/h = √3 (60°).

188. **What the 3d sweeps are.** A reader in 3d space has two readings and one magnitude (item 133). Laying its two
     readings as two turns crossed (left–right, then up–down) gives it a preferred up, poles, and a corner and median
     that depend on the layout; only the even sky reads the front half the same whichever way the reader's head is
     turned about home. So R79's across sweeps in 3d are best read as **one even sweep over the front half**, not
     left–right and up–down laid separately; "left–right" and "up–down" are then two ways of naming a direction in it,
     not two sweeps. Items 153–156 still hold (both across readings are needed and must be crossed at each address);
     what changes is how the addresses are laid. The earlier demos' layout is what put the poles in them. **Ruled
     (R84, 14:27, "ok, proceed").**

189. **Near order, tilted** (*Near Order, Tilted*; after R84). Near Order (items 175–176) ordered pieces by how many
     addresses they slide across as the reader sways, on crossed turns. Eight balls at depths 22 to 108, nearer ones
     low and farther ones high, each sized to look the same; the reader sways along its own left–right. Pairs of 28
     ordered rightly by slide:

     | | crossed turns | even sky | crossed Cauchys |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | level | 24 | **28** | 25 |
     | tilted ¹⁄₁₂ to ⅙ turn, level | 19–27 | 27–28 | 20–27 |
     | tilted ¼ turn (sway up and down) | 28 | 28 | 28 |
     | 193 random turns and tilts, mean (fewest) | 24.8 (16) | **27.2 (23)** | 24.7 (16) |
     | all 28 right, of 193 | 29 | 105 | 27 |
     | by g, every case | 28 | 28 | 28 |

     On crossed turns a high ball crosses more left-to-right addresses per unit of sway than a ball at the same depth
     level with the reader (by 1/cos of its up-and-down turn), so far high balls read nearer than near low ones. The
     surplus is the layout's: tilt the reader a quarter turn, so it sways up and down along the evenly laid up-down
     turn, and crossed turns order all 28. The even sky's misses are the sway's own geometry, the same for any layout:
     a ball off to the side of the sway line slides less than one square to it. g orders all 28 everywhere.

190. **The earlier demos' poles.** Of the findings made on crossed turns, the ones read in addresses crossed (the sway,
     the slide) carry the layout's up; the ones read along one address (g, the first border, contact) do not. Near
     Order's 26/28 by sway (item 176) was measured with things near level, where crossed turns and the even sky agree;
     away from level it would have been lower. Under R84 the slide is counted on the even sky, and near order by sway
     is then limited only by the sway's geometry.

191. **The ladder off the plane, measured** (*The Ladder in 3d*; Tom 14:52 "proceed"). Twenty-four posts anywhere in a
     block 100 across; eight are the corners of a cube of side 20. Readers stand round it 70 out, spread up and down, each
     facing somewhere across it, and read each post as a direction on its own even sky (R84), rounded to its nearest
     address. They pool **only relations**: two readers find each other from ten or more shared posts (the relation
     between two skies, no unit and no angle shared), and the pair places every post both read, up to a projective map of
     space; a five-post frame (a choice, nothing about the world) fixes that map; every further reader joins through
     seven or more posts already placed, and every post is re-placed from all the readers that read it. 20 worlds per
     setting; errors on the block 100 across. Coarse grain (1/300):

     | readers | reads per post | relations only: right up to a projective map | as a shape | + "a parallelepiped": up to affine | as a shape | + "a cube": up to a scale | + "one length": as it stands |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | 1 | – | nothing placed | – | – | – | – | – |
     | 2 | 2.0 | 0.25 | 13.2 | 0.71 | 8.3 | 0.80 | 0.81 |
     | 3 | 3.0 | 0.12 | 15.9 | 0.24 | 8.2 | 0.28 | 0.28 |
     | 4 | 3.9 | 0.10 | 16.9 | 0.20 | 8.2 | 0.21 | 0.22 |
     | 8 | 7.8 | 0.08 | 15.7 | 0.16 | 8.3 | 0.17 | 0.18 |

     Fine grain (1/1000): projective 0.07 → 0.02, cube 0.34 → 0.05, length 0.37 → 0.06, as readers go 2 → 8. Readers
     all level instead of spread up and down: 0.22 / 0.66 / 0.67 with 2, 0.09 / 0.20 / 0.22 with 8 (projective / cube /
     length), the same as spread. With no grain every rung is exact (below 10⁻⁹), so what is left is the grain's.

192. **What the 3d rungs say.** The ladder is the same ladder: relations give the whole block right up to a projective map
     of space and more readers only sharpen it (0.25 → 0.08), while as a shape it stays wrong by about 15 on 100 whatever
     the number; **no number of readers climbs a rung; only a shared fact does.** Three things change off the plane.
     (a) **The bottom rung costs a second reader.** On the ground one reader already had the whole projective map (a
     plane's readings are a projective image of it); in space one reader's readings are directions and place nothing:
     two readers' relation is the first rung (it is Two Lines' finding, items 163–164, now for every post at once).
     (b) **Joining costs more.** A reader joined the ground map through four shared posts; it joins space through six or
     more (seven here), and a first pair needs seven or more in common (ten here). (c) **The facts must be solid.** On
     the ground a parallelogram gave affine and a square gave a scale; in space the same rungs take a parallelepiped (three
     directions, three pairs of parallel faces) and a cube (right angles in three directions, equal edges). By counting,
     not measured: a parallelogram in space fixes two of the three numbers the affine rung needs and a square two of the
     five the similarity rung needs, so planar facts leave space short of the rung. Spread up and down made no
     difference here, unlike Two Lines (items 163–164): there each reader passed a line built from plane frames; here the
     readers pass their readings of shared posts directly. The forward reading (g) is left out, as on the ground;
     whether one reader with g stands on a higher rung than two without is open.

193. **What a reader's own sky and its g buy on the ladder** (*The Ladder in 3d*, v2; Tom 15:10 "proceed"). The ladders
     so far passed cross ratios only: nothing that depends on how a reader lays its addresses. A reader's sky is laid
     evenly (R84), so it also knows the **turn** between any two of its addresses; and it reads **g** along each, in its
     own unit (D1 = 1; the world's length is not known to it). Three ways of passing, same worlds, errors on the block
     100 across, coarse grain (1/300), 20 worlds:

     | passing | readers | right up to a projective map | shape, up to a scale, no shared fact | as it stands, no shared length | + cube + side 20 |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | cross ratios only | 1 | nothing placed | – | – | – |
     | | 2 | 0.25 | 13.2 | 37 | 0.81 |
     | | 8 | 0.08 | 15.7 | 38 | 0.18 |
     | + own turns | 1 | nothing placed | – | – | – |
     | | 2 | 0.25 | **0.26** | 39 | 0.98 |
     | | 8 | 0.07 | **0.13** | 40 | 0.18 |
     | + own g | 1 | 0.44 | **0.51** | 36 | 1.09 |
     | | 2 | 0.30 | **0.35** | 38 | 0.81 |
     | | 8 | 0.15 | **0.17** | 38 | 0.44 |

     Fine grain (1/1000): shape up to a scale 0.08 → 0.04 (turns, 2 → 8 readers), 0.13 → 0.05 (g, 1 → 8). With no grain
     both are exact (below 10⁻¹¹).

194. **Where each rung comes from.** Item 178's "no number of readers climbs a rung; only a shared fact does" holds for
     readers who pass cross ratios alone. **A reader's own even sky is itself the fact the middle rungs need**: two
     readers who pass their own turns have the shape right up to a scale with no parallelepiped and no cube (0.26, and
     the cube adds nothing); one reader with its own g has it too (0.51), placing each post along its address at 1/g.
     What no reading gives, in any of the three, is **the unit**: as it stands the map is off by about 37 on 100 until a
     length is shared, because every reader's turns are unitless and its g is in its own D1 (items 165–166: g does not
     cross standpoints). So the ladder is climbed three ways: the projective rung by relations between readers (two
     needed off the plane); affine and similarity together by the reader's own lay (its turns) or its own forward sweep
     (its g); the last rung only by a shared length. g sharpens with distance less well than turns do (its grain in depth
     grows with the square of distance: 0.51 with one reader against 0.26 for two with turns), and more readers sharpen
     both. *For the ground (item 178) this is expected by the same counting but not measured.* This bears on the open item
     "the turn, demoted and needed": the turn is what carries the middle rungs.

195. **Pieces instead of balls** (Tom 15:24 uploaded the rung editor, 3d.html, and chose "pieces into the demos"). Its
     presets are r(θ, h), a radius at each turn around a piece's axis and each height up it; the demos now cast each
     address against them (the first border where the address enters). *The Even Sky* (3,136 addresses): eight equal
     pieces lying round the ring, foot toward home, each showing the reader the same face. Addresses on a piece, most over
     fewest: knight 1.61 on crossed turns, 1.10 on the even sky, 1.18 on crossed Cauchys; rook 1.28 / 1.06 / 1.14; star
     1.37 / 1.12 / 1.19; cube 1.55 / 1.02 / 1.32; the even sky's figures unchanged by tilting, the others' surplus moving
     with the layout, as with the balls (item 187). On crossed turns the pieces at the sides are visibly squeezed along
     the ring and the ones at the poles stretched. *Near Order, Tilted* with eight different pieces (knight, rook, bishop,
     queen, pawn, vase, gem, star), each sized to look the same: the same ordering results as item 189 (slides are
     measured at each piece's middle), and g orders all 28 read off the pieces themselves.

196. **A piece is read from inside, and which inside reader holds it** (Tom 15:32 "ok, proceed"; computed, not rendered,
     `store.cjs`, `store2.cjs`). The rung editor stores each piece as readings taken from its own axis: θ evenly around ×
     h evenly up, the payload the first border out (r). That is a reader inside the piece, the serial reading of a
     shape. Three inside readers at the same budget (about 2,048 addresses), each address owning its cell (the nearest
     reading holds): the editor's axis reader (64 × 32); a reader at the piece's middle on crossed turns (64 around × 32
     up-down); the same reader on the even sky (2,048). Share of the piece's volume read wrongly (150,000 points):

     | piece | axis, θ × h | middle, crossed turns | middle, even sky |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | sphere / egg | 3.1 / 3.2 | 3.6 / 3.4 | **2.8 / 2.6** |
     | cube / rounded cube | 5.5 / 4.5 | 3.8 / 2.3 | **2.9 / 1.8** |
     | diamond | 4.2 | 3.5 | **2.6** |
     | pawn, bishop, queen | **3.5, 2.6, 2.5** | 10.0, 8.1, 7.9 | 5.6, 5.4, 5.8 |
     | rook, knight, cone | **1.9, 1.5, 1.9** | 13.7, 8.3, 8.5 | 13.0, 6.8, 6.0 |
     | vase, quartz, twisted | **2.6, 2.6, 3.4** | 6.7, 6.1, 7.9 | 4.5, 5.1, 7.4 |
     | star, spiked | **6.4, 5.6** | 12.2, 6.9 | 13.1, 6.8 |
     | gem, brilliant | **4.6, 4.6** | 5.1, 5.9 | 5.0, 5.9 |
     | cylinder | **0.0** | 5.9 | 4.7 |

     From the middle, the even sky holds the piece better than crossed turns for 15 of 19 pieces and as well for the
     rest (R84 again, now for a reader inside). But the axis reader holds most pieces best: the tall ones (rook 1.9
     against 13.0) because a point in the middle reads their long ends at a graze through few addresses; only the
     compact pieces (sphere, egg, cube, rounded cube, diamond) are held better from a point on the even sky.

197. **What each standpoint grazes** (and a prediction killed). I expected the editor's cap trouble to be the poles of a
     crossed lay. It is not: the axis reader has no poles, and in the cell reading its error does not gather at the
     caps by lay. It gathers where the surface **turns past the corner**, facing more along the axis than across it
     (|dr/dh| per unit height > 1), where its level addresses meet the surface at a graze: such surface is 5% of the
     bishop's and queen's skin and carries 15–17% of the error; rook 1% → 18%; vase 4% → 14%; cube 14% → 55%. The same
     for the point reader, whose error sits on the long sides it reads end-on. So **every standpoint holds best what
     faces it and worst what it grazes, and the corner (v = h) is where a surface stops facing it**; the lay only shares
     the addresses out (even sky: equally). This is the plane finding again (item 162: readers must look down on the
     plane, not graze it), now inside a shape. The editor's own cap artefacts come from its reconstruction between
     readings (the cosine and bridge kernels), which this test does not touch.

198. **A company of inside readers** (Tom 15:43 "Proceed"; `company.cjs`). The same budget (about 2,048 addresses)
     split among inside standpoints: the axis reader (64 × 24) with a point near each end (256 each, even sky); three
     points on the axis (683 each). **How readings join** matters first: if a point is inside whenever any reader reads
     it inside, the coarse readers' big cells overreach and the company is far worse than one reader (21–40% wrong); if
     each point is decided by the reader whose cell there is finest, the company is sound. Even then, at a fixed budget
     it does not beat the axis reader alone, except the pawn: rook 1.7% alone against 3.8% with the end points, queen 2.7
     against 3.7, knight 1.8 against 3.9, pawn 3.5 against 2.9 (three points). The axis already faces nearly all of a
     piece's skin; the grazed part (item 197) is a few per cent of it, and addresses moved there cost more on the
     sides than they gain. (The cube goes wrong with three points, 42%, because two of them stand outside it.)

199. **What no outside standpoint reads** (`vessel.cjs`). The vase welded into a vessel, as the editor's weld does: wall
     0.03, floor at h = 0.15, open at the mouth (radius 0.07 against the belly's 0.19). Readers stand evenly round a
     sphere 3 across; a point of the hollow is read if some reader's line to it meets no wall. Share of the hollow read:

     | outside readers | 1 | 4 | 16 | 64 | 128 | 256 | any standpoint outside at all | one reader inside |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | hollow read | 0% | 1.3% | 8.3% | 32% | 53% | 79% | 98.3% | **100%** |

     From outside, the hollow is read only through the mouth: each point of it has a narrow cone of lines that escape, so
     the readers needed grow roughly in proportion to how much of the sky that cone misses (about doubling the share per
     doubling of readers). 1.7% is never read from any standpoint outside, however many: the corners of the floor
     tucked under the lower wall where it narrows (heights 0.15–0.42). One reader inside reads all of it. **So the
     parallel company outside cannot reach what a single reader inside receives** (read all at once here; item 212 checks it
     for a reader reading one address at a time); this is the Sep 22 split
     between the parallel reader and the serial reader, on one object, with numbers. A reading taken from inside, as the rung editor's is, can hold what no outside company reaches.
     *The Vessel* (demo, Tom 15:47 "Yes") shows it: 2,400 points of the hollow, readers doubled from 1 to 512; read
     from outside 1.1% (4), 8.5% (16), 32% (64), 78% (256), 96% (512); 0.7% no standpoint outside reads (with finer
     checking than the 1.7% above); one reader inside, 100%.

200. **Any object can be the standpoint** (*A World of Pieces*; Tom 17:26: "if we created a world of the objects, could
     we pick one of those objects to be the view from somewhere", 17:27 "Yes"). Seven of the rung editor's pieces and
     the vessel stand on a board; tap one to become it. A piece's reader stands on its own head; the vessel's stands
     inside its hollow. Each reads its whole sky (2,000 addresses, evenly laid) once:

     | standpoint | yourself, share of your whole sky | reads the other pieces | read back by them |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | knight / rook / bishop / queen / pawn, on the head | 37.5 / 42.2 / 34.2 / 38.0 / 37.8% | all 7 | 6 of 7 (not the vessel) |
     | star / gem, on the tip | 20.1 / 20.0% | 6 of 7 (not each other: each hidden from the other) | 5 of 7 (not each other, not the vessel) |
     | vessel, inside | **99.6%** (the world gets in on 0.4%, through the mouth) | **none** | all 7 |

     (a) **A standpoint reads itself.** Standing on its head, a piece's own body is its nearest first border and fills a
     third or more of its sky (less on a pointed top); its shape is the near part of its own view from somewhere, the
     same kind of reading the editor stores for it from its axis. With D1 = 8, the part of the sky where g reads 1 is
     exactly the self here, but only because nothing else stands within 8 of any head; that is a choice of D1 against the
     board's spacing, not a finding. (b) **Reading is not symmetric between standpoints.** A clear line runs both ways,
     but a reader reads other things' surfaces from its own point: every piece reads the vessel, and the vessel, reading
     from inside its hollow, reads none of them; the world reaches it only as sky through the mouth, the same narrow
     cone by which the outside reaches the hollow (item 199), from the other end.

201. **The orbits, from somewhere** (*The Sky from Earth*; Tom 18:48: "could we observe the orbits from somewhere not
     anywhere?", 18:49 "yes"). The sun and six planets on their true orbits (J2000 elements; the view from nowhere,
     used only to make the readings and to score). A reader stands on one planet and keeps a diary: each night, where
     the sun and each planet stand on its sky, as turns in a frame held against the far stars, rounded to its grain.
     From that diary alone (120 years of it; `orbits-core.js`), with nothing else given: (1) its **year**, from the
     sun's drift round its sky; (2) each planet's **period**, from its drift and its passes across the reader's side of
     the sun (for an outer planet fitted over whole turns of its own, the loops fitted out); (3) its **own path**, Kepler's
     way: the planet whose returns find the reader at the most different places (for Earth, Mars) stands in one line
     with the sun at one opposition; each time it returns to that place, one period later, the reader is elsewhere, and
     the triangle sun, reader, planet gives the reader's distance **in the unit sun-to-planet**; (4) **every other
     planet**, where the lines from the reader's place on two nights one of its periods apart cross. Shape off, as a share
     of each orbit's size (from Earth, at Tycho's grain of 1′ / at 0.2°):

     | Mercury | Venus | Earth (you) | Mars | Jupiter | Saturn |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | 0.13% / 0.6% | 0.13% / 0.6% | 0.16% / 0.3% | 0.21% / 0.8% | 1.6% / 2.0% | 8.1% / 8.3% |

     **With one shared length** (the reader's distance to Mars on one day, given as radar or a transit of Venus gave
     it), the whole map is in AU: off by 0.001–0.003 AU for Mercury to Mars, 0.09 for Jupiter, 0.8 for Saturn (its
     period, from four of its turns in the diary, is 17 days long, and it is triangulated from lines under a few degrees
     apart). From Mercury the shapes come out within 0.7–1.8% (Saturn 14%); from Mars within 1.3–6.7%; from Saturn, whose
     own year is only four times in the diary, 4–33%.

202. **What the orbits say about the view from somewhere.** The sky from a planet is the reader's serial record: one
     night at a time, turns only; the loops (the planet stopping, running backward, going on) are what an orbit is from
     somewhere. Every rung of the ladder shows up as it did with posts (items 191–194): **no reading gives a distance**;
     two standpoints (the same place of the planet, the reader somewhere else one period on) give the shape; the
     reader's own turns carry it right **up to a scale**, and the **unit** comes only from one shared length, which is
     how it came historically (the transits of Venus, 1761 and 1769). Two things the posts did not show: (a) **time
     gives the second standpoint.** One reader, moving, is its own second reader, provided the thing read comes back
     to the same place, so a period read off the diary stands in for a second reader standing still; (b) **the far stars
     are the shared frame**: the reader's turns are held against them, so no rotation is left to find, only the scale.
     (c) **Where the reader's own path is sampled decides how well it is held.** The planet triangulated from must
     return while the reader is at many different places (from Earth, Mars: 687/365 = 1.88 spreads Earth's places all
     round); and its returns must be taken near the line moment, since each return carries the period's small error
     forward: from Mars, with returns taken up to 60 out from the first line moment in the diary, Mars's own path came
     out 11% wrong; with returns within 20 of a line moment in the middle of the diary, under 5%.

203. **The maze from inside** (*The Maze from Inside*; Tom 04:00 Sep 29: "what about mazes?", 04:02 "yes"; `mz.cjs`).
     A walled maze (8 × 8 cells, 10 across, walls taller than the eye); its plan is the view from nowhere, kept to make
     readings and to score. The walker reads its sky (first border and g along each address; for its map, 90 addresses
     laid evenly round the level of its eye) and knows its own acts, each step and quarter turn, which it **carries**
     forward with a small error (3% on each turn with a little scatter, 2% on each step): its frame is a carry. Its map is
     every reading placed where it believes it stood. It hugs the left wall (its choices read from its sky: a way is
     open if the address a quarter turn left, ahead or right meets its first border beyond half a cell) once round the whole maze (630 walked; five
     mazes each; errors in the maze's units, a cell 10 across):

     | condition | map off | its place off | its heading off |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | nothing carried wrongly | 0.02 | 0.00 | 0.00° |
     | no sky | 2.9 | 3.6 | 7.7° |
     | a far tower over the walls | 2.2 | 9.7 | 0.05° |
     | closing the loop at the start post | 3.0 | 0.04 | 7.7° |
     | tower and closing | **1.2** | **0.04** | **0.05°** |

204. **What the maze adds.** (a) **The maze is the case with no stars.** From a planet the reader's turns were held
     against the far stars, so only the scale was left (item 202); in a maze the heading is only what the walker carries
     from turn to turn, and it drifts (7.7° after one round). That is the drift *The Journey* named (R6: "identity
     erodes under drift and has no checksum"): the drift is the missing shared frame. (b) **One far thing is a star.** A
     tower far off over the walls, read whenever it stands in view, holds the heading to 0.05°; its turn is a reading,
     and a reading of something far enough does not drift. It holds nothing else: the place still drifts, by the carried
     error in the steps (9.7 here, worse than with no sky only because, without the tower, the heading's errors
     happened to fold the path back). (c) **Closing a loop is the checksum, and it checks only what the closing thing
     carries.** Back at the start post the walker knows where it is and spreads the error back along the way: its place
     comes right (0.04), its heading does not (a round post carries no turn), so the map stays bent. With the tower as
     well, both come right and the map is off by 1.2, a tenth of a cell. (d) The rungs again: the tower gives the
     **rotation**, closing gives the **place**, and the step's own error is the **unit**, which no reading gives: the
     walker's step is its unit, as D1 is. *The Journey*'s w = +1 lap is the same checksum in its topological form: the
     loop closes, so the frame can be certified.

205. **Escaping a maze with islands: strategy by strategy** (Tom 04:24 "proceed"; `strategies.cjs`). A perfect maze
     with some walls taken out (so walls form islands and the ways form loops), one gap in the outer wall, the walker
     starting in the middle, facing its chosen way. Its moves are true; what it believes of them, its count of turns, is
     carried with a drift that always leans the same way (so many degrees added on every quarter turn). 300 mazes each:

     | strategy | what it needs | escaped, 12 × 12 (12% of walls out) | escaped, 20 × 20 (20% out) |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | wall-follower | nothing carried: keeps the wall where it was | 21% | 3% |
     | Pledge, count exact | a turn count, carried exactly | 100% | 100% |
     | Pledge, drift 2° / 5° / 10° / 20° per turn | the same count, carried with drift | 100 / 100 / 85 / 42% | 100 / 99 / 71 / 22% |
     | Pledge, any of those drifts, far tower in view | the count's fraction of a turn read off the tower | **100%** (as exact) | **100%** (as exact) |
     | Trémaux | marks written on the ways walked | 100% (more moves: 59 against 38) | 100% (133 against 57) |

206. **What each strategy needs, and what the tower gives Pledge.** The accounting of items 203–204 predicted every row.
     The wall-follower carries nothing and so cannot drift, and it is blind to islands: it escapes only when the first
     wall it meets joins the outer wall. Pledge escapes any maze because it carries **a winding count** (net turning,
     *The Journey*'s w), and its guarantee is exactly as good as that carry: drift erodes it. A far tower does not give the
     count (a reading gives the facing only to within a whole turn), but it gives the **fraction** of a turn exactly, and
     the carry then only has to keep the **whole turns**, which it does as long as it drifts less than half a turn
     between readings: read fraction plus carried whole turns is the winding exactly, and Pledge with the tower escapes
     every maze in as few moves as with an exact count. Trémaux needs nothing carried and no sky: it writes its memory
     into the world, so every return is a closed loop (the start post, item 204, at every junction), and it pays in moves.
     So: **reading gives the part of a turn, the carry gives the count of turns, and shared marks give the places**; the
     winding, a topological number, is the one thing here that needs both a reading and a carry.

207. **tb_algebra and the lay, a tension with R84** (Tom 04:37: "could you use tb_algebra"; `polar.cjs`). *The Tangent
     Bridge Theorem* presents a reader's atoms continuously (exact at the atoms, bounded between) with its kernel, but
     only on a product of circles (§12); and its §15.2 (Jul 2026, conditional) finds that the budget selects **nested
     apertures, a circle of circles, over any monolithic spherical read**, the sphere's poles being the surveyor's
     completion. The even sky of R84 is a monolithic spherical read. A circle of circles need not put its poles up and
     down (crossed turns): put them at **home** and behind, every great circle through home is one of the line's own
     readers, laid evenly in turn. Measured against the other two (3,136 addresses each):

     | | crossed turns | even sky (R84) | circle of circles, pole at home |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | share within the corner / median v/h | 20% / 2.62 | 29% / 1.73 | **50% / 1.06** |
     | equal balls on a ring 50° out, most / fewest, any tilt | 1.31–1.68 | 1.03 | **1.00** |
     | the same, a ring 15° out | 1.00–1.11 | 1.13–1.21 | **1.00** |
     | near order by sway, of 28, tilt 0 / 45° / 90° | 22 / 27 / 28 | 28 / 28 / 28 | 25 / 27 / 27 |

     With its pole at home the circle of circles has no up (tilting about home changes nothing, exactly), and it keeps
     the line's own property that the corner is the median (50% within v = h); it bunches its addresses toward home, the
     fisheye, so things near home cross more addresses as the reader sways and near order by sway suffers (25 of 28).
     **Ruled (R85, Sep 29 05:04, "3"): both, in two roles** — the even sky to read and measure with, the circle of circles
     through home to present on (the Tangent Bridge's kernel).

208. **The views presented by the bridge** (after R85; `bridge-view.js`). The maze and the world of pieces now read
     their whole sky on the presentation lay (64 great circles through home × 96 leaves round each, 6,144 addresses; 3,072
     and 12,288 at the other grains) and present it with the Tangent Bridge's even-N kernel, first along each great
     circle, then round home (the spokes are themselves leaves of the circle round home). Checked: partition of unity and
     exactness at the leaves to 10⁻¹⁴; a smooth field over the sky presented to within 1.3 × 10⁻⁵ everywhere. What the
     picture shows is the theorem: smooth between the readings, finer toward home (the pole of the lay), and **ringing at
     every edge** (a wall against the sky, one's own head against the board): the bridge's overshoot at the bandwidth
     horizon (tb_algebra §16), not a fault of the drawing. The cells (the even sky's reading, one cell to an address)
     stay one button away.

209. **Address → address → payload: a live relay and a stored one** (*Mirror and Painting*; Tom 05:23: "what about
     address to address to payload, what does one more level of indirection buy us", 05:26 "proceed"; `relay.cjs`). A
     wall hides three balls from the reader; a panel beside it shows them, either as a **mirror** (it holds nothing of its
     own: an address that meets it goes on along its reflection and reads what is there now) or as a **painting** of
     exactly what the mirror showed from the reader's standpoint (it holds its own record: the address stops at the
     paint). 20,000 addresses laid evenly, facing the panel; the panel covers 639 of them.

     | | mirror | painting |
     |---|---|---|
     | read directly, over the wall | none of the three | none |
     | read through the panel (addresses on each ball) | 17 / 41 / 16 | 17 / 41 / 16 |
     | same colour as the other, from the painter's standpoint | **100%** | **100%** |
     | the same, after a sway of 0.25 / 0.5 / 1 to the side | 82 / 65 / 58% | |
     | g ×1000 on balls 1 / 2 / 3 (true path 15.8 / 14.1 / 18.5) | **254 / 283 / 216** | 417 / 403 / 433 |
     | slide per unit of sway ×1000 | **61 / 69 / 51** | 100 / 99 / 102 |
     | nearest first, by g or by slide | **2, 1, 3** (right) | 3, 1, 2 (the paint's own nearness, backwards) |

210. **What the indirection passes, and what it can only fake.** One more level of indirection **reaches**: a single
     reader reads three things no line of its own can, and from its standpoint the two relays are identical, address
     for address. What passes through depends on whether the middle address **holds a payload of its own**. A live relay
     holds nothing, so the forward sweep goes on through it: g is the whole way (the path through the panel) and the sway
     slides the images by their true distances along that path, so near order survives the hop. A stored relay holds its
     record, so the forward sweep stops at it: every image reads at the paint's distance and slides with the paint, and
     near order is lost (here reversed, by the panel's own slant). This is The Set's tell (items 179–186) from the other
     side: a painted world is a stored relay, and g and contact are what a stored relay cannot pass. It is also the
     line between the orbits' diary (a stored relay across time: the planet's turn *then*, handed on as a record, so only
     turns, never distance) and a mirror (live, so the forward sweep crosses). What neither relay gives: anything off
     the lines through the panel; and the unit, which the mirror passes only as the reader's own (its D1).

211. **The serial reader** (*The Serial Reader*; Tom 05:35 "proceed"; `serial.cjs`). Every demo before this read its
     whole sky at one instant, a moment the game loop supplies (R83). This reader reads **one address at a time**, in an
     order of its own, while the world moves; it holds each address as it was when last read. 16,000 addresses laid
     evenly over the middle 115° of its sky; three orders: the spiral from home outward (the even sky's own order), round
     home (a line through home sweeping from home to the horizon, turn by turn: the circle of circles, R85), and rows.
     Three moving balls (across at up to 4.5 a second, round at up to 6, bobbing at up to 2.3), still posts. Against a
     parallel reading at the end of each sweep, averaged over eight sweeps; addresses on each ball, received / parallel,
     and how far its received middle is off, in steps between addresses:

     | order | sweep | ball 1 (across) | ball 2 (round) | ball 3 (bobbing) |
     |---|---|---|---|---|
     | spiral from home | ¼ s / 1 s / 4 s | 199/184, 6.5 · 156/184, 20 · 153/184, 56 | 73/66, 6.5 · 48/62, 28 · 80/62, 5 | 517/516, 4.5 · 543/516, 14 · 576/516, 12 |
     | round home | ¼ s / 1 s / 4 s | 183/184, 3.6 · 180/184, 9.5 · 207/184, 28 | 56/61, 4.9 · 45/61, 16 · 42/61, 15 | 515/516, 1.7 · 640/516, 6.6 · 621/516, 11 |
     | rows | ¼ s / 1 s / 4 s | 186/184, 3.7 · 219/184, 12 · 185/184, 41 | 64/61, 3.0 · 34/61, 17 · 43/61, 30 | 516/516, 2.0 · 553/516, 8.1 · 489/516, 10 |

     With nothing moving, the serial record is the parallel reading exactly, whatever the order (the few per cent by which
     the still posts differ above are the moving balls hiding them at different moments). The reader swaying sideways at
     2 a second through one 1 s sweep: its still posts are displaced by up to 3 steps in the spiral, 11 round home, 17 in
     rows, by when each was read.

212. **What the serial reader receives.** (a) **Of a still world it receives everything**, exactly, whatever its order:
     time does not enter. So item 199 holds for a serial reader too, for a still hollow: one reader inside receives all
     of it, a sweep at a time. (Item 199 called the reader inside "serial" when the demo read all at once; this is the
     check it needed.) (b) **Its record is a stored relay across time** (item 210): each address hands on what was there
     when it was read. A moving thing comes out where it was when its addresses were read, off by its speed times that
     age, and stretched, shrunk or torn according to whether the sweep ran with it, against it, or crossed it in two
     passes (the round ball, 42–56 of 61 addresses, split). (c) **The order is a lay in time**, as the address lay is a
     lay in turn: every order distorts, differently, and none is free of it; the reader's own motion is written in by the
     same rule (the spiral, which reads home first, barely moved the posts near home). (d) What the serial reader has that
     the record alone does not: it **knows its order**, so it knows when each address was read; with its own clock it can
     carry each reading to a common moment, which is Common Beat's correction (items 169–170) done address by address.
     Measured in item 213.

213. **Carrying each reading to a common moment** (Tom 05:40 "proceed"; `carry.cjs`). The serial reader knows when it read
     each address. From its last two sweeps it finds how each thing's middle moved (the middle of the addresses that read
     it, at their mean moment), and carries every reading of that thing on to the end of the sweep. Centre off, in steps
     between addresses, and spread (received ÷ parallel), as read → carried:

     | | ¼ s sweeps | 1 s sweeps |
     |---|---|---|
     | a ball moving **straight** at a steady pace, alone | 1.1–2.0 → **0.2** | 4.7–8.2 → **0.3–0.5**; spread 1.15 → 1.01 |
     | the same ball among the others (passing behind posts and balls) | 1.7–2.5 → 1.6–2.2 | 4.2–9.6 → 2.8–8.6 |
     | swinging back and forth (slow) | 2.2–3.7 → 0.6–1.0 | 7.7–18 → 5.3–15 |
     | going round | 4.8–9.4 → 3.8–5.9 | 18–26 → **23–47 (worse)** |
     | bobbing (fast) | 1.6–4.1 → 0.9–2.3 | 6.2–13 → 5.9–24 (mostly worse) |

     Common Beat's rule holds address by address (items 169–170): **carrying corrects straight motion, not curved.** A
     thing going straight is put back where it is, whole; a thing going round or bobbing, carried along the chord of its
     last two sweeps, is put where it is not, and the longer the sweep the worse. Two things new: (a) **what is carried
     leaves holes**: where a moving thing stood when read, nothing behind it was ever read, so the carried record has dark
     places no carrying can fill (the demo's right-hand view shows them); (b) **hiding breaks the carry**: a thing passing
     behind others is read in parts at different moments, so the middle of its addresses is not its middle, and the carry
     fixes little (the straight ball among the others). The serial reader's own clock buys straight motion back, and only
     what it actually read.

214. **Where the unit can come from** (Tom 05:58 "#1"; `unit.cjs`). Five readers, each with its own unit (its D1, never
     given in anyone else's terms), its own body (a pole it stands on, of its own height) and its own clock (ticking at
     its own rate). Each reports the height of one post; readings of g and of turns rounded to 1/1000. Spread of the five
     reports, as a share of their mean, median of 200 worlds:

     | how they report | spread |
     |---|---|
     | each in its own unit | 34% |
     | each in **reader 0's body**, which every reader reads (g to its foot, the turn to its top) | **0.85%** (the grain) |
     | radar to the post's foot, timed on each reader's own clock (against its true distance) | 16% |
     | radar timed in the flashes of **one lamp all can see** | **0.00%** |

215. **The unit is an agreement, not a reading.** Every thread ended on "the unit comes only from a shared length"
     (items 178, 192, 194, 202, 204, 210). This says what that is. No reading gives a length: g is in the reader's own
     unit, turns carry none. But no number need be given either: **any thing all the readers can read, agreed to be 1,
     serves**, and readers who can read one another always have one, each other's bodies. Read that way, five readers
     agree to the grain on a post none of them was told the size of. The radar is the same agreement in disguise: a
     signal's speed shared by all and a beat shared by all (the lamp) make a length, the distance the signal runs in one
     beat, and on their own clocks, not shared, they disagree by their clocks (16%). So the last rung of the ladder
     ("+ one length", "the side is 20") was never information about the world: it was **agreement with a unit someone
     else chose**, here the view from nowhere's, used for scoring. Among readers, the rung is climbed by choosing
     something in common to call 1. (The metre is this: a signal's speed and a shared second.) **Ruled with the rest of
     the division (R86, Sep 29 06:54, "proceed as written").**

216. **The forward reading, laid in turn** (Tom 06:54 "proceed with next steps"; `depth.cjs`, part A). The across
     readings are laid evenly in turn (R84); the forward reading, along each address, had only g. Laid the same way,
     evenly in turn with d = D1·tan ψ (the 1d Cauchy along the address), its corner (v = h) falls at **d = D1**, its
     median is D1, and on that lay **g = min(1, cot ψ) exactly**: g is the forward reading read from its far end,
     clipped at the corner. So g holds the half of the forward reading beyond the corner and reads 1 for everything
     within it, which is why near order within D1 needed the sway (items 175–176). One address, 1,000 depth addresses:
