     | lay of the forward reading | within the corner | median d/D1 | depth grain Δd/d at d/D1 = 0.01 / 0.1 / 0.5 / 1 / 2 / 10 / 100 / 1000 | near order, 8 things at 0.01–1000 D1, of 28 |
     |---|---|---|---|---|
     | even steps (to 100 D1) | 1% | 50 | – / 1.0 / 0.20 / 0.10 / 0.050 / 0.010 / – / – | 25.7 (13) |
     | even g | 0% | 2.0 | – / – / – / – / 0.0020 / 0.010 / 0.10 / 1.3 | 23.2 (7) |
     | **even turn** | **50%** | **1.0** | 0.16 / 0.016 / 0.0039 / **0.0031** / 0.0039 / 0.016 / 0.18 / 0.85 | **27.7** (25) |

     Laid in turn, the depth grain is finest at the corner and the same at D1/k and k·D1 (the swap of v and h): the
     fisheye along the address, centred on the reader's own unit. Even g has no addresses within D1 at all; even steps
     never reach the horizon. (R80's "g is just [0, 1] … no corner" holds of g itself: the corner is in the forward
     reading g is clipped from.)

217. **The true 3d Cauchy** (`depth.cjs`, part B). With the across readings, the forward reading makes three. Laid as
     **one even lay** of all three (the 3d Cauchy: a quarter of the 3-sphere, evenly, read as ratios to its pole, so the
     reader's own point is home and the vanishing point lies in every direction), against the across readings crossed
     with a laid forward reading at each address:

     | lay of the reader's space | within the corner (r < D1) | median r/D1 | within 0.1 D1 | addresses per volume at r/D1 = 0.01 / 0.1 / 0.3 / 1 / 3 / 10 / 30 (÷ at 1), exact |
     |---|---|---|---|---|
     | even sky × even g (as now) | 0% | 2.0 | 0% | 0 / 0 / 0 / 1 / 0.012 / 10⁻⁴ / 1.2·10⁻⁶ |
     | even sky × even turn (crossed) | 50% | 1.0 | **6.4%** | **2·10⁴** / 200 / 20 / 1 / 0.022 / 2·10⁻⁴ / 2.5·10⁻⁶ |
     | **3d Cauchy (one even lay)** | **18.2%** | **2.26** | 0.04% | **4.0 / 3.9 / 3.4** / 1 / 0.040 / 3.9·10⁻⁴ / 4.9·10⁻⁶ |

     (Laid at random, 400,000 addresses; the shares match the exact figures.) The Cauchy family's corner shares are
     **50% (1d), 29.3% (2d), 18.2% (3d)**, and its medians 1, √3, 2.26. Crossed, the reader's space has **a pole at the
     reader's own point**, where every address's forward reading starts: address density per volume grows as 1/r², and
     6.4% of all addresses sit within 0.1 D1, a thousandth of the corner's volume. It is the poles of crossed turns
     (item 187) again, one reading up. The one even lay has **no pole**: near the reader it reads space evenly, like the
     view from nowhere's grid (density flat, 4.0 → 3.4 out to 0.3 D1); far off it thins as 1/r⁴, as even g does; the
     corner is where one hands over to the other. Near order in space (8 things at 0.03–30 D1, 20,000 addresses, of
     28): crossed 27.8, one even lay 27.4, as now 25.8, an even grid to 10 D1 24.3. **Ruled (R87, Sep 29 07:03, "proceed as written"):
     the forward reading is laid in turn, its corner at D1; with the across readings it is one even lay, the 3d Cauchy.**

218. **Where the reader puts its forward corner** (`l3depth.cjs`; *The Ladder in 3d*'s 'g' pass, one reader placing each
     post along its address in its own unit). Posts stand 41–116 from the readers (median 77). Shape up to a scale,
     error on the block 100 across, grain 1/300 (300 depth addresses), 20 worlds:

     | D1 | even g, 1 reader | even turn, 1 reader | even g, 2 | even turn, 2 | even g, 8 | even turn, 8 |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | 5 | 1.35 | 2.29 | 1.08 | 1.73 | 0.51 | 0.81 |
     | 15 | **0.51** | 0.70 | 0.35 | 0.53 | 0.17 | 0.29 |
     | 40 | 1.71 | 0.32 | 1.78 | 0.24 | 0.74 | 0.12 |
     | 70 | 8.79 | **0.26** | 7.82 | 0.20 | 3.06 | **0.10** |
     | 120 | 19.95 | 0.27 | 18.24 | 0.20 | 7.42 | 0.10 |
     | 300 | 20.20 | 0.50 | 18.24 | 0.35 | 7.56 | 0.16 |

     Laid in turn, the reader holds depth best where its corner is, so it holds the shape best with **D1 where the
     world is** (70–120 here), and one reader alone reaches 0.26, what two readers passing turns reached (item 193).
     Laid in g, it holds best with D1 just inside the nearest things, and fails once D1 passes them (everything within
     reads 1). Neither gives the unit: D1 is the reader's own (item 215). Item 193's "g sharpens less well than turns"
     belonged to the even-g lay.

219. **Carrying a curve: three sweeps** (`carry3.cjs`, `carry3b.cjs`). Item 213's carry follows the chord of a thing's
     middle over the last two sweeps. Carrying along the curve through its middles over the last three: one curved
     mover alone (no posts, no other balls), round home, centre off in steps, read → chord → curve:

     | mover | sweep | three sweeps, share of its period | read → chord → curve |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | swinging | 0.25 / 0.5 / 1 s | 0.11 / 0.21 / 0.43 | 2.5 → 0.5 → **0.3** · 5.1 → 1.8 → **0.7** · 10.8 → 5.7 → **4.0** |
     | round | 0.1 / 0.25 / 0.5 / 1 s | 0.08 / 0.20 / 0.41 / 0.81 | 1.8 → 0.4 → 0.3 · 4.6 → 1.9 → **1.2** · 9.2 → 7.1 → 7.0 · 16.6 → 25.7 → **34.9** |
     | bobbing | 0.25 / 0.5 / 1 s | 0.27 / 0.55 / 1.10 | 2.0 → 1.3 → **0.9** · 3.8 → 3.4 → **2.5** · 6.0 → 6.1 → 6.4 |

     **A carry follows a curve if the sweeps come faster than the thing turns**: three sweeps within about half its
     period; near a whole period nothing carried helps, and more sweeps make it worse. Among the other things (the
     world of item 213), hiding and tearing put the middles wrong, and the curve, reaching further, carries the error
     further: going round at ¼ s, 9.6 → 3.5 (chord) → 10.1 (curve). So the carry's reach is set by what was read: how
     often, and whole or in parts.

220. **The storage ladder** (Tom 07:03 "proceed as written"; `storage.cjs`). *Mirror and Painting*'s world, four panels,
     each holding more of its own: the **mirror** (nothing); the **painting** (one standpoint's colours); a **depth photo**
     (one standpoint's colours and forward readings: how far behind the panel each colour stood); **many-view** (nine
     standpoints' colours, 0.25 apart along the reader's sway, from −1 to +1; an address takes the stored colour whose
     standpoint's line through that point is nearest its own). The reader stands where the painter stood, then sways.
     Numbers held per point of the panel: 0 / 3 / 4 / 27. Shown ball agrees with the mirror's (of addresses where either
     shows a ball), and holes (addresses where the record holds nothing, because its standpoint never read there):

     | swayed | 0 | 0.5 | 1 | 2 |
     |---|---|---|---|---|
     | painting: same ball | 97% | 65% | 41% | 12% |
     | depth photo: same ball (holes) | 97% (0%) | 100% (1.2%) | 95% (2.3%) | 93% (4.6%) |
     | many-view: same ball | 97% | 100% | 100% | **36%** (past its standpoints) |

     | | mirror | painting | depth photo | many-view |
     |---|---|---|---|---|
     | g ×1000, balls 1 / 2 / 3 | 263 / 296 / 224 | 422 / 403 / 435 | **262 / 296 / 224** | 422 / 403 / 435 |
     | nearest first by g | 2, 1, 3 ✓ | 3, 1, 2 | **2, 1, 3 ✓** | 3, 1, 2 |
     | slide per unit sway ×1000 | 58 / 67 / 51 | 99 / 99 / 102 | 59 / 67 / 51 | **59 / 70 / 51** |
     | nearest first by slide | 2, 1, 3 ✓ | 3, 1, 2 | 2, 1, 3 ✓ | **2, 1, 3 ✓** |

221. **What a stored relay passes is what it stored.** A painting passes turns from one standpoint only. Storing the
     **forward reading** (the depth photo) passes **g**, the parallel reader's depth, and near order by g, at one number
     more per point; and from its one standpoint it can be re-read from elsewhere, but only what that standpoint read:
     where the sway uncovers what was behind, the record has **holes** (1.2% at 0.5, 4.6% at 2), the carried record's
     holes of item 213 again. Storing **standpoints** (many-view) passes the **sway**, the serial reader's depth, and the
     right ball everywhere inside the standpoints' span (100% to 1), and nothing beyond it (36% at 2); its g stays the
     paint's. So the played paper's two kinds of depth (3.7) are stored separately: g by storing the forward reading,
     the sway by storing standpoints. Only the live relay passes both, from anywhere, and it is the only one that passes
     *now*: every stored record is *then* (item 212), however much of it is stored. None passes the unit.

222. **g back from a many-view record, by relations between its standpoints** (Tom 07:09 "proceed"; `gfrom.cjs`,
     `gwhole.cjs`). The record's nine standpoints are readers: each stored view shows each ball somewhere on the panel,
     and the lines from two or more standpoints through those places cross at the ball's image (item 191's bottom rung,
     applied to a record; the record carries where its standpoints stood, in its own unit). The reader places itself in
     the record's frame from its own readings of the panel's four corners (a flat frame of four, items 161–162), and
     reads each image's distance in the record's unit. Near order comes back right from every standpoint tried (swayed
     0, 1, 2, 4: past the record's span, where its colours failed); ratios of g without any unit, 1.14 / 0.87 against
     1.12 / 0.85 true. **g itself needs one agreement**: that the record's unit is the reader's (both call the panel's
     width 5.2). With it, g on a ball's image comes back only as well as the stored views show the ball **whole**:

     | ball | shown whole in stored views (judged from the stored patch's own shape) | g ×1000, swayed 0 / 1 / 2 / 4 | true |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | 2 | 8 of 9 | **284 / 289 / 291 / 290** | 283 / 287 / 289 / 289 |
     | 1 | none (cut by the wall's edge) | 267 / 274 / 277 / 280 (all views, biased) | 254 / 257 / 260 / 263 |
     | 3 | none (cut by ball 1) | 232 / 237 / 241 / 245 (biased) | 216 / 220 / 223 / 228 |

     A ball's middle in a view where it is cut is not its middle (6–25 thousandths of a turn off, against 3 where whole),
     the hiding of item 213 again. So a many-view record holds g after all, not as a stored number but **as relations
     between its standpoints**, and from anywhere, not only within its span; it gives g to the grain for what its
     standpoints read whole, and to the unit only by agreement. Whether the uncut part of a cut outline places it as
     well is untested.

223. **A cut outline places a thing as well as a whole one** (Tom 07:50 "Proceed"; `goutline.cjs`). A stored ball patch
     borders three kinds of thing: background (sky, floor; there the ball is surely in front), the wall, or another
     ball. The reader keeps only the stretches against background, the ball's own outline for certain, fits an ellipse
     to them, and takes its centre where it took the patch's middle before. Share of each patch's border that is outline
     against background: ball 1 34–47%, ball 2 33–72%, ball 3 68–77%. Off per stored view, in thousandths of a turn,
     middle → outline: ball 1 19–25 → **1.3–2.3**; ball 3 6–13 → **0.9–1.3**; ball 2 (mostly whole) 2–3 → 2–3. g ×1000,
     crossing all nine views:

     | swayed | 0 | 1 | 2 | 4 |
     |---|---|---|---|---|
     | by middles | 267 / 305 / 232 | 274 / 311 / 237 | 277 / 313 / 241 | 280 / 312 / 245 |
     | **by outlines** | **254 / 284 / 216** | **260 / 289 / 221** | **262 / 291 / 224** | **264 / 290 / 228** |
     | true | 254 / 283 / 216 | 257 / 287 / 220 | 260 / 289 / 223 | 263 / 289 / 228 |

     Images placed within 0.06–0.10 (against 0.9–1.3 by middles). So hiding does not take a thing's place away, only
     its middle: **the place is in the outline, and a third of the outline, the part the thing itself bounds, is
     enough.** The remaining 1–3 thousandths are the grain and a sphere's outline centre standing slightly off its
     centre's line. What the reader must tell apart is which border is the thing's own; against background it always
     is. (Where two balls meet, the border is the front one's; this test did not use those stretches.)

224. **The carry by outlines** (Tom 08:58 "proceed"; `carryout.cjs`). Item 213's carry failed for the straight ball among
     the others: hiding split its addresses, so their middle was not its middle. Here each thing is placed, in each
     sweep, by the centre of a circle fitted to its outline where it meets background (floor, sky), and that centre gives
     how it moved and where it ends up once each outline point is carried on from its own moment. Scored against the
     thing's true centre on the reader's chart. Centre off, in address steps, as read → carried, by middles | by outlines:

     | order, sweep | straight, among the others | straight, alone | round (curved) |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | spiral, ¼ s | 3.2 → 3.7 \| 2.3 → **1.5** | 2.0 → 0.1 | 8.9 → 3.4 \| 9.1 → **1.4** |
     | spiral, 1 s | 7.5 → 5.0 \| 7.6 → **0.7** | 7.9 → 0.4 | 27.4 → 37.7 \| 25.3 → 33.4 |
     | round home, 1 s | 5.1 → 4.2 \| 5.0 → **0.8** | 5.3 → 0.5 | 19.8 → 22.0 \| 15.2 → 12.4 |
     | rows, 1 s | 4.8 → 3.0 \| 4.7 → **0.5** | 4.6 → 0.3 | 10.9 → 13.2 \| 11.8 → 12.6 |

     **Carried by its outline, a thing passing behind others is carried nearly as well as one alone** (0.5–0.8 against
     0.3–0.5 at 1 s; at ¼ s about 1.5, the circle's own grain divided over a short sweep). Swinging and round at ¼ s
     also carry better (0.4–0.9 and 1.1–1.4, against 1.5–1.9 and 2.3–3.4 by middles). At 1 s curves still fail: the
     outline fixes where the thing is, not the chord (item 219). An ellipse (five numbers) on outlines this short (a ball
     about 7 steps across) was unstable (once 27 off); a circle (three) holds. So the rule of item 223 holds for the
     serial reader too: **hiding takes the middle, not the place; the place is in the outline.**

225. **Outline and curve together** (Tom 09:00 "proceed"; `carryout3.cjs`, `carryout3c.cjs`). Outline centres over the
     last three sweeps, carried along the chord of the last two, the curve through all three, or whichever the reader
     chooses from its own centres (the curve if the middle centre stands off the chord of the outer two by more than 1.5
     steps). Among the others, centre off in address steps, outlines: chord / curve / chosen:

     | | straight | swinging | round | bobbing |
     |---|---|---|---|---|
     | rows, ¼ s | 1.4 / 2.1 / 1.9 | 0.4 / 0.5 / 0.4 | 1.0 / 1.2 / 0.9 | 0.9 / **0.4** / 0.4 |
     | rows, ½ s | **1.1** / 2.0 / 1.7 | 1.8 / **1.1** / 1.0 | 3.6 / 4.3 / 4.3 | 3.2 / 2.5 / 2.6 |
     | rows, 1 s | **0.5** / 0.9 / 0.5 | 8.1 / **3.8** / 3.9 | **8.2** / 18.4 / 18.4 | **9.9** / 14.2 / 14.2 |
     | spiral, ½ s | **1.2** / 2.2 / 1.5 | 3.9 / **1.6** / 1.6 | **15.3** / 20.3 / 20.3 | 8.2 / 8.4 / 8.4 |
     | round home, ¼ s | **1.7** / 3.8 / 3.1 | 0.6 / 0.8 / 0.7 | **1.1** / 2.0 / 2.0 | 0.8 / **0.3** / 0.3 |

     (By middles the same carries were 3.0–4.6 for the straight ball.) The outline serves every carry; the curve serves
     only where the three sweeps span well under half the thing's period (swinging to ½ s, bobbing at ¼ s), and on a
     straight path it only adds the centres' grain (1.2–3.8 against 0.5–1.7). **From its own three centres the reader
     can tell straight from bent** (its choice keeps straight motion on the chord at 1 s), **but not a path bent within
     reach from a thing turning faster than it sweeps**: going round at ½–1 s is always bent, the curve is chosen, and it
     fails (at 1 s, 18–57 off; at ½ s, 4.3–20.3 against the chord's 3.5–15.3; corrected Sep 29 on checking the table). There nothing carried serves; only reading more often does (item 219).

226. **Which of two things is in front, from colours alone** (Tom 09:01 "proceed"; `border.cjs`). A reader holding g
     never asks: along an address the higher g is in front. A record of colours alone must. 150 random worlds of 3–6
     balls, each stored from nine standpoints 0.25 apart; 2,864 borders where two stored balls meet. Two cues the record
     holds: **shape**, the border lies on the front ball's outline, so it fits the circle fitted to that ball's own outline
     against background (one stored view); **place**, cross the lines from the standpoints through each ball's outline
     centre (items 222–224) and see which comes out nearer (two views or more). Right, of borders decided:

     | | shape | place |
     |---|---|---|
     | balls apart (2,286 borders) | **97.4%** | **97.3%** |
     | balls passing through each other (578) | 77.7% | 76.2% |
     | shape, by how much nearer the front ball is: under 5% / 5–15% / 15–40% / more | 63% / 94% / 99% / 97% | |

     (Undecided: shape 5%, where a ball has too little outline against background; place 2.6%.) Where balls pass through
     each other the border is where they meet and neither is in front, so the question has no answer there. **So a record
     of colours alone holds the order in depth at its borders, in two ways: in one view by the outline's shape, across
     views by the places.** The shape cue fails only where the two are almost equally far (under 5%: 63%), where the
     border is nearly both outlines at once.

227. **Each reader's weakness is another's strength** (Tom 09:17: "my intuition, is that the weakness of each perfectly
     balances out the strengths of each to form a corherent system of obersvation"; 09:19: "create a ledger").
     *The Observation Ledger* lists every weakness measured in items 139–226 (the parallel reader's seven, the serial
     reader's six, the company's two, the view from nowhere's four), what covers each, and what is left. The parallel
     and serial readers cover each other point for point (magnitude without time; time without magnitude); every holding
     of the view from nowhere is rebuilt by readers as something shared (relations, a far reading, a beat, an agreement).
     Four residuals remain: the unit (an agreement), change faster than any reading comes round, what no standpoint read,
     and the grain. *Stated as a conjecture, not ruled*: serial and parallel readers, in company and with one agreement,
     recover everything of the world some standpoint reads at some moment, to the grain. Kill conditions and three
     probing tests (one reader that is both; a chain of agreements; something deforming) are in the ledger.

228. **Ruled: the unit is not geometric** (R89, Tom 09:25: "the unit is persuasive. that only comes from aggreement. it
     is not geometric."). Items 214–215 showed no reading gives a unit; the ruling takes it off the ladder. The geometry
     from somewhere ends at the shape up to a scale; the unit is an agreement laid over the geometry, not a rung of it.
     In the ledger the unit leaves the residuals: what remains are change faster than any reading, what no standpoint
     read, and the grain.
     **Refined 09:27:** "a solo reader would have no need for a unit, because that is how you communicate with other
     readers. if you only need to communicate with yourself, a unit can just be you." The record agrees: every solo
     reader measured was complete in its own unit (g in its own D1, 193, 218; the walker's step, 204; its own body,
     214's first row, each reader self-consistent), and the unit was needed only where readers compared reports (214).

229. **Ruled: the view from nowhere is the backside** (R90, Tom 09:30: "the god's eye view is needed if you need to
     consider the backside"; 09:32: "yes, that is clean definition of the view from nowhere, it it is the hidden view,
     the blocked view, the backside."). A reader holds two facings. The other two it reaches by turning, one half after
     the other, paid in warning (173–174), or through a company, from other standpoints, joined by relations and, for
     what moves, a shared beat (159–170, 213), and never where no standpoint reaches (the 0.7% of the hollow, 199). Only
     the view from nowhere holds front and back at the same moment. In the ledger it stops being only the apparatus that
     scores: it is what the readers' residuals point to.

230. **One reader that is both** (Tom 09:33 "proceed"; `both.cjs`; the ledger's first test). A serial reader that keeps
     g along each address (its own D1 = 2: alone, it is its own unit, R89), against readers at the same reading budget:
     a parallel snapshot taken exactly now (the game loop's now, which no reader has), a parallel reader's latest
     snapshot (taken T/2 before, on average), a parallel reader carrying its last two snapshots, and a serial reader with
     turns only. Each places four moving balls and six still posts at the moment of scoring; error after the best turn,
     shift and scale (world units; balls 1.6–2 across):

     | order, sweep | parallel, now (ideal) | parallel, latest | parallel, carried | serial, turns only | **serial + g** |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|
     | rows, ¼ s: balls / still posts | 0.05 / 0.03 | 0.26 / 0.02 | 0.13 / 0.02 | nothing in depth | **0.15 / 0.03** |
     | round home, ¼ s | 0.05 / 0.03 | 0.26 / 0.02 | 0.13 / 0.02 | nothing in depth | **0.17 / 0.03** |
     | rows, ½ s | 0.06 / 0.03 | 0.49 / 0.02 | 0.29 / 0.02 | nothing in depth | **0.28 / 0.03** |
     | spiral, ½ s | 0.05 / 0.03 | 0.41 / 0.03 | 0.27 / 0.03 | nothing in depth | 0.55 / 0.02 |
     | rows, 1 s | 0.06 / 0.01 | 0.63 / 0.02 | 0.53 / 0.02 | nothing in depth | 0.47 / 0.03 |
     | spiral, 1 s | 0.03 / 0.01 | 0.62 / 0.02 | 0.52 / 0.02 | nothing in depth | 0.75 / 0.04 |

     (Still posts fitted on their own.) **The reader that is both covers both weaknesses at once.** It places everything
     in its own unit, where the serial reader alone places nothing in depth (S1); it receives still things as the ideal
     does, to the grain (0.02–0.04); and it carries moving things to *now* better than the parallel reader's latest
     snapshot at short sweeps (0.15–0.17 against 0.26 at ¼ s). Its remaining error is the two already in the ledger:
     change faster than the reading (at 1 s every carry fails alike) and the serial order's tearing (the spiral, which
     crosses the balls twice, 0.55 against 0.28 in rows). The parallel reader carrying two snapshots is the other form of
     "both" (parallel reading, serial time), and does as well or a little better on moving things, each snapshot having
     one moment. **No new residual.** Found on the way: placing a still post by the middle of its addresses let the
     moving balls, hiding parts of it at different moments, move it by up to 10 steps and wreck the carry; placing it
     halfway between its left and right edges against background put it right to the grain. The outline rule (223) holds
     for things that are not round: a thing's place is in its edges against background.

231. **Something deforming** (Tom 09:46 "proceed"; `deform.cjs`; the ledger's third test). Two things move straight
     across, one **breathing** (its size swells and shrinks by 35%) and one **stretching** (it lengthens and shortens by
     40% along one axis), with a deforming period of 4 s or 1.5 s. The target is what a parallel snapshot taken exactly
     now reads (the conjecture's "what some standpoint reads at some moment"): the breathing thing's outline size, the
     stretching thing's outline proportions (across over up). Each reader carries place and size along the chord of its
     last two readings. With nothing hiding them, centre off (address steps) · size off (% of the snapshot now), order rows:

     | thing, period, sweep | parallel, latest | parallel, carried | serial, as read | serial, carried |
     |---|---|---|---|---|
     | breathing, 4 s, 0.1 s | 0.7 · 1.8% | 0.1 · **0.5%** | 0.7 · 1.7% | 0.1 · **0.6%** |
     | breathing, 4 s, ¼ s | 1.8 · 4.4% | 0.1 · **1.3%** | 1.8 · 4.4% | 0.2 · **1.3%** |
     | breathing, 1.5 s, 0.1 s | 0.7 · 5.0% | 0.1 · **1.3%** | 0.7 · 4.8% | 0.2 · **1.3%** |
     | breathing, 1.5 s, ½ s | 3.6 · 23% | 0.3 · 33% | 3.7 · 24% | 1.0 · 32% |
     | stretching, 4 s, ¼ s | 1.4 · 6.0% | 0.2 · **3.3%** | 1.6 · 6.6% | 0.2 · **4.3%** |
     | stretching, 1.5 s, 0.1 s | 0.6 · 6.2% | 0.1 · **2.9%** | 0.7 · 7.3% | 0.1 · **3.6%** |
     | stretching, 1.5 s, 1 s | 5.5 · 55% | 0.5 · 81% | 6.3 · 58% | 0.5 · 80% |

     (A snapshot's own reading jitters 1.0% for size and 1.6% for proportions between neighbouring moments: the grain.)
     **Deformation carries exactly as motion does**: along the chord when the reading comes round fast against the
     change, not when the change turns within a few readings; the place is carried whatever the shape does (0.1–0.5
     steps). The serial reader and the parallel reader do alike. On the way two scenes broke it, and neither was
     deformation: the stretching thing passing behind a post, and its first path running through a post (item 226's
     things passing through each other). **Killed on the way:** I blamed the stretching failures on "long over short"
     folding at 1 (the corner, where the long axis switches); carrying the unfolded proportion changed nothing, and the
     cause was the post. **No new residual**: change of shape joins change of place in "change faster than the reading
     comes round"; hiding and the grain are the other two.

232. **The standard of the claim** (Tom 09:53: "we won't be able to prove it definietvelyu, because this is synethetic
     and digital, but hopefully we can at least show that is logical and likely to be true, at least within our own
     assumptions."). The conjecture is held as **logical** (the pairings follow from the definitions: magnitude at a
     moment, ratio over time, every facing at once) and **likely within our assumptions** (every entry measured; the
     probing tests found no unpredicted residual). The assumptions are listed in *The Observation Ledger* §7; the two most
     likely to matter hand the reader something that is itself a reading outside the simulation: **identity** (the
     payload says which thing an address read) and **background** (what an outline is taken against).

233. **Ruled: geometry is the sweep of addresses; the magnitudes make the backside** (R91; Tom 10:42, to ChatGPT: "the
     big, potentially paradigm changing idea here is that geometry is not shapes, it is the sweep of addresses that point
     to a magnitude. says nothing about what the magnitudes are."; 10:43: "yes, the magnitudes are what create the
     backsides and the hidden geometry. so they are not not irrelevent."). The record's split: payload-free are the lay
     results (the Cauchy family's corner shares, crossed lays' poles, the forward reading's corner at D1, the grain);
     made by the payload are the first border, occlusion, contact, the backside and every hole (199, 213, 220, 223); the
     shape is recovered from payloads through the addresses as far as the ladder allows. The boundary: the ladder and the
     cross ratio need the payload to be what a straight sweep meets (the synthesis paper's assumption 4); for a payload
     that is a value at a point, the addressing results hold and the rest is untested.

234. **Ruled: the span is π/2 at every g** (R92; Tom 13:16 asked for the derivation of π/2 as "the sum of all possible
     relations between horizontal and vertical": ∫₀^∞ ds/(1 + s²) = π/2, the relations counted evenly in turn, the corner
     splitting it into π/4 and π/4. Then 13:24: "it is always PI/2 at every g."; 13:25: "at g = 1, it simplifies away.").
     g sets the count of hangers expressing the span (gN, spaced π/(2gN)), not the span. **Killed on the way:** folding g
     into the measure, E = ∫ min(1, 1/s) ds/(1 + s²) = π/4 + (ln 2)/2, treated g as cutting into the breadth; it does not.
     At g = 1 the factor simplifies away and the classical equation is what remains: classical accounts are the g = 1 case,
     which along the forward sweep holds from the reader out to the corner.

235. **ψ₁ = 2/π is the height at home, times g** (Tom 13:40: "amazing. sitting in plain sight. … here it is in the
     cauchy, but it is simplified away if g = 1."; the idea pulled from the archived paper *The Constant ψ₁*, Dec 2025,
     and boma.js's `PSI = 2/π`; 13:42: "the 2/PI paper was archived, its not being maintainted, but we can pull ideas from
     it."). With g, the Cauchy's height at home is f_g(0) = g · ψ₁, and the height times the span is g · ψ₁ · π/2 = g: the
     share expressed. The archived paper's "open × closed = ψ₁ × π/2 = 1" is the g = 1 case; boma's
     `virtualSize = N · ITEM_EXTENT · PSI` is the count density at home for N fully expressed hangers, likewise g = 1.
     At g = 1 the constant reads as the Cauchy's normalisation and is taken for bookkeeping; with g it is a height and
     the 1 is one value of g. (The archived paper's "the unit is a theorem" is a dimensionless total, forced to 1 only at
     g = 1; R89's unit is a length passed between readers. Different units.)

236. **Ruled: the sweep of g is the radius of the addressing scheme** (R93; Tom 13:46: "right, but as you reduce g from 1 to 0, you create the unit sphere or unit circle. At 1, all N is included to create the addressing scheme. g is simply how much of N is being used. but its addresses, not data. And its not a specific g, its the sweep of g. you don't pick a g of 0.5 or example, it is all g between 0 and 1."; 13:48: "Yes. than what ever system you are observing is overlayed onto this addressing scheme. g is never chosen, it is the sweep over the full expression of N to empty expression of N."). Two sweeps
     together: the turn (angle, span π/2) and g (radius, 1 down to 0). At radius g the span carries gN addresses, which
     is the arc there, g · π/2 (N addresses to π/2 of arc). The sweep of g fills the quarter unit disc (area π/4; the
     half disc across two facings, the full disc across four); with two across readings, the unit ball, rim the unit
     sphere. The unit circle is not put in: it is what the sweep closes at full expression. The observed system is
     overlaid on this scheme. **Correction:** the curves f_g for single g (items 234–235, the plot of 13:29) are slices
     through the one disc, not separate cases. *Observation, not ruled:* along each forward sweep g = D1/d runs from 1 at
     the corner to 0 at the vanishing point; placing each point at its direction with radius g is inversion in the
     corner's sphere (x ↦ x/|x|², in units of D1): space beyond D1 fills the unit ball, the corner-sphere is its rim, the
     vanishing point its centre, and everything nearer than D1 lies on the rim at g = 1.

237. **The sweep of g, foregrounded** (Tom 13:51: "this should be foregrouned as the major contribution of this paper. the
     way cauchy hides this fact by simplying g away at the limit, but g is very much present as the sweep over a system,
     but not the system but the address to the system."). *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* restructured: subtitle "The sweep
     of g"; the abstract opens on it; §2 leads with the Cauchy with g (§2.1: the classical Cauchy is the g = 1 slice, where
     g simplifies away) and g as a sweep over addresses (§2.2: turn × g fills the unit disc, rim the unit circle; the
     system is overlaid); the address/payload split follows as §2.3; §12 names it first among what is new.

238. **boma.js is written at g = 1** (Tom 13:53: "we can illustrate this with boma.js. notice, it assumes a g=1, but it is
     never considered, but could be, arctan apparently assumes a g=1."; `boma-g.js`). boma's extent is
     N · ITEM_EXTENT · PSI and its step π/N: both assume all N addresses in use. arctan itself does not: it returns the
     turn, whose span is the same at every g (R92); g enters where a turn becomes an address (step π/(gN)) and in the
     extent (g · N · ITEM_EXTENT · PSI). Written in, with g = 1 as default: identical to boma on 20,000 random calls
     (`project`, `invert`: difference 0; `realDimension`: 0 mismatches). N = 1,000, aperture 0.5: g = 1 / 0.75 / 0.5 /
     0.25 gives 297 / 223 / 149 / 75 addresses in the view, steps 3.14 / 4.19 / 6.28 / 12.57 mrad, extents 25,465 /
     19,099 / 12,732 / 6,366 (item 40), and the same span of the view, 0.9273 rad, at every g.

239. **The sweep of g, built; and g is not the zoom** (Tom 14:00: "notice that this works with linear data, whether that
     linear array closes or has any facings is irrelevent. we calcualte the addresses using cauchy and lay an array down
     on top of it. but that is for just one g. there is no sweep in boma.js. we could create the sweep version of
     boma.js that created the addresses for every g between 0 and 1."; 14:06: "its what ever the math says. yes, in the past we have characterized g as just the zoom or the level of detail. I think that is partially correct, but its actually as we discoved above, just the cauchy formula including the g."; `boma-sweep.js`). The sweep version
     gives the addresses at every g in (0, 1]: gN in use per ring, arc spacing span/N on every ring; a view over the
     whole sweep has ∫ count dg = (window) · N/(2 · span), matched to 0.03 in three windows. Which address is which
     across g: two candidates, measured on four addresses (N = 1,000, span π/2), cross ratio at g = 1 / 0.75 / 0.5 /
     0.25: **arc-fixed** (turn = arc/g) 1.33509 / 1.33647 / 1.34046 / 1.36329; **turn-fixed** (thinned in place)
     1.33509 at every g; the Möbius zoom s → s/g also 1.33509. **Ruled (R94): the formula decides.** f_g keeps s and
     scales the density, so an address keeps its turn and g thins: turn-fixed (the thinned set: gN in use, 50.00% within
     the corner at g = 1, 0.5, 0.25, N = 100,000). g is the level of detail; the zoom is the scale γ, a separate dial.

240. **The discovery is g's connection to the Cauchy** (Tom 14:07: "discovering its connection cauchy is the discovery
     here."). g had been in the corpus as hangers expressed (R80) and as level of detail or zoom; what is new is that it
     is the Cauchy's own factor, f_g(s) = g · (2/π)/(1 + s²), separate from the scale γ, and that the classical Cauchy is
     its g = 1 case, where g simplifies away. The paper's subtitle, abstract and §2 heading now lead with it: *g in the
     Cauchy*.

241. **Ruled: the scheme addresses any linear system, of any N** (R95; Tom 14:13: "right, this is the addressing scheme to any linear system of data, irregardless of its peridocity, or facings. the linear data could close into a circle, but that is not necessary. the system itself, can be of any size N. the addressing scheme perefectly accomodates any system N because it is from 0 to PI/2 is fixed and the number of addresses that creates of N can be infiniately densy packed."). The span is fixed; N sets the
     density span/N and may grow without limit; closing is allowed, not required. The Sep 3 qualification carried with it:
     indifferent to completion except at the horizon, which for closed data is a seam.
242. **Ruled: with N unknown the addresses are laid differently** (R96; Tom 14:18: "If N is not know, things get interesting. seems like changes the situation, the addresses get arranged differently."). Without N the span cannot be divided, so R95's even lay (ψᵢ = (i + ½)·span/N) is unavailable. Addresses that stay put and keep the list's order must rise and stay below π/2, so they bunch toward a limit; evenness, stable addresses and order cannot all be kept (the evenly refining lay 45°, 22.5°, 67.5°, 11.2°, … keeps evenness and stability but not order). The lay that keeps order and stability is a **fixed step in reading from home**: item k at s = k·step, turn arctan(k·step), bunching toward the horizon, which holds what has not arrived (growth-invariance, Sep 3). **g changes role.** With N known, g multiplies the Cauchy (f_g = g·(2/π)/(1+s²)): thinning across the whole span, turns fixed (R94). With N unknown, g is the Cauchy's *integral* up to the reach: g(n) = (2/π)·arctan(n·step), a sector growing out from home. g hits ½ at the corner where n·step = 1, so the step (the unit, or the reader itself, R89) decides where the corner falls. Checked: n·step = 1, 2, 3, 10, 100, 1000 → g = 0.5, 0.705, 0.795, 0.937, 0.9936, 0.99936. The share still at the horizon, 1 − g = (2/π)·arctan(1/(n·step)) ≈ (2/π)/(n·step), is the fat tail again, with 2/π as its coefficient. g = 1 is the horizon, never reached (Sep 22: a horizon means N unknown). Tom 14:20: "update the paper with this" → R96; paper §2.2, §2.4 (SCHEME_OPEN, Prop. 2.2), abstract.
243. **Gemini review of the synthesis paper (29 Sep, 14:27), audited.** (1) *Does N-unknown g blur address and payload?* No: g(n) counts arrivals, not what they carry or their rate; an arrival claims an address as an in-use address does with N known. What changes is that the sweep is unfinished (0 to g(n), the rest at the horizon). Sentence added to §2.2. (2) *Is D1 geometric or convention?* Neither magnitude nor agreement: the reader's own unit (R89's "a unit can just be you"). It cancels in g = D1/d as a ratio, places the corner and so decides contact, and touches no turn. Paragraph added to §7.4, linking the open lay's step (corner at n·step = 1). (3) *Does the game loop smuggle in a global ontology?* Already disclosed: §3 (readers never use the loop; scoring does) and §11 assumption 1. No change. (4) *Deformation at the sweep's rate.* The rate failure is already the ωT ≲ 1 bound (breathing 1.5 s at ½ s: 32–33%). The review's reference to SCHEME_OPEN in outline fitting is a confusion; the two are unrelated. A real gap underneath: both deformations tested were symmetric about the middle, so "whatever the shape does" overclaimed. Qualified in §10; lopsided deformation (the middle moves without motion) added to §13 as untested.
244. **Lopsided deformation, run** (Tom 14:30: "proceed"; `game/lopsided.cjs`, output `game/lopsided.txt`, NOPOSTS=1, N = 16,000, 8 moments). Across semi-axis 0.8·(1 + 0.4 sin 2πt/P), left end held; still, held end moving at 1.3/s, and rigid control; P = 4 s and 1.5 s; sweeps ¼ s and 0.1 s; parallel (two snapshots) and serial (two row sweeps). Reader reads the outline's two ends across (mean of the extreme three outline points) and their middle. **Carry to now**: every end and the middle 0.0–0.5 steps at P = 4 s; at P = 1.5 s with a ¼ s sweep up to 2.1 steps (ωT ≈ 1.05, the known bound), 0.2–0.4 at 0.1 s. **Motion** (mean |error| in steps/s against the anchor's true motion, 0 still, 11.8 moving): from the middle 3.0–3.6 (4 s), 5.8–8.5 (1.5 s); from the held end 0.0–1.7 (4 s), 0.1–1.6 (1.5 s); rigid control 0.4–1.9. The middle's error matches half the growth rate (predicted mean 3.1 steps/s at 4 s). **Reading**: the two ends separate what the outline says; which point is the thing's place is not in it, and needs identity (assumption 2). Written into paper §10 (table) and §13 (the untested bullet replaced).
245. **Ruled: the unit circle and the unit sphere are addressing schemes** (R97; Tom 14:34: "seems like this captures the unit sphere and circle perfectly. they are just an addressing schemes that you can lay any system of any size onto them."). Qualifications written with it into paper §2.2: the sphere's lay is only near-even for general N (the no-go of 2D §7.1; Fibonacci lattice); with N unknown the whole is never laid (R96); adjacency is the system's (R95).
246. **Ruled: the circle and sphere are needed when the system has periodicity and facings** (R98; Tom 14:36: "they are needed when your system has periodicty and facings."). Linear → the span 0 to π/2 suffices (R95); periodic or faceted → the whole circle, which closes where the data does (no seam at the horizon, the Sep 3 seam being what the quarter span leaves); facings all round → the sphere. Caution recorded: doubly periodic data is a torus, not a sphere. Paper §2.2.
247. **Ruled: the turn is another address for an array, by density, for sweeping** (R99; Tom 14:40, quoted there). Index = breadth (extent N·unit, a view's size grows with N); turn = density (span fixed, Cauchy lay, g). Exact conversion (ADDRESS/READING); a view by turn is 2 arctan a at every N and g (Prop. 2.1). Paper §2.2 "Index and turn".
248. **Ruled: the sweep is of breadth and density at once** (R100; Tom 14:41). Density along the sweep (the Cauchy lay) and across it (g); the disc = turn (breadth) × radius g (density). An index sweeps breadth at one density. Paper §2.2 "Index and turn" revised.
249. **Ruled: the Cauchy sets up the sweep of a system's breadth and density** (R101; Tom 14:44: "thats key, …"). Foregrounded in the paper's abstract (after R92–R94) and §12's statement of what is ours.
250. **Ruled: g in the hand metaphor** (R102; Tom 14:49): fingers fully extended g = 1, closed fist g = 0; a curled finger is out of use, not re-aimed; the span holds at every opening. Paper §2.5 hand paragraph.
251. **Ruled: two degrees of freedom in the hand, N (fingers) and g (extension)** (R103; Tom 14:51). The count carries only gN; N and g separate by which addresses are in use: N = 1,000 at g = ½ (turn-fixed thinning keeps the odd addresses, ψ = (2j + 1.5)·span/1000) against N = 500 at g = 1 (ψ = (2j + 1)·span/1000), the same count a quarter of the coarser step apart. Paper §2.5.
252. **Demo: the Cauchy sweep** (`demos/cauchy-sweep.html`, published artifact NB5jYFkJkgkTgXJuUtUSoa; Tom 14:53). One point in the quarter disc sets both sweeps: angle = where along the breadth (a view of fixed turn 0.2), radius = g. Panels: the disc (40 rings of in-use addresses, turn-fixed thinning), the array by index (in use / in view), the Cauchy g·(2/π)/(1+s²) with the in-use readings as ticks. N 4–400. Auto-sweep of g (9 s) with the view drifting (23 s); touch via pointer events; arrow keys.
253. **Demo: the semicircle sweep** (`demos/semicircle-sweep.html`, artifact FrwJJ4JwQZ42UZJhD7EvLk; Tom 14:59: "create the same thing for the semi-circle. it would be for data that has two facings, left or right."). Span π, ψᵢ = −π/2 + (i + ½)·π/N; home straight ahead, horizons at the base; index 0 at the left horizon, N − 1 at the right, home in the middle. Density g/(π(1 + s²)), area g, half to each facing; v/h labelled as a magnitude on both sides (sign from the facing, RGA R37).
254. **Demo: the circle sweep** (`demos/circle-sweep.html`, artifact 6EZ8omsX2oWYYYBis1e8Md; Tom 15:03: "lets make the same demo, but for the circle. it is for data that have 4 facings."). Span 2π, ψᵢ = −π/2 + (i + ½)·2π/N; two homes (front up, back down), two horizons A and B between; the view wraps (no ends, R98). Density read as two semicircles, each from its own home, g/(2π(1 + s²)), area g/2 each: horizon A ends the front row and starts the back, B the reverse. The ratio v/h alone does not tell front from back (each reading occurs once in each half); the facing does.
255. **Demo: the sphere sweep** (`demos/sphere-sweep.html`, artifact Wjub6fmUt2T2h5zAX395kf; Tom 15:07: "do the same for the sphere, its for data that has 8 facings."). Fibonacci lay of N (near-even, R97); eight facings = the octants cut by the horizon (z = 0) and two meridians; homes at ±z. A point in the ball needs three numbers, so one drag cannot set both sweeps: drag turns the ball under a fixed view cap (0.38 rad) at the centre (breadth), and pinch, wheel or slider set g (density). Back addresses drawn faint (the backside). Density per half by two readings (x/|z|, y/|z|): scatter with the corner circle r = 1, and radial (g/2)·r(1 + r²)^(−3/2), share within the corner 1 − 1/√2, median √3. Index order runs front home → horizon → back home; a view lights scattered cells (adjacency is the system's, R97).
256. **What the four sweep demos showed** (15:11–15:14; Tom said yes to recording). (1) A control needs one more number than a facing: the flat demos set breadth and density with one point (angle, radius); the sphere's direction needs two angles, so with g three numbers and two gestures (drag, pinch). (2) Doubling facings is gluing: semicircle = two quarters sharing a home; circle = two semicircles sharing both horizons (no ends, no seam; its view runs round, the others reverse); sphere = two four-facing halves sharing the horizon circle. Homes 1, 1, 2, 2. (3) The area stays g and the height at home is shared out: g·2/π (one facing), g/π (two), g/2π per half (circle); in two readings the profile is r(1 + r²)^(−3/2), half the area per side. (4) → R104. (5) Adjacency keeps only in one dimension: flat demos light one run of cells (wrapping on the circle); the sphere lights scattered cells (R97). (6) The fisheye is in turning turn into reading: a view of fixed turn holds the same count everywhere but a narrow band of v/h near home and a wide one near the horizon. (7) Turn-fixed thinning is visible as spokes at low g (R94).
257. **Ruled: with four or eight facings the backside is in the shape** (R104; Tom 15:14). Paper §2.2 new paragraph "Where the backside is in the shape", §2.5 magnitudes paragraph revised (the backside's two sources), §3 view from nowhere.
258. **Ruled: the quarter turn and the semicircle are not shapes** (R105; Tom 15:15). Open, with ends at the horizons; the circle and sphere close, and closure makes the shape, which is why only they have a backside in the shape (R104). Paper §2.2 paragraph revised.
259. **Ruled: relation, density and magnitude** (R106–R107; Tom 17:16–17:37, thinking aloud to the result). Route: asked for the third dimension beside "breadth and density"; candidates offered (zoom γ, a second angle, the standpoint; then time, magnitude). Tom: breadth is x and y as a modelling choice (17:22); breadth = N, density = how much of N is visible, no magnitude yet (17:28); v/h is on [0, ∞) (17:33); that is the relation, the magnitude was divided out (17:34); breadth was mislabelled, division removes breadth or scale (17:37); the three are relation, density, magnitude, the headline (17:37). Paper: subtitle and abstract lead with it; new §2.3 (table, breadth divided out, addressing vs world, not x, y, z with two cautions); "breadth" → "relation" in the abstract, §2.2, §12 and Appendix B; R100–R101 annotated; §13 gains "Nearness and density" (the 3d paper's g = D1/d is a magnitude by §2.3). Demos relabelled.
260. **Ruled: ranges of the three dimensions** (R108–R110; Tom 17:44–17:57, "Update the paper"). Density is the reader's, magnitude the world's, relation between. All three native [0, ∞): relation v/h; density as a count (N unbounded, R95); magnitude as an amount. Each bounded by the Cauchy's turn: arctan(v/h) (no unit), g = share of N or (2/π)·arctan(n·step) (N or step), arctan(d/D1) (a unit, R87/R89). Nearness g = D1/d = cot ψ read as magnitude laid in turn (observation, not ruled). Paper §2.3 table rewritten, two new paragraphs; abstract; §13 item revised.
261. **Ruled: not dimensional — divide, sweep, point** (R111; Tom 19:06, with a ChatGPT gloss pasted after it). "Dimensions" in R106–R107 withdrawn: x, y, z are orthogonal dimensions of the world; observation from somewhere divides, sweeps and points. The nowhere/somewhere contrast ("the view from nowhere is dimensional; the view from somewhere is relational") is ChatGPT's phrasing, adopted into the paper as the reading of R111, not a separate ruling. Paper: subtitle, abstract, §2.3 rewritten as "Divide, sweep, point" (table's rows renamed as steps), §12, §13 wording.
262. **Ruled: three dimensions, nested rather than orthogonal** (R112; Tom 19:15–19:18). "Kind of dimensional": the complete set of relations → the complete set of densities → the complete set of possible magnitudes. Independent freedoms, ordered (none rotates into another; x, y, z do). Paper: abstract headline, §2.3 "Three dimensions, nested" (replaces "Not dimensions"), §12; the nowhere/somewhere contrast is now of arrangement (orthogonal vs nested), not of count.
263. **Ruled: the parallel reading defines the range of addresses; no snapshot** (R113; Tom 19:24–19:39). Two layers of indirection: the serial reading points into the range, the range into the system; magnitudes exist separately and are never copied. The simulations' parallel reader (a snapshot) is a record; the latency weakness P2 is the record's. Paper: abstract, §2.3 sentence, §3.2 (parallel and serial bullets, a note on the simulations), §10 table annotated; ledger P2 annotated. Measured results unchanged. (Withdrawn in discussion: Claude's suggestion that "nowhere" is the system; it stays R90, the backside.)
264. **Ruled: formal definition of the unit circle and sphere** (R114; Tom 19:46–19:48). Paper new §2.4: Definitions 2.1 (address: pair in a facing, identified by relation), 2.2 (unit circle: all addresses over four facings = sign patterns, at g = 1; homes at the horizontal ends, horizons at the vertical ends), 2.3 (unit disc), 2.4 (sphere/ball: triple, two relations, eight octant facings); Proposition 2.1 (one representative per address on h² + v² = 1; the g = 1 slice is the classical circle; sphere likewise). Old §2.4–2.5 renumbered to §2.5–2.6 (all 8 in-text references updated). Propositions renumbered too: the new one is 2.1; the old 2.1 (view span) is now 2.2 and the old 2.2 (N unknown) now 2.3; earlier items cite the old numbers.
265. **Ruled: the three in plain words** (R115; Tom 19:52: the "complete set of relations points to…" sentence is awkward). Relations = the sweep from horizontal to vertical; densities = from the full shape to the unit circle; magnitudes = the world's. Paper abstract, §2.3 list, §12.
266. **Ruled: magnitudes are not swept** (R116; Tom 19:57; first written into the paper at 19:54 during a turn Tom cancelled, with a paraphrased quote, then corrected to his words). One specific value per address, defined by the system; the reader sweeps relations and densities and meets the magnitude. Paper: abstract, §2.3 list and nesting paragraph, the ranges note.
267. **Ruled: the Cauchy need not know the magnitude; the system is orthogonal to the sweep** (R117; Tom 20:00–20:01). One sentence pair added to §2.3 after R116.
268. **Demo: Near or Far, level 1** (`game/near-or-far.html`, artifact 5d1YS94ZfDYwPLrBdWLDMu; Tom 03:09 "yes" to a game built on what the view from somewhere cannot do, tell size from distance). Built on Horizon Defender's renderer. Each round: shapes at separate bearings, every one covering the same turn (size = 0.16 × distance), half near (4–7) and half far (45–90), still, facing the reader; all drawn alike, the disc and the payload bars hidden (they would give distance away). Drag or A/D sways the reader sideways (±4); near shapes slide much faster (parallax). Tap fires along the address: near hit gold, far hit grey (wasted). Round clears when the near ones are gone; rounds grow 6 → 12 shapes.
269. **Demo: Standpoints** (`game/standpoints.html`, artifact DzhQhUDjyzmfz2iAWNS9gq; Tom 03:20–03:22: "smarter game play, things would be more self contained … kind of a simulation, and the user could just switch to different pieces, to watch the simulation play out from different perspectives"). Horizon Defender's renderer; the game loop only moves bodies (R83). Two teams of six readers and nine walls in an arena of radius 30. Each reader senses every 0.15 s by 40 addresses over its own front half (first border only; 60 units), and acts on that alone: fire along an address that finds the other team; take cover on the far side of the nearest wall from the reader it sees; flank toward where it last saw one (6 s memory); step off a bullet's address when the bullet is on its own front half and not hidden; otherwise turn and wander. Bullets fly straight at 24/s and stop at the first border (teammates and walls included). Three hits: back to its side after 3 s. The user only watches: tap a reader in the view to see from it, tap the disc or press V for the next. Near or Far (268): sizes restored to equal true size and the radar restored on Tom's objection ("shouldn't far away objects be smaller? where is the radar?").

270. **Demo: Wander, and what building it showed** (`game/wander.html`, artifact TnRv8p1qx44X4ARgHqvcAW, Sep 30; Tom 03:39: "how about a world full of objects that i just walk through and shoot. shooting an object removes it from the game. I should be able to move through the world."). Grown in steps on Tom's asks: flying on the front half of the sphere, both across and up laid evenly in turn, with tilt as a true turn of the whole front half (03:47 "if it was the sphere than you could fly through the objects in 3d"); solids taken from the rung editor's presets, each a radius r(θ, h) about its own axis (03:56); every pixel one address on the graphics chip, each keeping its own first border (04:08); a behaviour of its own for every object (04:25); a world with no edge, built cell by cell, each cell fixed by the cell alone (04:28 "the world should extend in all directions, with no noticable circularity. its the cauchy that creates the circularity of observeration."); tap an object to orbit it, looking at it (04:36); gravity, star systems, belts, pairs of stars and bodies passing between (04:39). Three observations, not yet tested:
   (a) *An unbounded world and the view from nowhere.* The view from nowhere had to choose a range and a scale, and the choice showed: the log-scaled radar packed everything held into a band whose edge drew as a ring, and the straight-scaled radar had to stop at a cut (30 units). The view from somewhere needed neither: each address is a relation, magnitude divided out; distance goes into g, falling toward 0 at the vanishing point; the horizon holds v/h → ∞ at a finite address; and a first border makes whatever lies behind it unneeded. This is N unknown (R96, Prop 2.3) taken all the way. The program holds only about 42–86 units of world either way; for the view from somewhere that limit falls where things are already specks near g = 0, while the view from nowhere has to draw it. Tom 04:34: "this would be a challange with view from nowhere".
   (b) *The poles of the two-relation address pair.* Tilted to straight up, the top row of the picture is one direction stretched across the whole width: the pair (across, up) collapses at the poles, where facings meet. Taken as a feature of the sphere's addressing scheme (§2.4 of serial-parallel-nowhere: two relations over eight facings), not of the renderer.
   (c) *Two addressings meeting.* Each solid carries its own addresses (θ a full turn, h from base to top, payload r); a reader's address meets it where it first falls inside r. The reader's first border is found by stepping through the object's addresses, and a shape change (morph) is exact at every stage because it is a blend of two payloads on the same addresses.

271. **Wander, the full loop** (`game/wander.html`, artifact TnRv8p1qx44X4ARgHqvcAW, versions 12–14, Sep 30 05:14–05:41). Tom 05:14 on the game loop: "i think this is where the game loop really hurts us. it subverts the situation." (one shared now, a snapshot every frame, pulling not receiving, the frame swallowing serial, the engine holding everything). 05:18: "yes, a reader receives multiple signals. each of the other objects in their parallel read would be a distinct signal." 05:33: "from the v/h we create an address back to the parallel reader, who has the address to the system." Tom 05:08 on parallel: "paralellel reading provides a range of address, that is set by the reader. it does not take a snapshot, but creates a range of addresses to scan with the serial reader." Rebuilt so, step by step:
   1. *The system.* Its own magnitudes and its own time; each system steps at its own rate (18–35 steps a second, none shared) and at each step sends a signal tagged with its own time.
   2. *Propagation (the world's side).* Signals travel at 20 units a second; the reader holds, for each body, the latest signal to have reached it. Where two arrive along one address the one behind is blocked. What is handed over for each point is two unit pairs, across (h forward, v across) and up (h level, v up), each on h² + v² = 1, so no distance goes with them, and a payload naming the system.
   3. *Parallel (the reader's range).* Set in advance: the front half, laid evenly in turn, one address per pixel. No snapshot.
   4. *Serial (arrival).* v over h, with the facing each pair carries (atan2), is the address; the pair is then discarded. The range entry at that address keeps only an address back to the system: which system, and where on it (its own θ and h).
   5. *The system read through the range.* Each entry is followed to its system and the system is read there (its r(θ, h), its turn, its colour); the magnitude is met, not swept (R116). Tapping reads the range at the tapped address and follows it back (a tap on a pawn read "pawn" and began an orbit); the payload strip reads twelve entries down each turn.
   6. *g from arrivals, fed back.* g = how many of the reader's addresses a system's points spread over, against the system's own count of addresses (80 × 52); it rises with size and falls with distance without the reader knowing either (a large star at 37 units and a small planet at 11 both read 0.12; a pebble at 89 read 0.0002). It sets how many of the system's addresses are laid next (gN of the N, R94): 80 × 52, 40 × 26 or 16 × 10.
   7. *The reader acts.* Its motion changes what arrives next; its own signal goes out, and the curious and shy bodies act on the reader that has reached them (they are readers too).
   The three nested dimensions each have a place: relations are the pairs turned into addresses; densities are g, measured from arrivals; magnitudes stay with the system, reached only through the pointer. Checked: every body arrived at its distance ÷ 20 (to within one of its system's steps); what is seen of a body lay a mean 4.7 units (up to 11) from where it was; a body shot at 11.1 units (before shooting was removed) left the view after 0.65 s against 0.56 expected. Seams, stated: blocking uses distance on the world's side (the one place a magnitude decides something before the reader); addresses between two laid points are filled by joining them, so they point to places on the system that sent no pair of their own (g keeps the joins about one address wide); the simulation itself must hold every position in order to propagate; the screen still refreshes but only shows what has arrived. Bullets and the radar removed on Tom's word ("they are not helpful here"). Tom 05:40, on v/h used only to place: "oh, yeah, and that works?"; 05:41: "thats awesome".

272. **Wander: the first uses of a history of pairs** (Sep 30 05:58; Tom: "lets do the easy things first, so proceed with your suggestions"). A tapped point's arrivals are kept for 6 s, each with the reader's own heading and tilt at that moment and the signal's own time tag. (a) *Trail.* Its addresses drawn as a fading line, either as they arrived, or with the reader's own turning taken out (each past direction turned from how the reader faced then to how it faces now; the reader knows its own turning). Taking out the reader's travel as well would need nearness, which a history of turns alone does not give. (b) *Arrivals against ticks.* The time between two of its signals' arrivals over the time between their tags: 1 when the distance holds, below 1 when it closes, above 1 when it opens; a ratio to the signal's own speed, with no unit. Checked against a star with the reader facing it: still, 0.998 (distance changing 0.03 a second); flying toward it, 0.868 (−3.13 a second; 1 − 3.13/20 = 0.84); backing away, 1.197 (+3.20; 1.16). The small excess each way is from reading arrivals once a frame and from the flight speeding up at the start. Motion along the address, read from timing alone.

273. **g renamed: the sweep, from magnitude to address** (R118; Tom 06:38: "i dont think g 'density' is the correct word, its just the sweep from pure magnitude to pure address."; 06:41 "yes"). g = 1 is the magnitude fully expressed (the full shape, every address with its r); g → 0 is pure address (the unit circle, one representative per address). *Wander* shows the density reading and the sweep reading are one: measured from arrivals (item 271), g is how many of a system's addresses the reader tells apart; near 1 every address arrives with its own r, near 0 the whole system is one address. "Density" is kept for the Cauchy factor g multiplies; the relation is called the turn, so "sweep" means g alone. *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* carries the vocabulary through its abstract, §2.2–§2.4, §8.5, §12, §13 and Appendix A; quotes keep their words.

274. **The sweep's two ends, and where the divide by zero lives** (R118 refined; Tom 06:45: "or more specifically, at g=1 all N is used as the divisor. g = 0, N is unused as the divisor, so its the unit circle. notice this is a divide by zero situation. I'm not sure what that means."; 06:48: "but N is not necissarily known anyways."; 06:49 "yes"). With N unknown the sweep is a turn of the count, g = (2/π) · arctan(n · step) (R96), and never divides by N: g = 0 is home, the unit circle as a scheme (pure address, nothing laid); g → 1 is the horizon, the completed set carrying its magnitudes, never reached. Written as a share of a known N (the magnitude over gN) it divides by zero at the empty end; that belongs to the known-N case only. As for v/h, what goes underneath decides which end is home and which the horizon (R2). "The unit circle" had named both the scheme and the completed set; *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* Def. 2.2–2.4 and Prop. 2.1(b) now keep them apart (same point set, different amount laid). A ChatGPT reading that g = 0 is "the continuous closure" of the scheme was not taken: the continuum is the limit of a fine lay, N without bound, which is the other end.

275. **Wander: nearness without a unit** (Sep 30, 06:54 "yes"). Two chosen bodies, A and B. For each, over the last 0.6 s: ω, how fast the direction to the body's middle turns once the reader's own turning is taken out, and θ, the angle between that direction and the reader's own travel. A still thing's direction turns at v sin θ / d, so d ∝ sin θ / ω, and in the ratio of two the reader's speed cancels: B is (sin θ_B / ω_B) / (sin θ_A / ω_A) times as far as A, with no distance and no unit. It needs travel (turning alone moves every direction alike), and the reader's travel direction, which it has as its own. Checked against the true ratio of distances for four pairs of stars while the reader stepped sideways for 1.2 s: 2.43 against 2.53, 1.13 against 1.17, 0.40 against 0.39, 0.84 against 0.84. A first version tracked the tapped point on the surface and read 1.92, 0.79, 0.26 and 0.46: the point turns with the body's own spin, which reads as nearness, so the comparison uses the body's middle. A body with motion of its own (a planet going round) will read wrongly by the part of its motion across the address. Bears on *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §13, nearness and the sweep: nearness from a history of pairs, as a ratio, is available without g.

276. **Wander: distance in the reader's own lengths, the approach, and the body's own path** (Sep 30, 07:00 "proceed"). (a) *Distance.* sin θ / ω (item 275) is the distance in seconds of the reader's travel; times the reader's own speed, which it has as its own, it is the distance in the reader's own lengths (the unit is the reader's, R89; the rest is relations). A still round body stepped past sideways: 14.8 and 15.1 against 17.0. (b) *Coming straight at you.* g counts addresses across and up, so it grows as 1/d²: measured exponent 2.02 on a round body (4.6 on a long body seen from a changing angle, whose outline grows faster than its distance shrinks). The time to reach the reader is 2g / (rate of g), read at the middle of a 1 s fit and carried to the present: 3.15, 3.13, 3.17 s against 3.03, 2.97, 3.02 on a round body flown at. The direction's own turn over that time, as a share of the distance, says how wide it will pass; under 0.12 the readout says it is coming straight at you. Bodies that turn or breathe read the time wrongly. (c) *Trail with travel taken out.* Each past arrival placed at its distance along its own direction from where the reader was then, and seen from where it is now: the body's own path, with the reader's turning and travel both removed. A body with motion of its own makes the distance, and so this trail, approximate. Readout cycles three trail modes on a tap.

277. **Wander: periods and Kepler's third law, from relations alone** (Sep 30, 07:35 "Yes"). With a star as B and a planet as A, and the reader holding still (tapping A again stops going round it), the readout follows where A sits from B as the reader sees them, its own turning taken out: two turns, across and up of B. If A goes round B this goes round too. One loop's time is the mean spacing of the upward crossings of the wider-swinging turn; its size is half the loop's full span along its long axis (a circle's outline seen from any side is widest across its own diameter, so for bodies going round one B at about the same distance from the reader the ratio of sizes is the ratio of their loops, whatever their tilt; taking half the span evens out the near side seen larger and the far side smaller). Two loops about the same B give (size ratio)³ against (period ratio)². Measured from 57 units, a star with two planets: periods 18.4 and 30.3 s against 17.9 and 29.9 (two-body truth); sizes 0.1656 and 0.2321 against a/d = 0.164 and 0.231; (size ratio)³ = 0.363 and (period ratio)² = 0.369, against 0.358 for both. A first measure of size, the widest reach from the loop's middle, read 0.182 and 0.248 (Kepler 0.398 against 0.369): the near side is seen larger. The world had to change for this: with every body's mass its size cubed, planets were a tenth of their star and pulled each other off their orbits (distances from the star wandering 5.5–19.6 over a minute); planets and all bodies but stars are now 0.02 of that, and the same two planets held 9.3–9.7 and 13.1–13.5.

278. **Which of two is in front, from pairs: a measured test** (Sep 30, 08:43 "yes"). Not "whose arrivals continue unbroken": that break is the world's own blocking, so it is circular. The reader-side cue is parallax: under the reader's own travel the nearer body's direction turns faster (sin θ / ω, item 275). Test: six headings; every body received; pairs whose outlines overlap on the reader's addresses (one hides part of the other) and differ in distance by more than 1; the reader's order compared with the world's. (a) *One sideways step of 1.5 s:* 580 pairs, 481 right (83%), 1 undecided; every one of the 98 wrong involved a body with motion of its own (a planet, moon or wanderer); none wrong between two stars. By distance ratio: above 1.5, 304 / 314; 1.2–1.5, 85 / 105; below 1.2, 92 / 160, near chance. (b) *A step each way, 0.9 s right then 0.9 s left:* the part of each direction's turn that reverses with the reader's travel is parallax; the part that stays is the body's own motion, and cancels. 560 pairs, 508 right, 15 undecided, 37 wrong (all with a moving body): above 1.5, 283 / 283; 1.2–1.5, 106 / 111; below 1.2, 119 / 151. So the reader can order what overlaps from pairs alone, surely when one is half again as far as the other, mostly when closer, and only while it travels. Not yet put in place of the world's blocking in the demo: it needs the reader to have travelled, and it errs on near-equal depths with moving bodies.

279. **An address back to the magnitude; 3d is the sphere plus one relation** (R119; Tom 09:11: "it also gives you an address back to the original magnitudes. can we prove that with our framework? seems to me, that is what 3d is."; 09:12 "yes"). Proposition 8.6 of *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere*: a still point read from two standpoints P₀, P₁ = P₀ + b (b the reader's own travel), with α₀, α₁ the turns from the travel to the two arrivals, has d₀/|b| = sin α₁ / sin(α₁ − α₀), unique unless α₁ = α₀. Addresses on the right; on the left a v/h with the world's magnitude over the reader's own step. Turned, it is the forward lay d = D1 · tan ψ with D1 = |b| (R87). The triangulation is classical; the framework's part is that depth is not a third turn of the sphere but this relation, and that it needs motion (serial). Items 275–278 are the proposition applied to arrivals.

280. **Wander: size that holds, in the reader's own lengths** (Sep 30, 09:19–09:20; Tom: "so what can be done with the original magnitude? seems to me that makes it possible for readers to develop intuition for certain objects. how they exist in their envinoment."; "yes"). With the address back to the magnitude (Prop. 8.6), a body's size is the widest turn between any two of its arriving points times its distance: 2 d sin(w/2), exact for a ball whose middle is d away. The distance is kept current from where the body's middle was placed at the last parallax reading and where the reader is now, so it holds when the reader flies straight at the body or away (no parallax then). A star of true diameter 8.36: 8.13 at 23.8, 7.96 at 35.4, 8.80 at 17.4 (its span on the reader's addresses doubling over that range while the size read held within about 5%). A first version used 2 d tan(w/2) and read 8.25, 8.01, 9.09. A planet (a vase going round its star, 3.52 tall) read 2.66, 2.19, 3.16 at 11.7, 19.8, 10.3: its own motion spoils the distance, and a long body's span changes with the side it shows. Size constancy, the first piece of a reader's intuition for a thing, from pairs and the reader's own travel.

281. **Wander: weighing a star** (Sep 30, 09:25; Tom: "proceed", following the proposal to weigh a star from a planet's loop). With a star as B, measured for distance earlier (made A and travelled past), and a planet as A with the reader holding still: the loop time T comes from where A sits from B (item 277), the loop's reach in the reader's own lengths is its widest turn times B's distance (a = d·s/√(1−s²)), and 4π²a³/T² is how hard B pulls, GM, in the reader's own lengths and seconds. Weighed only once two loop times agree within 3%, since the first count can take half a loop for a whole one (it read GM 148 and 369 before this guard). Three stars: GM 34–39 against 39 (reach 6.6–6.9, true 6.5–7.1; T 18.1); 96–98 against 83 (reach 9.4, true 8.7–9.1; T 18.5); 92–93 against 84 (reach 9.4, true 8.9–9.2; T 18.9). Loop times hold; the reach comes out about 4% long, and cubing makes that 12–17% in the weight. The star's placed middle is now kept as a point when its distance is measured, so the distance stays right after the six-second history has moved on.

282. **Wander: the loop's reach as the longest chord through B** (Sep 30, 09:35; Tom: "Proceed"). The 4% long reach of item 281 came from the method, not from the arrivals: run on the exact arrived positions it still read 9.37 against 8.93, while the arrived loop's true extent was the same as the world's (8.93 both; a late signal changes when a point is seen, not which points). A loop tilted toward the reader has both ends of its widest span leaning to the near side, so the widest turn reads long by about half of (reach/distance)² when edge-on. The fix: lay the directions flat (across and up over forward, on a sheet square to B) and take the longest straight chord through B itself. Every chord through B is a line through B in the loop's plane; the one square to the line of sight has both ends as far from the reader as B is, and on the flat sheet it is the longest while reach/distance is below √½, at 2 × reach / distance. Allowing a chord to bend two degrees through B put 3% back on. The weighing now also waits until the loop time holds as one more loop comes in (a frame-to-frame check let a half-counted loop through as GM 311). Four stars: 37–39 against 39; 83 against 84; 151–152 against 150; 86–88 against 83 (reach 9.11 against 8.72–9.11, an orbit not quite round). It now takes about four loops, a minute or so, before B is weighed.

283. **Wander: prediction, and the signal's speed in the reader's own lengths** (Sep 30, 09:50; Tom: "Proceed"). Once B is weighed, the reader rebuilds A's circle in its own lengths: centred on B's placed middle, its reach from item 282, its one square-on diameter along the longest chord through B, its tilt from how much narrower the loop is square to that chord. Which half is nearer and which way A goes round are fitted against the last loop's arrivals (two sides × two ways × a tilt a little either way × 72 starting places); on a loop seen nearly edge-on, directions alone cannot tell the near half from the far. A goes round evenly in its own ticks, which each arrival carries, not in the reader's arrival times, since its near side's signals come sooner. The gap between an arrival and its tick, set against A's distance on the rebuilt circle, is a straight line: its slope is the time per length of the reader's own, so its inverse is the signal's speed in the reader's own lengths per second; its start is only the offset between A's ticks and the reader's clock, which need not be shared. The readout draws where A will be over the next half loop (blue dots) and, every half second, keeps a guess for three seconds ahead, checked against the arrival when the time comes. Four stars: the rebuilt circle's plane matched the true orbit's (cosine 0.994–1.000), its centre within 0.06–0.21; signals at 18.9–22.6 lengths a second against 20; three seconds ahead, off by 0.13–0.35 of the reader's lengths (2–5% of the reach), one refit at 0.68. On the way: taking the way round from one second of the track flipped it (misses of 2–5 lengths); taking it from the loop's signed area on the sheet still failed edge-on; advancing the circle in arrival time left a steady 1.2 lengths from the signal's own travel; reading the tilt from the sheet alone was sometimes 10° out.

284. **Wander: B's family, found from shared motion and checked by Kepler** (Sep 30, 10:12; Tom: "proceed"). With B chosen and the reader holding still, every body in the range within 60° of B's direction is laid on the same sheet square to B, five times a second. A body belongs to B when its track goes round B (B inside its loop, not beside it), its loop time holds as another loop comes in, and it weighs B as the rest do: each member's loop time and reach (longest chord through B, times B's distance) give GM again, and a body whose GM is more than 25% from the family's middle value is ringed grey as "not B's". Directions alone cannot tell a body going round B from one going round another star that lies along nearly the same direction; the weight can. The family is ringed blue and listed with loop times and reaches in the reader's own lengths. Four stars, 200 s each: every body listed was a true member once the weight test was on (5 of 5 planets for one, 3 of 4 bodies for another, 4 of 6 for another; the rest were moons or had not finished two loops). Members' weights for B: 145–161 against 150 (one at 180 from a single slow loop), 37–42 against 39, 83–95 against 83, 81–96 against 84. Reaches against true distances: 9.3/9.3, 13.7/13.6, 18.6/18.4, 6.8/6.9, 9.7/9.8, 12.8/12.8, 16.3/16.1. Before the weight test, outsiders read GM 81, 202 and 444 against 150 and 39.

285. **Wander: the shape in the round, side by side with what its address points to** (Sep 30, 10:32; Tom: "can we tap an object and show the recovered shape, with just an address?", then "Both, side by side"). With A chosen, two panels turn slowly about the same upright. Left: what A's address points back to, the system's own shape, back and all. Right: the shape rebuilt from arrivals. Each address of the range that points to A also names which point of A's own surface it shows, (θ, h), A's own address for it; per point, the reader keeps the directions it arrives along and the direction of A's middle at those moments. For a rigid body the step R from its middle to the point obeys R = s u − m w at every arrival (u the point's direction, w the middle's, s and m their distances); the reader's own place and A's travel drop out, and so does s, once R is taken square to u. The middle's distance at each moment is solved together with the points (alternating; each moment's distance is what makes all points agree), which leaves one scale: the shape is had up to a scale. A turning body is handled from the range too: its own h runs along its axis, so how h grows across the addresses in each view fixes a plane holding the axis, and the axis is square to all those planes' normals; the rate of turning is the one that makes the points agree, and each view is turned back to the present. Only points seen whole in a view are used (a point cut by the outline centres wrongly). Checked up to a scale against the system's own shape: a still gem 1.6–2.5%, a still twisted piece 2.0%, a slowly turning bishop 2.3–3.0% (axis to 1.000, rate 0.805–0.85 against 0.80), a spinning emerald 2.0% (rate 2.86 against 2.86), cubes 9–13% (far and small), a small fast-spinning cube 9–18%. On the way: carrying the middle's distance by g failed, since an outline's g changes as a body turns (9.5% for the gem, and the scale drifted); counting only whole-seen points did not help alone; the bishop sat at 50% until its turning was found; its distance from the reader's own travel while going round it read twice the truth (14.5 against 7.1), since going round a moving body the reader shares its motion, so that distance is now marked as holding only if A is still. Tom (11:15): "this is the best result yet.   congratulations.    add it to the papers." Written up as §8.8 of *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere*, with Proposition 8.8 (shape from named points: the standpoint cancels, the point's own distance drops out square to its direction, and a common factor in the middle's distances is the only one left).

286. **Wander: the jitter** (Sep 30, 11:20; Tom: "the drawing can get quite jumpy at times.  like a continuous jitter."). Three causes, timed in a software-rendered browser. Reading the range back for the rebuild every 0.15 s made the graphics card finish before the page could go on (235 ms each time there; far less on a real card, but a stall each time). The rebuild's solve took 34–143 ms once a second, and more for its full sweep every 8 s. And going round a moving body, the reader's place followed the body, which steps at its own system's rate (18–35 a second), not the frame rate, so the whole view moved in small steps. Now the range is read in the background (asked for in one frame, collected a frame or two later), the solve is done in steps of at most 3 ms a frame, and the body's place is carried on from its last step at its own speed. Rebuild cost per frame: median 4.2 ms, worst 13.9 ms (before: up to 531 ms); the gem's shape unchanged (1.4%). Bodies are still drawn as their signals arrive, at their own systems' rates.

287. **Wander: bodies drawn between arrivals** (Sep 30, 11:35; Tom: "its jittery when a irregular shape is selected"). Each body was drawn as its latest signal left it, and signals come 18–35 times a second, so a turning body turned in small jumps: invisible on a round one, plain on an irregular one, and plainer once the view itself moved smoothly (item 286). Now each body is drawn between its last two arrived signals, one step behind: from the earlier as the later arrives, gliding to the later by the time the next is due. Only what has arrived is shown; the tick it carries is carried the same way. In a test browser drawing about 3 frames a second, the frame-to-frame spread of a spinning cube's drawn turning rate halved (0.68 to 0.33, about −3.2 against −3.07); at 60 frames a second the earlier version held a body still for two or three frames and then jumped. The rebuild's solve steps were also made finer (longest 4.4 ms).

288. **Wander: a still reader and a turning body** (Sep 30, 11:49; Tom: "#5 , #6", testing the implication of Prop. 8.8 that a body's own turning can supply the views). The reader kept a fixed offset from a spinning body, so that nothing but the body's turning changed the view.
