Given the body's axis, the named points gave the shape up to a scale from the turning alone: a cylinder 1.9%, an emerald 0.7%, a cube 13.6%, the rates found 2.38 (2.37), 2.86 (2.86), 3.07 (3.07). The proposition holds. What a still reader lacks is its own way to the axis: from one standpoint every view's plane is the same, so how h runs across the addresses fixes the axis only to that plane, not in how far it leans toward the reader. A search over the lean, with the rate taken from the point named at the body's middle (it turns past at the body's own rate), found it for the cube (axis to 0.999, 15%) but not reliably for the cylinder (0.95–0.985, 26–44%) or the emerald (the one-standpoint case not recognised, a wrong rate taken); it was not kept. First runs held the reader still while the planet went round its star (it receded from 7 to 25, and its own travel changed the view), then moved the reader with the body by jumps, which the reader took as its own travel (distances read nonsense); both were faults of the test.

289. **Wander: identity, stripped** (Sep 30, 11:49). §11's assumption 2, identity given, tested: A followed with no name. Each arrival is taken unnamed: the direction of its middle (the reader's own turning taken out) and how large it spans on the reader's addresses. From the tapped address (the arrival whose middle is nearest it), the follower predicts where A's next arrival will be from where its arrivals have been heading and how fast, and takes the unnamed arrival that fits best in direction (within three times the expected miss) and span (within a factor of about two); if none fits it says it has lost A and carries on along the prediction. The name is used only to score it. Going round: 8 planets and 6 belt bodies, on A 100% of the time. Travelling sideways while watching A, so that everything else sweeps across it: 8 planets on A 100%, with other unnamed arrivals inside the follower's window in 306–1603 frames a run (144–489 of them of similar span); 6 belt bodies on A 96%, one lost for a while and said so. Wrong 0% throughout. On the way: picking up at the tap by middle-over-span took a large star behind; a loose fit took the next body when A left the reader's front half (it now says lost); test taps on bodies behind the reader, and flights straight at A, were faults of the test. Limit: a body wholly behind another still arrives as an unnamed signal here (blocking is at the address, §8.5), so passing behind is not yet tested; nor are two bodies of like span crossing at like speed.

290. **Wander: everything with names off** (Sep 30, 12:44; Tom: "proceed", on "run everything without names" then "necessity switches"). The follower of item 289 became the reader's own tracks of every arrival at once, each with its own label (#12). Each frame the unnamed arrivals and the tracks are matched, closest fit first (direction within three times the expected miss, span within about a factor of two); an arrival no track wants starts a track; a track nothing fits carries on along its prediction, for 3 s, or for 20 s if its prediction lies within a nearer arrival's outline (gone behind it), its size held as last seen and its window widening the longer it is unseen. A body wholly behind a nearer one does not arrive (blocking decided before arrival, §8.5). A switch in the readout (tap its line, or N) turns names off: A, B and B's family are then tracks, the tap takes the track whose arrival covers the tapped address, and going round follows the track's arrival, not the true body. All tracks, all bodies at once, 30–40 s each: a track moved to another body 0.15 (flying), 0.35 (strafing), 0.47 (turning), 1.17 (going round) times per 10,000 track-frames, about once a track every two minutes or more, nearly all a planet and its moon or two bodies along one line; a body unseen for longer than its track lives comes back under a new label (1.03–2.04 labels a body). With names off, three stars weighed by a planet's track: 83 against 84, 36–37 against 39, 125–127 against 123; their families as tracks 80–85, 36–37, 121–131; three seconds ahead 0.14–0.87 (0.16–1.35 while settling); the signals 19.7–20.3 lengths a second once settled; the planet's track on the planet 83–100% of the time, the star's 100%. The shape is the one reading that needs names: which addresses are A's (the range's pointer), and the left panel is the pointer itself. On the way: a body passing beside the reader wrapped round its view and read as enormous, hiding everything (now capped and not counted as blocking); a planet hidden for three seconds by a bishop 1.5 lengths from the reader came back under a new label until its size was held while unseen; a loop time of 0.1 s got through and the circle fit found nothing (guarded); a test that tapped a star's track as A and B together (guarded).

291. **What each result needs, by switching one thing off** (Sep 30, 12:44). From items 270–290, with one new run: the reader never travels (two stars, 140 s). Without its own travel: no distance, so no size in its own lengths, no weight, no circle, no prediction, no signals' speed; loop times and loop sizes as turns stay (so Kepler's ratios), and so does the family by shared motion (without the weight test, outsiders along one line get in, item 284). Without ticks: no arrivals against ticks, no signals' speed, and prediction in arrival time misses by 1.2 lengths (item 283). Without the surface names (θ, h): no shape in the round and no axis; size stays (the outline's widest turn). Without names: everything but the shape (item 290). Without the range's addresses: nothing. Written up as a table in §11 of *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere*.

292. **Wander: the last world-side steps closed** (Sep 30, 13:26; Tom: "#1 and #3"). Blocking: the range pass kept, at each address, the arrival with the least true distance. Now each body's own shape decides its own front and back (each point's depth about the body's middle along the line of sight, the body's own geometry), and which body is in front is decided by the reader's own distance to each. With names, that distance comes through the address: the system holds the body's own shape and how it is turned; set far off along the direction its middle arrives from, the shape spans a patch that shrinks as one over the distance, and the distance is the one at which it spans the patch its arrivals do (size constancy, run backwards). Against the true distance: within 0.2% (10th to 90th percentile); overlapping pairs ordered right 99.8–99.9% (28,000–33,000 pairs). Without names there is no shape to follow to, and the distance comes from parallax per track (only the part of a track's turning that runs against the reader's own travel): 67–74% of overlapping pairs right (22,000–40,000), 0.3–5 times the truth; before that, a simple ratio of track turn to travel gave 70–73%, and span against a common size (the only thing left when the reader does not move) gives 70%. Going round: it placed the reader on a circle about the true body. Now it uses only what arrives: A kept at home on the reader's addresses, its span held at the size zoomed to, a step sideways at the chosen rate, all scaled by the reader's own distance estimate; A's own motion is followed by moving with any turning of its direction that the reader's own step does not account for. With names: 0.5–0.95 turns in 23 s at 0.25 rad/s asked, span held at 1.02–1.14 of the wanted, the reader's own distance within 0.1 of the truth. Without names the reader's distance is too rough to follow A's motion by, so only the span and the centring are kept: the reader stays with A (2.4–9.6 lengths), span 0.83–0.91 of wanted, going round unevenly (0.07–0.8 turns). On the way: several steps between two arrivals each expected only one step of the reader's own sideways travel, so the rest was read as A's motion and cancelled (the reader barely went round) until the expected direction was chained step to step; with names off, following A's motion scaled by a rough distance carried the reader 65 lengths away. The shape rebuild (gem, emerald) holds with the new going round (emerald 2.9%). What remains world-side: the range is drawn from the arrived states' positions, and the scoring.

293. **Wander: the shape without names** (Sep 30, 13:26). With names off the range still says, at each address, whether something arrived and which point of that thing's own surface it is, (θ, h); only not which thing. A's addresses are found from the address of A's middle (its track's arrival), spreading through neighbours whose surface names run on (a step of under 0.06 of a turn and 0.1 in h), within the patch A's arrivals span; where the surface names jump, another body begins. The same rebuild then runs on them (Prop. 8.8). Of the addresses found, on A: a still twisted piece 99.3–99.6% (shape within about 2%), a still gem 97–100% (1.9–3.3%, one late check at 25% when something passing leaked in), a spinning cube 99.1–100% (rate 3.08 against 3.07, 13%), a spinning emerald 46% rising to 87% (rate 2.85 against 2.86, 10–12%). Without the patch limit the emerald's search leaked into neighbours whose surface names happened to run on (28–71%). The whole shape the address points to is the one thing names alone give; the left panel says so with names off.

294. **Wander: the jitter in Chrome** (Sep 30, 14:00–14:25; Tom: "the jitter is back for irregular object.s", "much improved, but irregular objects still jitter when selected", "is an up/down jitter, but only when it is selected", "safari is not jittery, only chrome"). Three causes, found in turn. (1) Going round from arrivals (item 292) followed A's own motion by moving with any turning of A's direction that the reader's own step did not account for, but the next frame's expectation left out the turning that this move itself caused, so the correction swung every frame (0.004, 0.024, 0.004 …); the expectation now includes it. (2) The reader's distance to A through the address was worked out with A set far off, which near an irregular body turning swung 0.87–1.05 of the truth as it showed different sides; for the chosen body it is now corrected three times with the shape set at that distance, perspective included (1.000–1.005). (3) The one Chrome alone showed: the strip at the foot of the view read the range from the card address by address, 576 reads a frame, each making Chrome wait for the card ("GPU stall due to ReadPixels", Chrome's own warning, every frame); Safari does not wait. Uneven frames show as jolts in anything moving steadily, so in a body gone round or turning. The card now shrinks the range onto the strip's 48 × 12 grid itself and the page reads that small grid in the background a frame later: 99.2% the same entries, no stall warnings. Also on the way: a chosen body is drawn at full detail and other bodies keep their level of detail until clearly past a threshold; a body's arrived state never steps back to an earlier one when the reader moves a little toward and away; the rebuild's reads rest when the readout is hidden, and come every 0.3 s. Ruled out by measurement: frame work (median 5.5 ms selected), the tilt and the reader's height, A's drawn place (under half a pixel a frame).

295. **Wander: collisions, and the three coincidences** (Sep 30, 14:35–15:30; Tom: "what about collisions. would a view from somewhere framework have something new to say about that?", then "write the document first, then plan out how we should add collisions.", then "proceed with your suggestions." (R120)). The world: some cells hold a cluster, five to seven bodies moving straight inside a round bound they cannot see (the world's side turns them back at it), colliding and bouncing, momentum kept, collisions only within one system (no shared now between systems). A first cluster round a hidden centre of pull threw bodies out (to 109 lengths) and softened bounces did not hold it; the bound does (bodies within 8 of the centre, 9–15 touches a minute). Contact against overlap, over every pair of one cluster meeting on the reader's addresses, 60 s from 22 lengths off: from eight other standpoints around each pair, a contact still met on the addresses from all of them (100%), an overlap from 19%: contact is the coincidence every standpoint agrees on. The reader's three ways to it: through the address (each body's own distance from its own shape), right on 100% of contacts and 95.9% of overlaps; from the ticks (two bodies of one system shown at one moment carry ticks differing by their depth difference over the signals' speed), 100% and 96.7%; both with a tenth of the touching distance allowed (without it, contacts 78–83%, overlaps 99–100%). The signals' speed for the tick test the reader learns by flying straight away from a star: ticks over arrivals is 1 + v/c exactly, so c = v/(1/ratio − 1): 20.1 against 20 (the first-order form, c = v/(1 − ratio), read 15.3). Prediction: from the two bodies' placed middles, a straight fit over the last second, the time the gap first closes to touching: made one to three seconds ahead, within 0.25 s of when the touch reaches the reader, most within 0.1 (first scored against when the touch happened, which differs by the signal's travel, and first predicting the nearest approach instead of the first touch, which read 0.5–1.3 s late). Weighing by bouncing (Mach): each body's change of speed over the other's is their mass ratio; kept only when the two changes point opposite ways (momentum between them alone): 1.63/1.64, 2.37/2.36, 1.64/1.64, 3.11/2.94, 1.85/1.86, 1.48/1.50, 0.41/0.42, 1.06/1.06; some off by 15–32% (2.14/1.86, 1.53/2.26) that the check did not catch. With names off, across three clusters and 28 touches, no track moved to another body; one label a body.

296. **Pool: a demo for collisions alone** (Sep 30, 15:40–16:30; Tom: "we could create a new demo for testing collisions, such as a pool table with pool balls."). A table 20 × 10 of the reader's lengths, a white ball and ten others racked in a triangle, one of them heavy (radius 0.6 against 0.45, mass 2.37), cloth that slows them, cushions, six pockets; bounces keep momentum. Everything reaches the reader as signals sent every fortieth of a second at 50 lengths a second, each ball's arrival interpolated between the last two ticks to arrive, and what has arrived stays arrived. The reader stands round the table facing its middle, and the edges of the table are drawn through the address lay, so they bend; nearer balls are drawn over farther ones by the reader's own distance to each, from its span. The signals' speed is learned as the reader walks in and out, from still balls: the ticks run at 1 + (speed toward it)/c of arrivals, fitted over many frames at once (a running average of c frame by frame read 44 against 50, since dividing by a small, noisy difference leans; the fit reads 49.4–50.3; a jump of the reader, faster than 40 a second, is left out). Scored against the world's side, from 26 lengths off: the touch shows as CONTACT when it arrives, 0.4–0.6 s after it happened (the signal's travel), never on a pair apart; predictions, once half a second of the moving ball has arrived, within 0.13 s of when the touch is seen (the first one made, while the fit still spans the start of the motion, is off by 0.5–1.0 s); weighing by bouncing, white against the heavy ball 0.41, 0.41, 0.42, 0.43 against 0.422, and against ordinary balls 0.95, 0.97, 0.99, 1.00 against 1, all passing the opposite-ways check. Caveat: the reader is given each ball's size (the heavy ball's larger radius included) and works out its distance from the span with it, so the placing is names on, as Wander's size constancy is; the mass ratio itself comes only from the changes of speed. And every scored run had the white and one other ball alone on the table: a full break, many touches at once and balls blocking each other, is not yet tested. Touch: drag back from the white ball to shoot, drag elsewhere to walk round, pinch or wheel to come in, tap for A and B; on a narrow screen the readout wraps and sits below the table. Demo: `pool.html`.

297. **Pool, pushed: told nothing about the balls** (Sep 30, 15:30–17:45; Tom: "proceed. lets see how far we can push this to be a demo of view from somewhere."). The reader is now given nothing but its own steps and the arrivals: not the balls' sizes, not the ticks' zero (the table counts its ticks from a zero of its own, hidden). What it reads, and in what order. (1) *Which ball is nearer*: a later tick is a nearer ball, before anything is learned (the drawing order uses only this). (2) *The signals' speed*: the reader's clock stamps each tick's arrival; walking toward a still ball, ticks come at 1 + (its speed toward it)/c of its clock. Read per frame, the one-step-behind drawing runs in a sawtooth while the reader moves (rate 1 between arrivals, then a jump), and c read 38–44 against 50; from the arrival stamps, over a quarter second or more of the reader's own path, 49.5–50.0. (3) *Sizes and the ticks' zero*: a still ball's tick lag is τ = (r/c)·q + β, with q = 1/sin(span/2); walking in and out spreads q, each ball gives its slope and all share β. β came within 0.001–0.002 s of the hidden zero; then every ball, moving or not, is as far as c(τ − β), and as big as that times sin(span/2): sizes 0.986–0.991 of the truth (the heavy ball's larger size read, not given), distances 1.009–1.014. (4) *Weighing every touch it sees*, over full breaks and follow-up shots, four racks. Three things decided it, found in turn. First, a third ball within half a second spoils a touch that still passes every check (off 2–3.5×); the reader sees those touches too, so each ball's before and after curves now stop short of its other touches, and before it comes to rest. Second, the curves were fitted against arrival time; a ball moving toward or away from the reader has its arrivals squeezed or stretched by that motion (up to 20% here), and each ball differently; fitted against the ticks the balls sent, the table's own time, the median touch went from 2–6% off to 0.1–0.8%. Third, the table sends 40 ticks a second, so a touch mostly falls between two ticks and no tick shows the pair touching (at one tick they came no closer than 1.26 against 1.05 touching); the reader finds the touch where the gap between them, read at one tick, closes and turns open within one tick's travel of touching. With these, from touches of two alone (groups of three or more set aside), each ball the middle value its touches give it: after one rack every ball weighed against the white within 10%; after three, all ten within 0.1% (heavy ball 1.001 of its true 2.37); the median touch exact to three places, one touch in 30 off by 26%, outvoted. Touches kept: about a third of those seen; the rest had a third ball or a cushion in their windows. Tried and dropped: weighing groups by Σ m·Δv = 0 across a group; groups rarely come clean (3 of 23), and when two balls' changes of speed nearly line up the weights run off (329 and 403 against 1). Also: the pair readout gives the gap at one tick, and predictions say when you will *see* the touch (the tick of the touch plus the lag of the pair's ticks), within 0.1 s once half a second of motion has arrived. Demo: `pool.html`.

298. **Pool, names off** (Sep 30, 16:30–17:40; Tom: "proceed"). A button takes the names away: every ball the same grey, no colours, numbers or stripes. The reader keeps its own tracks (labelled a, b, c … in the order it starts them). Each arrival goes to the track whose next place it best fits, nearest fits first: its address against the track's address carried on, counted in the track's own half-spans; its span against the track's span; and how far it is, from its tick lag. A track not fed for a second is let go, and when you re-rack, which is your own act, the old tracks end. The white is a name too, so the reader cannot be told which ball is the white: it weighs against **the ball you strike**, known by its own act (with names off any ball can be struck; the first struck in a rack is "yours", and if its track ends the next one struck is). What it took. (1) The tick lag steps a tick at a time while the reader walks, so carrying it on between ticks broke tracks: 105 tracks and 56 switches over 10 s of walking in and out; comparing the lag with its last value, allowing a tick of the reader's own walking and 0.6 lengths more, 11 tracks and none switched. (2) A fault in the world's side, found here: after a re-rack, the one-step-behind drawing reached back into a tick slot from before the rack (only once the record had not yet wrapped), which stopped the frame; now it reaches back only among ticks sent since the rack. Scored against the table over four racks of full breaks and follow-up shots: no track switched over about 140 touches plus the walking; sizes 0.987–0.988 of the truth on every track, new tracks within a second of a rack; the ball you strike was the white in every rack (after the white was pocketed and put back, a new track, then the next strike). Weights against the ball you strike, one rack at a time, since without names nothing ties a ball in one rack to a ball in the next: 2–9 balls weighed a rack, most within 5%, the worst 8–11% (fewer touches a ball than when four racks are pooled with names on, where all ten come within 0.1–1.3%). Only the heavy ball could be carried across racks without names, by its size. Touch: tap to choose, drag back from any ball to strike it; checked on a phone. Demo: `pool.html`.

299. **Pool: a full rack, and riding a ball** (Sep 30, 16:55–17:25; Tom: "could you do a full rack of balls? and toggle between the different balls to see what the ball sees?"). Fifteen balls in five rows, one of them the heavy ball. "Seen from" puts the reader on a ball, each in turn: its eye on the ball's top, going where the ball goes, facing the way it rolls (turning toward it gently), and wherever the hand turns it; the ball does not see itself, and every other ball reaches it late by its distance from it. From the white before the break the rack sits on the horizon; as the white closes in, the near balls swell to fill the view while the far ones still show the rack at rest, their news not yet arrived. Drawing from a low eye needed the table's outline cut where it passes behind the reader's facing, and the cut itself laid point by point so it bends with the addresses (drawn straight, the cloth ended short of the foot of the view). Two things about changing standpoint. The reader's record of where it stood joined the ball's path to its seat when it came back, so for a moment it seemed to have moved twenty lengths in a frame, and the signals' speed drifted from 49.7 to 53–55; the record now starts again at each change of standpoint, and the speed holds (49.7 before, 49.6–49.7 after). And a ball thrown about by its touches is not a place to learn the signals' speed or the ticks' zero from: those are learned while you walk, and riding uses what was learned. With names off, riding ends the tracks, as a re-rack does. Touch: the button cycles, drag looks round; checked on a phone. The code was then tidied (Sep 30, 17:10–17:50), same behaviour: dead paths from tried-and-dropped methods removed, the table, what arrives and the reader in separate sections, the reader's state gathered, each tuning value named with the item that set it, the scoring hooks only with `?test`; rerun, names on all fifteen within 1.3% after three racks, names off no switches in four racks and sizes 0.99, pairs 1.006 and 0.423 against 1 and 0.422, the signals' speed held through a ride (49.6). Demo: `pool.html`.

300. **Pool: the reader's clock is not given either** (Sep 30, 18:05–19:20; Tom: "Proceed", to "take the last piece of the view from nowhere away"). Until now the reader's clock ran at the table's rate, from an unknown zero. Now its clock runs at a rate of its own (hidden, 0.85–1.15 of the table's) from a zero of its own, and it stamps arrivals on that clock. What it reads, in order. (1) *The rate*: over a baseline of its own clock, a still ball's ticks advance by rate·(its seconds) + (rate/c)·(its own step toward the ball); in the moments it is nearly still toward a ball (the walk turns round often), its ticks come at the rate itself; the middle of those readings gives the rate, exact to five places (0.93866 against 0.93866, 1.07399 against 1.07399). (2) *The signals' speed*, in its own lengths a second of its own clock: what is left of the ticks over its own step, one ratio screened against the moment's others, as when the rate was given: within 0.5–0.7%. (3) Sizes and the zero as before. Three things found. First, fitting the rate and c together in one least squares left the rate 0.16% off, and with fifteen balls tight in a rack and tracks switching it failed outright with names off (c read 77 against 47); the rate from near-still moments and c as one screened ratio fixed both. Second, a measured own step must use the ball's direction midway through the step, not at its end (second order, several percent over a long step to a ball off to the side). Third, and the one that matters: lags are counted from the reader's first stamp, so a rate error grows with every second, a 0.16% error moving every distance by about 0.1 length a second. The reader keeps its clock in step the way a clock is kept: once sizes are read, each still ball of known size says where the zero is now (its lag less r·q/c); the zero is re-read every moment, and a steady drift of it against the ticks is the rate error itself, which the reader corrects (the walking fit must then leave the rate alone, or it undoes the correction on the next frame). After one correction the rate was exact, distances held at 1.008–1.010 for a minute standing still. Sizes must not be frozen early: each ball's size read under the old rate carries its own error, and a size error scales that ball's speeds and so its weight (frozen sizes left weights within only 5%); sizes keep refining, readings under a rate far off counting for little. Scored, the same as with the rate given: names on, per-touch median 0.3%, 90th percentile 2%, thirteen of fourteen weighed balls within 2% after three racks; names off, learning holds on the tight rack, weights per rack mostly within 5%; pairs 0.423 against 0.422; predictions of when a touch will be seen, by its own clock, within 0.06 s once motion has arrived (anchored to the pair's latest arrivals; anchored to the first stamp, a small rate error grew to 0.3 s). What the reader is now given: nothing about the balls or its clock; only its own steps, the ticks the signals carry, and that ticks count evenly. Demo: `pool.html`.

301. **Wander: the pool fixes, tried, and why they do not help there** (Sep 30, 19:25–20:15; Tom: "Proceed", step 2 of "take the pool lessons back to Wander"). Wander's weighing by bouncing was ported to read each body's placed middles by the system's tick, compare the two at one tick, find a touch between ticks from the turn of the gap, and fit straight lines in ticks' time (no cloth in Wander), keeping a touch only if the lines hold and the changes point opposite ways. Scored against the world's side with a harness that puts the reader 22 lengths from a cluster, lets it settle, and chooses the pair about to touch (choosing is the user's act; the harness chooses by truth), the old version and the port the same way, twelve minutes each: old, 14 touches weighed of 42 watched, median error 11.7%, 90th percentile 27%; port, 13 of 33, median 11.7%, 90th percentile 35%. No gain. Why: in Pool the distances come from the tick lag and are exact, so the stretching of arrivals by each ball's own motion (and touches falling between ticks) was the largest error left; in Wander the distances come from size constancy, and a clean straight run of placed middles strays 0.04–0.13 lengths from its line (seen in the port's own check, which first rejected genuine touches at 3% of the touching distance), about a tenth of a change of speed over half-second windows, which buries the stretching (5–12% at Wander's slow signals, 20 lengths a second). Wander is left as it was; the port is kept aside (`wander.tickport.html`). What would carry over is the other half of Pool: distances from tick lag, c(τ − β), for bodies of one system, in place of size constancy. Also seen: the first touch after the reader is put somewhere is read wrongly in both versions (2–3×), before the second body's arrivals have settled.

302. **Wander: distances from the tick lag, and both halves together** (Sep 30, 19:20–20:30; Tom: "proceed"). For bodies of one system, the distance is now read from the tick lag, c(τ − β), in place of size constancy. In Wander the lag is exact: a body drawn one step behind shows the tick it left at, and now − that tick is its flight time plus one of its system's own steps, which is fixed, so each system has a zero β. The reader learns it slowly from many arrivals of the system's bodies, as τ − (distance from its size)/c; size constancy's scatter averages away because the zero does not change. Until a system's zero is known, its bodies are placed by their size as before. The signals' speed is the one learned by flying at a star (20.1, item 295; the harness gives it rather than flying there). Scored with the item-301 harness, twelve minutes each:

| Wander weighing by bouncing | weighed | median error | 90th percentile |
|---|---|---|---|
| as it was (arrival time, size distances) | 14 of 42 | 11.7% | 27% |
| ticks' time only | 13 of 33 | 11.7% | 35% |
| tick-lag distances only | 22 of 44 | 13.4% | 28% |
| both | 23 of 44 | 2.6% | 14.6% |

Neither half helps alone; together they cut the error by a factor of four, as in Pool: each removes the error that buries the other's. Published as Wander's version 44. Also: the first touch after the reader is put somewhere is still read wrongly (the harness lets it settle six seconds); with names off, nothing changed (tracks have no system to give a tick lag a zero, R120's contact through ticks being within one system).

303. **Demo: the breadth sweep** (`demos/breadth-sweep.html`, artifact 9meeXKP2M292ktHCeNycYv; Tom 20:57: "the typical situaiton is breadth, not an array. make a new demo, based on the cachy sweep. but instead of an array, use breadth as the N. the cauchhy two dgrees of freedom point to particular point on that breadth."; then 21:03: "breadth is just like N, except that it continuous, not an index."). The Cauchy sweep (item 252) with a breadth B in place of N: an extent, continuous, any value from 0.1 to 10,000 of its own lengths. Read as R118 has it with B for N (g = 1, all of B the divisor; g = 0, none, the unit circle), the two degrees of freedom point to one place: x = g · B · turn / (π/2). g says how much of the breadth the quarter turn is divided over (home to g·B, where the horizon lands; the corner at g·B/2), the turn how far through it. Panels: the disc (every ring whole now, since every turn is a place; the turn swept from home to the place drawn along this g's ring), the breadth as a ruler in its own lengths (the divided part, the corner's and horizon's landings, the place), and the Cauchy g·(2/π)/(1+s²) with the place's reading s = v/h marked: the area up to s, over g, is the share of the divided part before the place, (2/π)·atan(s) = x/(g·B). Touch, mouse and keys; auto-sweep as before. The reading x = g·B·turn/(π/2) is Claude's, from "just like N" and R118, offered for correction. Corrected the same evening (Tom 21:07: "if there is only one facing, you only have one breadth. breadth is either known or unknown. when known, it is the quarter circle."; 21:11: "g is the same, for all situations. g is actaully just the radius. when radiius=0, than breadth = 1. when the radius = breadth, the breadth is fully expressed."). Rebuilt: g is the radius, in the breadth's own lengths; a toggle between breadth known and unknown. Known: the breadth is the quarter circle, the radius runs 0 to B, and what it has expressed is E = 1 + radius·(B − 1)/B (1 at radius 0, the unit circle; B at radius B, fully expressed); the place is the turn's share of it, x = E·turn/(π/2), on a ruler from home to B; the Cauchy is drawn with area E/B. Unknown: the radius has no top (the disc's rim is dashed, radius → ∞), E = 1 + radius grows without a far end and is never all of it; the ruler follows E; the Cauchy has no whole to be a share of, and the area up to the place's reading is still the turn's share, (2/π)·atan(s) = x/E. The straight run of E from 1 to B between the two stated ends is Claude's, offered for correction. Then (Tom 21:15: "so this is one facing. two facings is semi-circle. so two breadths. one to the left and one to the right. if the breadths are known, than its the semicrcle. same for circle at 4 breadths, same for sphere with 8 breadths."): a facings choice, 1, 2 or 4, laid as the array sweeps lay them (items 253–254). One breadth per facing, all sharing B and the one radius: 2 facings, home straight ahead and a breadth to each horizon (a ruler through home, a breadth each way); 4 facings, front and back homes, horizon A ending the two right breadths and B the two left (two rulers, front and back). The place falls on the breadth of the facing the disc points into; each facing reads its own Cauchy from its own home (v/h alone does not say which facing), drawn with area (E/B)/k. Eight facings, the sphere, needs the ball (a direction takes two angles, so a third number and a second gesture, item 256) and is to be its own page. Confirmed (Tom 21:20): "correct, when known, the breath is the same for each facing." One B for every facing when known. Then (Tom: "yes, add the 8 facing button"): 8 facings on the same page, not its own. The ball: homes at front and back (±z), the eight octants by the signs of the direction at the view centre; drag turns the ball, pinch, wheel or a radius slider sets the radius. Eight rulers, one per octant, all sharing B and the radius; the Cauchy drawn with area (E/B)/8. On the ball the place is read along the turn from the facing's home (front or back) to the centre direction; the azimuth within the octant only picks which facing. That reading is Claude's, offered for correction.
304. **The unit circle and the unit sphere, with the breadth known** (R121; Tom 21:29: "this demo answers what the unit circle and unit spheres actually are. this is for known breadth. and g is [0,1] or the radius from 1 to the breadth."). With the breadth B known, g ∈ [0, 1] is the radius in the breadth's own lengths, r = 1 + g(B − 1). g = 0, radius 1: four facings, the unit circle; eight, the unit sphere. g = 1, radius B: the breadth fully expressed, the circle (sphere) of the breadth. The place is x = r · turn/(π/2), and the Cauchy's share to the reading is (2/π) · arctan(s) = x/r. The demo relabelled to match: a g slider and readout on [0, 1], the radius read from 1 to B (unknown: 1 + g/(1 − g), no top). Written up in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 (Defs 2.5–2.6, with a table of 1/2/4/8 facings) and noted in *Reader Geometry as Addressing* §5.3 and ruling 121. (The number R120 was already the three coincidences, item 295.)
305. **The address stays with its magnitude; infinite breadth and infinite density** (R122; Tom 21:37: "notice that at each address, from radius = 1 to radius = breadth, it stays connected to the same magnitude, but between the unit circle and the full expression of magitude, it is the ratio of between 0 and 1. but the key point is that the addres is always the same for each magnitude. and because circles can be infinitely dense, this supports. infinite breadth. this how infinite breadth and infinite density are connected."). In the demo's terms: at a fixed turn, x/r = turn/(π/2) at every g, so the address does not move as the radius runs from 1 to B; g is the ratio between the unit circle and full expression. The address does not depend on B, the unit circle can hold addresses without limit, so a breadth without limit is still fully addressed. Breadth is how far the radius reaches, density how many addresses the circle holds, and the address is what they share. Against the Sep 24 ruling that dropped "infinite density" from the view from nowhere: Claude offered that the two do not clash because the density here is the addresses', not the world's. Tom 21:39: "that is incorrect, this demo is correct." So the clash is real and the demo stands; the Sep 24 drop of infinite density does not hold against it. Written into *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* §5.3, ruling 122.
306. **Density is the bridge; the reader is the unit circle** (R123; Tom 21:42: "seems to me that breadth is the bridge between discreteness and continuity. the view from somewhere is discreteness, the view from nowhere is continuity, and the bridge between them is density."; asked which of the two he meant, 21:47: "the bridge is density. somewhere is discrete, nowhere is continuous. no density means the unit circle, full density is the world. reader than is the unit circle."). Somewhere is discrete, nowhere continuous, density between. No density: the unit circle, addresses only (g = 0, radius 1 in the breadth sweep). Full density: the world, every magnitude fully expressed (g = 1, radius B). The reader is the unit circle. Claude's gloss in item 305 ("density is how many addresses the circle holds") is withdrawn as it reads against this; §2.4 now says density is the bridge. The word "density" returns to the sweep, which R118 (06:38) had taken off it; asked whether R118 is superseded.
307. **Drop "density": g is the ratio between the reader and the world** (R124; Tom 21:50: "its word soup, i need to sit with this for a day. think about each word. what do you think/"; Claude: "density" had named three things, the spacing of addresses, g, and the continuum's lack of gaps, and is the one word in the demo that cannot be pointed to; Tom 21:56: "yes, density is the culprit here. the world is always fully expressed, regardless of who is observing it. the world is breadth. the reader than must be the unit circle. between the unit circle and the world is how much of the world and how much of the reader. 0=reader. 1=world. between them is just parts of both."; 21:57: "yes, lets try to drop density if that possible. its just ratio between the reader and the world and its reflected by the ratio 0 to 1."). The demo's radius is the mix itself: r = 1 + g(B − 1) = (1 − g)·1 + g·B, (1 − g) parts reader and g parts world; the straight run Claude had flagged as its own choice is this. In *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* the word is gone from the prose (kept only in quotes and as "probability density", the Cauchy's formal term): spacing for the spacing of addresses, the ratio between reader and world for g, continuity for the continuum; §2.4's R123 paragraph rewritten as "The reader, the world, and the ratio between them"; R124 added; R81/R108 ("g is the reader's") noted as refined. In *Reader Geometry as Addressing*: §5.3 sentence rewritten, ruling 124 added with a reading note; its body's remaining uses are probability densities and Definition 8 (floored places per unit of frame, i.e. spacing), left as they are for now.
308. **Observation is not dimensional, and not three things** (R125; Tom 22:02: "lets not call observation dimensional, that is another confusing word. its something different, it needs a new word so not to confuse the situation."; Claude offered links, tiers or stages; Tom 22:05: "lets not even say observation is three things, we have been trying to make observation three dimensional to fit in the 3d box. Its just what it is, consisting of the various things."). *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* rewritten at its headline: the abstract's first paragraph ("observation from somewhere is not dimensional"), §2.3's "Three dimensions, nested" replaced by "Not dimensional" with a plain list of what observation consists of (relations, addresses, g, magnitudes; "the list is not a count"), the "three freedoms in a chain … a tower rather than a box" sentence removed, "all three"/"the three" wording replaced, and §12's claim restated. Kept: divide, sweep, point as what a reader does, and that each part is reached through the one before. In *Reader Geometry as Addressing*: ruling 125 added; rulings 107 and 112 marked withdrawn.
309. **The uniform unit circle** (R126; Tom 1 Oct 03:52: "the uniform unit circle is for situations where the breadth is known and can be divided evenly across the address space. it is for static resolved geometry where every address points to a known magnitude. in a sense, it is the view from nowhere."). The even lay needs the breadth known and divided evenly across the address space; it is static, resolved geometry, every address pointing to a known magnitude; in a sense the view from nowhere (no standpoint, every direction alike). The breadth sweep's known case is this (each breadth laid evenly from home to B; x = r · turn/(π/2)). A reader from somewhere lays from its home by the Cauchy, and with the breadth unknown by a step, never complete (R96). Open: how this sits with R124's "the reader is the unit circle"; Claude's reading, offered for correction, is that the reader's unit circle is the one laid from its home and the uniform one is the view from nowhere's. Written into *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 126.
310. **The reader is not the unit circle** (R127; Tom 1 Oct 04:05, answering item 309's open question: "the reader is not the unit circle, the uniform unit circle is read evenly across the entire address space, no point of view, it is the view from nowhwere. the unit circle is where v and h can be represented in terms of each other symetrically. it is read uniformly with no situated observer."). The unit circle is where v and h are represented in terms of each other symmetrically, read evenly across the whole address space with no situated observer: the view from nowhere (as Sep 25, R28). Withdraws "the reader is the unit circle" (R123–R124) and Claude's reading in item 309. Papers updated: the abstract, §2.3's list and the other "reader, the unit circle" appositives in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* no longer identify the reader with the unit circle; §2.4 gets "The unit circle is not the reader". Open: R124's "0=reader" put the reader at radius 1, which the breadth sweep draws as the unit circle; which end is the reader now is asked.
311. **The situated reader: the same geometry, the breadth unknown** (R128; Tom 1 Oct 04:10: "the situated reader is the same geomeitry, with one difference, the breadth is unknown."). One geometry, one difference. Breadth known: divided evenly across the address space, the uniform unit circle, no situated observer, the view from nowhere. Breadth unknown: nothing to divide by, so addresses are laid from a home by a step, the Cauchy, with a horizon (R96): the situated reader. The breadth sweep's known/unknown toggle is exactly this pair. Written into *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 128. The demo relabelled (Tom 04:12 "ok"): the buttons read "Breadth known: the view from nowhere" and "Breadth unknown: the situated reader", with the captions and lede to match.
312. **The situated reader is the fisheye** (R129; Tom 1 Oct 04:17: "the view from somewhere or the situated reader is the fish eye, not the unit circle."). The view from nowhere has the unit circle; the view from somewhere, the situated reader, has the fisheye. Demo button relabelled "Breadth unknown: the situated reader, the fisheye". Recorded in both papers.
313. **The breadth sweep is breadth known only** (Tom 1 Oct 04:19: "remove the breadth unknown button. the fisheye needs its own demo."). The known/unknown buttons and every unknown-breadth path are removed; the lede says the breadth here is known, divided evenly and read with no situated observer, the view from nowhere, and that a situated reader has the same geometry with the breadth unknown, the fisheye. The fisheye demo is to be its own page.
314. **Demo: the fisheye sweep** (`demos/fisheye-sweep.html`; R130; Tom 04:25: "lets build the fisheye demo similarly, so we can contrast the two situations. seems to me the front side is lesss then he corner and the back side is greater then the corner."). The breadth sweep's twin with the breadth unknown, same look and layout. 1 or 2 facings (the situated reader's; 4 and 8 left to the breadth sweep). No g and no breadth: the addresses are laid from home by a step of 1/n (slider, n = 2 to 40 steps to the corner), and the horizon (dashed) holds what has not been reached. Panels: the fisheye (each facing's quarter split at the corner into the front side, shaded, and the back side, lighter; one tick per step on the rim, spreading near home and crowding toward the horizon; the place, and its mirror across the corner, v and h swapped); the reading as one line, home to the corner to the horizon, the front half laid evenly in v/h 0 → 1 and the back half in h/v 1 → 0, so the front holds exactly n steps and the back's steps crowd toward the horizon without end; the Cauchy with front (s < 1) and back (s > 1) shaded, half each. Drag or touch sets the turn; arrow keys; Sweep. Tested at 1100 and 400 px, both facings, no errors. Drawing the back side on the reading line as h/v is Claude's choice, offered for correction.
315. **v and h reversed in both sweeps** (Tom 04:30: "reverse the v and h in both demos."). With one facing, home (h) is now drawn straight up and the horizon (v) to the right, as with two facings, where home is ahead and the horizons are at the sides. Readings unchanged (v/h 0 at home). Read as a swap of where h and v are drawn, not of the reading itself; offered for correction.
316. **The fisheye's marks concentrated at h** (Tom 04:32: "the fisheye marks should be reversed, concentrated at h"). The marks are mirrored across the corner: step j now sits at h/v = j/n, turn arctan(n/j), so they crowd toward home (h) and spread toward the horizon. On the reading line, the back side (h/v 1 → 0) now holds the n steps laid evenly, and the front side's steps crowd toward home without end, the reverse of item 314's wording; lede and captions changed to match, and the readout counts steps from the horizon (n at the corner). Item 314's "front finite, back unending" and the matching sentence in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 (R130) were Claude's, and now read the other way; the paper sentence is corrected.
317. **The fisheye's back side is logarithmic** (Tom 04:35: "how did you calculate the mark locations?"; Claude: step j at h/v = j/n, turn arctan(n/j), a mirror of its first even-in-v/h lay, not derived; 04:37: "the back side is logrithmic"). The marks are now readings s = v/h: front side s = n/j (j > n), concentrated at h and crowding toward home; back side s = 10^(m/n), n steps per factor of 10, crowding toward the horizon. Marks closer than about a pixel to the last drawn are skipped, so both crowdings show as solid bands. Lede and captions say so; the step readout is dropped. Open: whether the front side stays as it is (harmonic in v/h, i.e. even in h/v) or is logarithmic too, which would make the two sides mirror images across the corner.
318. **No home and no horizon in the breadth sweep** (Tom 04:51: "there is no home or horizon in the breadth sweep,"). The view from nowhere has no situated observer, so it has neither. Every home and horizon label in the breadth sweep is now named by h and v: one facing, h up and v across; two, h ahead with v right and v left; four, h front and h back with v A and v B; the ball, front h and back h, its solid circle h = 0. The turn is read from h toward v; the corner (v = h) stays. Captions and lede say "no home and no horizon". Open: the Cauchy panel, which is a situated reader's, still sits in the breadth sweep.
319. **No corner either** (Tom 04:52: "no corner either."). The breadth sweep loses its corner: the dashed corner rays on the disc, the corner label, the half-way mark on each breadth, and the corner line and label on the Cauchy. Lede and caption: "no home, no horizon and no corner". The corner stays in the fisheye sweep. Still open: the Cauchy panel in the breadth sweep.
320. **No division in the view from nowhere** (R131; Tom 04:56: "no need to divide v from h in the view from no where, it just h relative to v and v relaive to h."). The breadth sweep drops v/h: the readout shows h and v of the place, each relative to the other, and the Cauchy panel (a reading of v/h, the situated reader's) is removed; the lede says "h relative to v and v relative to h (no division, no v/h)". R131 gathers items 318–320 (no home, no horizon, no corner, no division) and is written into *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* rulings 130–131.
321. **In the fisheye, h is known and v unknown** (R132; Tom 05:06: "the fisheye, h is known, and v is unkown, expresed in terms of h."). The division belongs to the situated reader because one side is unknown: v is read as a multiple of the known h. The fisheye sweep says so: home labelled "h, known · home", the horizon "v, unknown · horizon", the readout "v = (v/h) h", and a lede sentence. Recorded in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 132.
322. **The fisheye needs no v** (R133; Tom 05:08: "so we do't even need a v in the fisheye situation."; 05:09 "yes" to applying it). The fisheye sweep drops v: the readout is the place as a count of h's ("0.555 h"); labels "h, known · home", "corner · 1 h", "∞ h · horizon"; the reading line "0 → 1 h" and "1 → ∞ h"; the Cauchy drawn over the count of h's; the mirror is the reciprocal count. Lede: every place is a count of h's. Recorded in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 133.
323. **Why the unit circle is a unit** (R134; Tom 05:23: "in the unit circle the breadth of v and h are known and equal, so we can nomralize them to 1."). Both breadths known and equal, so each is taken as 1. Set against R132–R133: in the unit circle h and v are both known, equal and normalized to 1, each relative to the other; in the fisheye only h is known and the place is a count of h's. The breadth sweep's lede says so. Recorded in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 134.
324. **The fisheye cannot be normalized** (R135; Tom 05:25: "you cant noramlize the fisheye, it is asymetric. h breadth known, v breadth unknown."). Normalizing takes both breadths known and equal (R134); the fisheye is asymmetric, h's breadth known and v's unknown. The fisheye sweep's lede says so. Consistent with *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.1 (2/π is the height of full expression, not a normalisation). Recorded in that paper's §2.4 and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 135.
325. **Both h and v inform; v has no baseline** (R136; Tom 05:27: "but we are still getting information about both h and v, just that we have no baseline about v."). Refines R133: what the fisheye lacks is a baseline for v, not information about v; v is had only in terms of h. Recorded in both papers. Asked whether the fisheye sweep should bring v back as "v, in h's" (it was removed under R133).
326. **Four situations; the Cauchy is situation 3; shapes are views from nowhere** (R137–R139; Tom 05:33–05:54, in chat, then "update the papers."). R137: "1. circle, 2. all other shapes, 3. fisheye. 4. impossible." — by what is known of the breadths: circle (known, the same every way; symmetric), all other shapes (known, varying; asymmetric), fisheye (h's known, v's not; neither symmetric nor asymmetric, one side unknown; "in the fisheye, v exists, its just not known."), impossible (neither known; pathological). R138: "so we could give v a place holder value, but it would be of type 2 with a place holderfor unknown v." "so cauchy is what happens in situation 3." Claude: the placeholder is the Cauchy's γ (a shape with v's breadth b times h's reads v/h = b · tan(turn)); the standard Cauchy is γ = 1, which places the corner; what survives any placeholder is the projective part. R139: "there is no frontside or backside in situation 1 or 2 because there is no situated place to observe them." "it goes. shapes are views from nowhere. only fisheye is view from somewhere." R104 withdrawn; its uses in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* (§2.2, §2.3, §2.4 Def. remarks, §3, the view from nowhere) marked or rewritten. R135's "asymetric" superseded. Written into that paper's §2.4 (with a four-row table) and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* rulings 137–139; the fisheye sweep's lede reworded ("one side of the relation is unknown").
327. **g in the four situations** (R140–R141; Tom 05:57: "notice that situation 1 does not have a g, because its symetric. situation 2 has a g, the ratio of situation 1 relative to situation 2."; 05:59: "yes, situation 3 has g, but its more complicated becuase v is unknown but exists."; "yes" to recording). 1, the circle: no g (normalized, every radius is the same circle; the breadth sweep's g only rescales). 2, all other shapes: g the ratio of the circle to the shape, r(θ) = (1 − g)·1 + g·B(θ); settles R127's open end (0 = the circle, 1 = the shape). 3, the fisheye: g real but toward an unseen end, read only as R96's count g = (2/π)·arctan(n·step), whose ½ (the corner) moves with the placeholder (R138); Claude's reading. 4: no g. Note for the demos: the breadth sweep is situation 1, so a g that does work needs breadths that differ between facings. Recorded in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* rulings 140–141.
328. **The corner on shapes with unequal breadths: a test** (`vh/corner.py`; Tom 06:06: "the cauchy creates an even turn, so even if the the breadh of v and h are different, you will get a unit circle as result of the eveness of the turn. its interesting that cauchy creates situation 1. so you could do the cauchy on sistuation 2 to simulate the situation of situation 3."; 06:33: "if its an elipse, there will be 2x times values after the corner than before it."; Claude: more after, but not exactly 2×; the exactly-2 is the middle at v/h = 2; 06:35: "yes. lets test this to get an exact statement about the corner."). Ellipse, h's breadth 1, v's breadth b; point at share-turn t is (cos t, b sin t), v/h = b tan t. Three lays, 20,000 addresses each. **(A) even in share-turn** (the circle's even lay carried to the shape): share before the corner = (2/π)·arctan(1/b) exactly (b = 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 5, 10: 0.5000, 0.3744, 0.2952, 0.2049, 0.1257, 0.0635; formula 0.5000, 0.3743, 0.2952, 0.2048, 0.1257, 0.0635); middle at v/h = b exactly (1.0000 … 10.0000); quartiles b·tan(π/8), b·tan(3π/8) (b = 2: 0.8285, 4.8287 against 0.8284, 4.8284): the lay is the Cauchy with γ = b. So for the ellipse with b = 2, 70.5% falls after the corner, 2.39×, not 2×; what is exactly 2 is the middle. **(B) even in length along the ellipse**: neither (b = 2: 0.3728 before, middle 1.4786). **(C) even in direction from the centre**: the corner is always the middle (0.5000, middle 1.0000 at every b): read as directions, v/h carries no breadth at all; b lives only in the magnitudes. **A reader not knowing b** (situation 3) reads it as the middle of its arrivals: median relative error 0.31, 0.11, 0.032 for n = 11, 101, 1001 (b = 2 and b = 5 alike), about 1/√n. **A placeholder γ** puts the middle at b/γ (b = 2: γ = 0.5, 1, 2, 4 give 4, 2, 1, 0.5); the cross ratio of four readings is 1.331595 under every γ. The statement, for correction: *the corner is v = h; it is the middle of a lay only when the breadths are equal; laid as the circle is, a shape with v's breadth b times h's has its middle at v/h = b and holds (2/π)·arctan(1/b) before the corner; the gap between corner and middle is the breadth ratio, which a situated reader can read from the middle of its arrivals, to about 1/√n.* Depends on the lay: true for (A), not (B) or (C). Written into the papers on Tom's "add it to the papers" (06:37) as R142: *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 (statement, table, bounds; `corner.py` in Appendix B) and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 142.
329. **Situation 3 in terms of 1, 2 and 4** (R143; Tom 06:39: "so we know can now explain situation 3 in terms of situations 1,2 and 4."; 06:40 "yes, and the serial, parallel paper."). The world is situation 2 (a shape with breadth ratio b); the lack is situation 4 on the v side only; the reader's assumption is situation 1 (γ = 1); the reading is the gap between the corner and the middle of its arrivals, b, to about 1/√n; situation 3 closes on 2 as arrivals grow, never reaching it; the cross ratio holds throughout. Written into *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and, with R137–R142, a new abstract paragraph ("Four situations"); *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 143.
330. **Serial, Parallel and Nowhere reworked around the four situations** (Tom 06:41: "yes, rework the other parts of the paper to reflect this much more nuanced understanding of the situations."; 06:49: "proceed on you interpretation of #1 and #2."). An audit by a separate agent found about 150 passages. Done: the abstract leads with the four situations; §1's reading and marks labelled the situated reader's; §2.4 opens with Definitions S0–S4 (breadth; the circle; all other shapes; the fisheye; the impossible), the 29–30 Sep definitions kept as the record with superseding notes, Prop 2.1 without g; the disc with g as radius withdrawn (§2.2, §2.5); **decision 1**: the classical Cauchy read as situation 3 with its far end assumed (g = 1 written as if v's breadth were known), R96's running total taken as situation 3's g; **decision 2**: the D1/d quantity formerly called g renamed **nearness** throughout §2.6–§10 and the kill log (61 lines), g kept for the situations' g (including §8.5–§8.8's g from arrivals); "a reader" stated once as situation 3; the view from nowhere given no standpoint and no back; "back side" kept for R130's s > 1 and "behind" used for what is hidden; §12's claims restated; §13 items added and the nearness item closed; Appendix A marks (R81, R82, R90, R107, R108, R112, R118, R121–R124, R135) and an R120 note; Appendix B pre-R131 demo notes. A second, independent check found 30 further items (leftover g-as-nearness in the abstract, the old unknown-breadth radius, R80 misattributed to nearness, γ-as-placeholder stated as ruled, "back side" in two senses); all fixed. γ as the placeholder stays marked as Claude's reading.
331. **How the library's shapes open into a fisheye** (`vh/unclose.py`, data `vh/primitives.json` and `vh/unclose.json`; Tom 06:57: "so the fisheye just un-closes the shape, with the situated reader at the midpoint of that unclosure. however, shapes wll unclose differently, the unclosed circle will have a different corner position and long tail than the unclosed ellipse for example."; then sent svg.html). The 73 single-outline shapes of svg.html's PRIMITIVES (27 compound shapes without one outline, e.g. donut, gear train, Koch, skipped; the lemniscate is degenerate from its centroid), each laid evenly along its outline (400,000 points), read from its area centroid, h = |x − cx| (home along h), v = |y − cy|, s = v/h; b = max v / max h. Four numbers per shape: share before the corner, the middle (median s), the tail s·P(S > s) at s = 40 (how the outline crosses the horizon direction) and the head P(S < e)/e at e = 0.025 (how it crosses home); each against an ellipse of the same b laid the same way. Results. **(1) Laid evenly in direction from the centre, every star-shaped shape opens identically**: corner 0.5, middle 1, tail and head 0.6368 = 2/π (the circle's); the shape is not in the reading at all. **(2) Laid along its outline, each shape opens into its own fisheye**, set by four numbers. **(3) Swapping h and v**: shapes symmetric under that swap (circle, square, rounded square, squircle, octagon, dodecagon, cross, x, L, wedge, gear, astroid, eight-point star, burst, flower, leaf, rose, hinged 4) have the corner exactly at the middle (0.5000, 1.0000) whatever their shape, and differ only at the ends: tail = head = 0.636 circle, 0.500 square, 0.570 rounded square, 0.547 squircle, 0.604 octagon, 0.662 dodecagon, 0.274 gear, 0.308 x, 1.070 eight-point star, 0.859 burst, 0.826 flower, 3.141 astroid. **(4) A quarter turn exchanges front and back**: arrow and arrow-up, corner 0.5434 / 0.4566 (summing to 1), middle 0.8308 / 1.2037 (reciprocals), tail and head exchanged (0.217, 0.762 ↔ 0.762, 0.217); likewise ellipse and tall oval. **(5) The same-b ellipse predicts the corner and middle for round convex shapes** (egg 0.5025 vs 0.5022; pentagon 0.4967 vs 0.4920; crescent 0.5220 vs 0.5233) **but not otherwise**: spoon middle 4.81 vs 2.39, bolt 2.39 vs 1.25, star6 corner 0.548 vs 0.477, snowflake 0.573 vs 0.477, cloud middle 0.514 vs 0.694; grazing shapes' tails fall to 0.02 against the ellipse's 0.34. So b alone does not set how a shape opens; the ends carry the rest. **(6) The ends read how the outline crosses the two axes**: a cusp on the axis makes them heavy (astroid 3.14; heart's tail 1.71 at its notch), a flat or grazing crossing makes them thin (graze200 0.019; gear 0.27). Reading the reader's position as the area centroid, and h along x, is Claude's; offered for correction. Written into the papers on Tom's "yes" (07:02) as R144: *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 (with a table; `unclose.py` in Appendix B) and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 144.
332. **The shape-opening test as a lab in svg.html** (Tom 07:05: "Add it as a lab. Replace a lab to keep the file size down."; 07:08, on the suggestion to replace *play the observer*: "sounds good. replace it."). The walker group's *play the observer* (`wgm`: the fogged maze game, its keys, its fog and its reveal line) is removed; the other eight walker labs are unchanged and still run. New group **opening the shapes**, button *open the shapes*, code `unc` (`?lab=unc`), placed after the Cauchy tests. It is item 331 ported to the file's own code: each single-outline primitive laid evenly along its outline, read from its area centre as v/h, with corner, middle, tail and head under each shape and the same four for the same-b ellipse on a last line. It is drawn as a lab sheet (one cell per shape, one undo brings the page back). Contour colour is the distance of corner and middle from the same-b ellipse (teal < 0.02, gold < 0.1, red beyond), stated in the menu as a distance, not a verdict. The status line gives the circle's four numbers and the control laid evenly by direction. Checked: at 400,000 points the lab's numbers match `unclose.json` on all 73 shapes to 1e-13 (default is 200,000 points for speed); no page errors at desktop and phone width; the walker maze and release still run. File 2,810,714 → 2,805,151 bytes.
333. **The opened line is scale-free; its addresses point back to the sized shape** (Tom 08:31: "So the cauchy is just the unrolled scale free version of the shape with the  addresses pointing back to the rolled up scaled version of the shape."; 08:33 "yes" to recording it with three refinements). v/h cancels a size common to h and v, so the opened line holds only ratios, and each place on it addresses a point of the closed shape, which keeps its size. Refinements (Claude's, accepted): (1) the Cauchy itself is the circle's opening — laid along the outline every other shape opens its own fisheye (item 331; laid by direction all open as the Cauchy); (2) only a common size cancels — stretching one side changes b and the opening; (3) |x|, |y| fold the four facings together, so one v/h has up to four addresses unless the facing is kept. In situation 3 the address points back to the situation-2 shape with v's placeholder (R143), so the reader follows it only in terms of h. Recorded as R145: *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and Appendix A, *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 145.
334. **The opening demo** (`demos/opening.html`, artifact SVgfPbczCK3aiQR7P4THSs; Tom 08:39 "Yes" to it). R144 and R145 in one picture: a closed shape (from the library) beside its opened line (home at 0, the corner at v = h in the middle, the horizon), with the circle's opening, the Cauchy, as a reference row. Touching the line shows every point of the outline that place addresses (rays from the reader's place, the area centre, at v/h = s in each facing); touching the shape shows where that point opens to. Size slider: the line does not move. Stretch-v slider: b and the line change. Laid along the outline / by direction: by direction every shape's marks fall on the Cauchy's. Four facings folded / one facing kept: kept, a place has one address for a shape that is star-shaped from its centre. The demo shows one thing R145 did not say: for a shape that is not star-shaped from the reader's place, a ray can cross the outline more than once, so one v/h can have more than one address within a single facing: the chevron shows two in one facing (checked by sweeping the line in 400 steps; the bolt and the check, also not star-shaped by `unclose.py`, showed at most one at that spacing). 16 shapes (chevron and check added for this).

Home goes to the middle of the line, the two corners (v = h) to the quarter marks, and the outline toward ±v to the two horizons at the ends; the line's height for each point is the fisheye reading line, sign(v)·pos(v/h). 337. **The fisheye opening's line is now the Cauchy** (Tom 10:59: "where is cauchy"; 11:00 "yes"). The first version placed opened marks by the fisheye sweep's reading line, pos(s) = s/2 up to the corner and 1 − 1/(2s) past it, which matches the Cauchy only at home, the corner and the horizon (s = 0.5: 0.25 against 0.295; s = 2: 0.75 against 0.705). The line now places each mark at its share of the quarter turn, (2/π)·arctan(v/h), the Cauchy's cumulative form (R141's g), so opening only straightens the turn and every mark keeps its share. Line labelled "the Cauchy". Taking Tom's "yes" as the first of the two options offered (make the line the Cauchy, rather than add a Cauchy line beside pos) is Claude's reading. v1 kept at /tmp/claude-0/fisheye-opening.v1.html.
338. **Proportional before the corner, logarithmic after** (Tom 11:03: "its proportional before the corner and logrithmic after the  corner."). The marks stay as laid by the Cauchy formula (even in turn, v/h = tan(k·π/(4n))), and the line now counts h's straight: a mark sits at its v/h, the corner at 1 h, ticks at 2, 3, 4 h, the line shown out to 5 h with the horizon beyond its ends. Closed, the marks are even in turn; opened, they are close together at home and spread faster and faster toward the horizon, so marks past 5 h leave the line. (Tried first: the fisheye sweep's bounded line, s/2 then 1 − 1/(2s); its spacing only varies by a factor of 2 and still read as near uniform.) v4 kept at /tmp/claude-0/fisheye-opening.v4.html.
341. **The fisheye opening rebuilt on svg.html's fisheye** (Tom 11:11: "see svg.html for correct fisheye"). svg.html's reading view (`drawCauchyAddresses`, rulings of Sep 7 and Sep 11) has n addresses even in direction, built outward from home at ±(j + ½)·2π/n; on the front half (|φ| ≤ 90°) the lens draws φ at atan(k·tan φ), spread near home and squeezed toward the horizon at 90°, which does not move; the back half is unlensed; k = HOME_UNIT·n/(2π) with HOME_UNIT = π/24, so the width at home stays one unit whatever n (k = 1.33 at n = 64, 5.33 at 256). The demo now uses exactly that: the reader's circle with h known (to home) and v's breadth unknown, the lensed front half, the unlensed back half faint, and Open straightening the lensed front half into a line by arc length (home in the middle, the corners at atan(k)/(π/2), the horizons at the ends). Items 337–340 (marks on the Cauchy line, proportional/logarithmic, even in turn, a straight count of h's) were Claude's and are superseded; their spacing was the reverse of svg.html's (crowded at home, spread outward). The placeholder slider is dropped. v5 kept at /tmp/claude-0/fisheye-opening.v5.html.

Artifact DWYyYEMQwExbQotuJSsSzD deleted; `demos/fisheye-opening.html` removed from the papers and the bundle (last copy at /tmp/claude-0/fisheye-opening.deleted.html). Applied now (26 changes plus two new notes): a note at the top of the lab pane saying every shape in the editor is known, so it works in situations 1 and 2, and that the Cauchy and fisheye labs simulate situation 3; the Cauchy tests' note says the same; the lens toggles no longer call the Cauchy chart "the observer's own"; the reading view says "a circle of directions", not "the reader's circle", and marks its fisheye click as simulated; "the unit curve, a statement about the reader" now says the register's step; the opening-the-shapes lab no longer says it opens shapes "into the fisheye"; the arc-element labs say positive and negative extent instead of in front of and behind the reader (no situated place in situations 1 and 2); horizon and corner are no longer used for other things (drift limit, the recovery's floor, the edge of the held resolution, the register's reach).
