Tom then (11:53): "the lab and edit view are for testing and exploring ideas, so it does not need to be perfect, just usable for us." So the held items stay as they are; the audit file keeps them on record.
344. **What a view from somewhere is** (Tom 11:56: "the fisheye is view from somewhere. i want to be precise what that means exactly."; 12:05: "1) yes there could be other situations for V.    the four situations are not exhaustive.   2) h is the known side by definition."; 12:05 "yes" to recording). A view from somewhere is a reading in which h's breadth is known and v's is not; h is the known side by definition (whichever side's breadth is known is called h); v arrives only as a count of h's. From that one difference: division (v/h), home / corner / horizon as counts of h, the front and back sides, the forced Cauchy, no normalising, g counting toward an unseen end. The view from nowhere (situations 1, 2) has none of these. The four situations are not exhaustive; other situations for v are possible (partly known, bounded: Claude's examples). Open: whether "somewhere" names a place or only the one-known-one-unknown difference. Recorded as R146 in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and Appendix A, and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 146.
345. **Assuming v = 2h in situation 3** (`vh/assume2.py`; Tom 12:18: "situation 2 and situation 3 are the same, just that v is not known. i wonder if it would be helpful to just assume v is known in situation 3, say 2x h. how do you use cauchy in situation 2?"; 12:20: "yes, lets see if assuming v is 2x h makes the situation simpler in any way."). Situation 2 with breadth ratio b: the circle's even turn t, stretched in v, gives readings v/h = b·tan t, a Cauchy with scale b (checked to 2e-7 at six points, b = 1, 2, 4). For b = 2: the middle is at v/h = 2, the shape's own v = h falls at 0.2952 of the turn (2/π·arctan ½), and 70% of the turn reads above one h. **Counted in binary rungs of v/h, b = 2 is the b = 1 reading slid up exactly one rung**, with identical shares (0.2048, 0.2048, 0.1392, 0.0768, 0.0394, … either side of the middle; max difference 0); b = 4 is two rungs. So v's unknown breadth is one number, the scale, and on a logarithmic count it is one offset: each doubling of the assumed breadth moves the whole reading one rung, and nothing else changes. Whether that makes situation 3 simpler: the reading's pattern is the same for every b; b = 1 is the one that puts the pattern's symmetry (the middle) at the reader's corner. Assuming 2h moves the middle one rung off the corner, so it adds a known offset rather than removing anything. Tom 12:23: "its claryifing, not simplyfing." Recorded as R147 in *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and Appendix A, and *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 147.
346. **Arctan is not the sweep** (Tom 12:25: "g is applicable in both situation 2 and 3."; 12:28: "arctan is not the sweep, it is engineering solution to bypass the sweep.    sweeps must be swept, starting from home.    arctan subverts that, and is just an engineeer convience."; 12:29: "yes, also the papers."). A sweep is swept step by step from home; arctan gives where a step would land without sweeping, and its 2/π divides by the whole quarter turn, out to the horizon a situated reader never reaches, so it brings back the known end only the view from nowhere has. g applies in situations 2 and 3: in 2 it is a position between two known ends (circle 0, shape 1, R140); in 3 it is the count of steps swept from home toward an unknown end, and (2/π)·arctan(n·step) (R96, R141) is the shortcut for it, not its definition. Arctan-computed results (the corner statement, R147's rung shares, the fisheye demos) say where a sweep would land; they were not swept. Recorded as R148: *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and Appendix A (R141's entry marked; every g = (2/π)·arctan(n·step) marked as the shortcut), *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 148 (extends 18 and 24; its two occurrences marked). Refined by Tom (12:32): "arctan can be useful, it makes sweeps act more like indexes.   but it obfiscates the actual geomtery, and should be avoided if possible because of that." Added to R148 in both papers.
347. **The working horizon** (Tom 12:35: "although the breadth of V is not known, you do know the corner.   so you could sweep to the corner."; 12:38: "thats clean if true, and i think it is.    it is a  working horizon, the one we see as situated readers, the sweep to the corner is the one we experience."; 12:39: "yes.     the backside then must be past the corner."). The corner is known in situation 3 (v reaches one h), though v's breadth is not. The sweep home → corner has two known ends, so it is proportional (n/C), with no arctan: the sweep a situated reader experiences. The corner is the working horizon [corrected by Tom 12:43: "the working horizon is the frontside." The working horizon is the whole front side, home to the corner; the corner is its edge. Corrected in R149 and R150, Definition S3, both papers]. The back side is past it: no end, counted by doublings (logarithmic), mapped onto the front by the swap s ↦ 1/s. "The horizon" alone keeps R77's meaning (v/h → ∞). Recorded as R149: *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4 and Appendix A, *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 149. Refined by Tom (12:40): "the corner then must define the frontside." Home starts the sweep but bounds nothing; the corner gives the front side its extent and so its proportions (n/C); the back side is defined only as past it. Added to R149 in both papers.
348. **Situation 3 is a hybrid** (Tom 12:41: "so situation 3 is a hybrid,   its like sitatuation 2 before the corner but adds a backside after the corner."; 12:42: "yes, and the papers."). Before the corner it behaves like situation 2: home and the corner are two known ends, and a place on the front side is a plain proportion (n/C), set anywhere between them as situation 2's g is. Past the corner it adds a back side, no end, counted by doublings. g splits at the corner: between known ends on the front, a count toward an unknown end on the back (R148's two kinds of g both live in situation 3). Situation 2 itself still has no home, corner or sides. Recorded as R150: *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* §2.4, Definition S3 and Appendix A; *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 150.
349. **A fifth situation: v approximately known** (Tom 12:54: "things started to click for me when i realized that there actually was four distinct situations or even a fifth one where V is approximately known."; 12:55: "yes, add the fifth situation, for completeness."). h's breadth known, v's known approximately. v's breadth enters as one number, the Cauchy's scale (a whole-rung offset, R147): situation 2 knows it, 3 does not, 5 knows it within bounds (e.g. v between 1 h and 4 h: a band two rungs wide). Otherwise as situation 3: corner known, front side the working horizon, back side past it; only where the shape's middle falls relative to the corner is approximate. Bounds form Claude's reading. Recorded as R151: *Serial, Parallel and Nowhere* Definition S5, §2.4, Appendix A; *Reader Geometry as Addressing* ruling 151 (its header now reads R1–R151). Corner, middle, tail and head read out for each shape match `unclose.json` (ellipse 0.589, 0.760, 0.470, 0.784); the circle's are exact (½, 1, 0.637, 0.637). Touch and pointer drag on both canvases, keys on both; no errors at desktop and phone width.

350. **Three older papers read against the situations** (Tom 13:02: "here are three older working papers. how have they changed in light of our more recent insights?"; *The Relation Space*, *What a Relation Is Made Of*, *The Unit Rule*, as uploaded; Claude read the first, two independent reads did the others). All three already had the split now ruled as R149–R150: *The Relation Space* §9, §19 ("Before the fulcrum, the relation space is proportional in terms of the reader. After the fulcrum, it is logarithmic"), *The Six Things* §14 ("the proportional and the logarithmic regimes are divided there, and the division is exact"), *The Unit Rule* §5 and §7. *The Relation Space* §9 also has the corner known to the reader ("the exact moment the writer matches him"), and makes the reader H, the known side (R146). What changed, in all three: two kinds of space where there are now five situations, and their "asymmetric space" is situation 3, not R137's situation 2; "fulcrum" for the corner; the reader sometimes treated as the circle; angle and arctan as addresses (R148). Particular: *The Six Things* §12–§15 derives the fisheye from the circle and counts rungs both ways from the corner; *The Unit Rule*'s register is logarithmic on both sides of the corner, never says how it reaches home, calls its step ratio g, and uses arctan and 2/π in §7's lens part; its race framing and real-signal tests predate the Sep 26–27 rulings. *The Relation Space* §20's "interior horizon" collides with the working horizon, and its horizon is the reader's eyes closing, where now h is known and v runs past any count. Verdicts (Claude's): revise, don't supersede, all three; *The Relation Space* needs least.


## 8j. Oct 1: the situations, the corner and the working horizon

*Gathered from items 304–350 and the rulings R126–R151 of 30 Sep – 1 Oct (numbered as in* Reader Geometry as Addressing*; written up in* Serial, Parallel and Nowhere *§2.4). Quotes are Tom's, with the time. Items above that these change carry ⚠ **Oct 1**.*

351. **The situations: what the reader knows of the two breadths** (R137, R146, R151; items 326, 344, 349). "things started to click for me when i realized that there actually was four distinct situations or even a fifth one where V is approximately known" (12:54).

     | situation | h's breadth | v's breadth | what it is |
     |---|---|---|---|
     | 1. the circle | known | known, equal to h's | symmetric; normalised to 1; the view from nowhere |
     | 2. every other shape | known | known, varying with direction | asymmetric; also a view from nowhere |
     | 3. the fisheye | known | exists, not known | neither symmetric nor asymmetric; the only view from somewhere |
     | 4. | not known | not known | impossible |
     | 5. | known | known approximately | as 3, with v's breadth known within bounds |

     **h is the known side by definition**, and **the situations are not exhaustive** (12:05: "1) yes there could be other situations for V. the four situations are not exhaustive. 2) h is the known side by definition.").
352. **The reader is not the unit circle** (R126–R127; items 309–310). "the reader is not the unit circle, the uniform unit circle is read evenly across the entire address space, no point of view, it is the view from nowhwere" (04:05). The uniform unit circle is situation 1.
353. **The view from nowhere has no home, horizon, corner or division** (R131, R139; items 318–320, 326). "no need to divide v from h in the view from no where, it just h relative to v and v relaive to h." (04:56). With no situated place, situations 1 and 2 have no front side and no back side either.
354. **What a view from somewhere is** (R146; item 344). A reading in which h's breadth is known and v's is not; v arrives only as a count of h's. From that one difference: division, home (no h's), the corner (one h), the horizon (beyond any count), the two sides, the forced Cauchy, no normalising, and g counting toward an unseen end. Open: whether "somewhere" names a place.
355. **Situation 3 is situation 2 with v's breadth unknown** (R143, R147; items 329, 345). "situation 2 and situation 3 are the same, just that v is not known." (12:18). v's breadth enters the reading as one number, the Cauchy's scale; counted in binary rungs, b = 2 is the b = 1 reading slid up one rung. Assuming a value "is claryifing, not simplyfing" (12:23). Situation 5 knows that offset within bounds (R151).
356. **How shapes open** (R144–R145; items 331, 333). Laid by direction, every shape opens as the circle does, into the Cauchy; laid along its outline, each opens its own way, by corner share, middle, head and tail. Opening a known shape is situations 1 and 2 (10:46: "this demo is for situation 1 and 2"). The opened line holds only the ratio; each place on it is an address back to the closed shape, which keeps its size.
357. **The corner statement** (R142; item 328). Laid evenly in turn, a shape with v's breadth b times h's has its middle at v/h = b, with (2/π)·arctan(1/b) of the turn before the corner; the corner is the middle only when b = 1. (The arctan form is the shortcut, item 358.)
358. **Arctan is not the sweep** (R148; item 346). "arctan is not the sweep, it is engineering solution to bypass the sweep. sweeps must be swept, starting from home. arctan subverts that, and is just an engineeer convience." (12:28). "arctan can be useful, it makes sweeps act more like indexes. but it obfiscates the actual geomtery, and should be avoided if possible because of that." (12:32). Its 2/π divides by the whole turn, out to a horizon a situated reader never reaches. Every g = (2/π)·arctan(n·step) in the papers is marked as the shortcut.
359. **The corner is known; the front side is the working horizon** (R149; item 347). "although the breadth of V is not known, you do know the corner. so you could sweep to the corner." (12:35); "it is a working horizon, the one we see as situated readers, the sweep to the corner is the one we experience." (12:38); "the backside then must be past the corner." (12:39); "the corner then must define the frontside." (12:40); "the working horizon is the frontside." (12:43). The front side, home to the corner, has two known ends, so it is swept in plain proportion, n/C, with no arctan. The corner is its edge and defines it. The back side is past the corner: no end to sweep to, counted by doublings. "The horizon" alone still means v/h → ∞ (R77). ⚠ **Oct 4:** the working horizon is the near horizon, the corner (R174), and R149 itself is ongoing research (item 369).
360. **Situation 3 is a hybrid** (R150; item 348). "so situation 3 is a hybrid, its like sitatuation 2 before the corner but adds a backside after the corner." (12:41). Before the corner, known ends and plain proportion, as in situation 2; past it, a back side that only a situated reader has.
361. **g in the situations** (R140–R141, R148, R150; items 327, 346, 348). Situation 1 has none. In situation 2, g is a position between two known ends, the circle at 0 and the shape at 1. In situation 3, g splits at the corner: a position between known ends on the front side, a count toward an unknown end on the back. "g is applicable in both situation 2 and 3." (12:25).
362. **svg.html is a situation 1 and 2 editor** (items 342–343). "its a situation 1 and 2 editor, and we used it to explore cauchy and fisheye." (11:40). Its Cauchy and fisheye labs apply the Cauchy to known shapes and so simulate situation 3. "the lab and edit view are for testing and exploring ideas, so it does not need to be perfect, just usable for us." (11:53).
363. **Formal names for the two sides** (12:44; Claude's answer, unruled). The plainest pair is **proper and improper fractions**, (0, 1) and (1, ∞), exchanged by the inversion s ↦ 1/s, which fixes the corner. "Below and above the median" works for the Cauchy only when its scale is 1. In ln(v/h), the negative and positive half-lines.

## 8k. Oct 3–4: every octave a sweep, level of detail, the ruling check

*Gathered from SPN's rulings R172–R175 and the ruling check of 4 October (SPN Appendix A, §3.3, §3.7, §3.8, §14), the pre-registered level-of-detail tests (`spn/lod-preregistration.md`), and the other session's paper* The Sweep All the Way Down *with its review. Quotes are Tom's, with the time. Items above that these change carry ⚠ **Oct 4**.*

364. ⚠ **Oct 5: its unit, ½c to 2c, and its map are superseded by R183 (item 381); breadth adding and octaves nesting carry over with a doubling as the unit. The 0.011 and the address of 1.37h were computed on R172's map.** **Every octave is a sweep** (R172; Oct 3, 20:20: "my previous comments were just speculative exploration. I think the current insight is closer to the truth.").
     - The unit is the octave with two facings, ½c to 2c around a corner c. R167's corners at ½h and 2h became its edges.
     - One projective map sends the octave to 0, 1 and ∞: ρ = 2(s − ½)/(2 − s). It is forced given that premise, and the flip sends ρ to 1/ρ.
     - A value's place is n + g. Across octaves the readings add (breadth); within one they recurse (nesting), giving an address (n; j₁, j₂, …). For example, 1.37h is (0; 1, −1, 0, 1).
     - Inside an octave the share and the sweep differ by at most 0.011, at s ≈ 1.53 and its flip 0.65: proportional in the share, a sweep in the address.
365. **Level of detail is a sweep** (the other session's note, Oct 3; SPN §3.8, not ruled). A register of step σ holds a figure of swing S, and the rung span R = S/σ is a v/h reading.
     - The record is silent below R* = 1/(1 + |u|). This holds by the rounding rule.
     - Railing starts at (C + 1)·S/(2·far). That is proved; the pre-registered C·S/(2·far) was killed by its arithmetic.
     - **Tested** (pre-registered, eight new shapes, run once):
       - what is left falls as S²/(12·R²·Var f), a quarter per doubling of R, within 30% on six of seven; the hexagon failed, with too few distinct reaches;
       - "one corner per feature" was killed: a smaller feature riding on a larger one is carried at 0.06–0.34 of S/(2A), because the larger feature dithers it;
       - "the overshoot's sign follows kinks" was killed.
     - **Proposition 3.10** (SPN, corrected Oct 4): at a coarse step the record holds home's reach in every direction, and it draws as the chart's unit curve. The square in the circle's chart and the circle in the square's chart are each other's flip, f ↦ −f, by construction.
       - The first wording called the circle "the reader's unit" and "R170's g = 0". That conflicted with R127 and R169 and is corrected (items 14, 352).
     - Recorded, not ruled: *a bounded grammar, a world of any variety*. The convention table is read as a measured map of where the reader's grammar runs out.
366. **Signs by situation** (R173; Oct 4, 04:20: "seems like v and h can be negative in situation 1 and 2 but not 3.").
     - In situation 3, v and h are magnitudes, and the facing carries direction (R37, dated 25 Sep).
     - In situations 1 and 2 the pair may be signed: its signs are the quarters of the full turn.
     - This places *The Boundary*'s two fours (B57). The quarters, cyclic and signed, belong to the views from nowhere; the facings, two and unsigned, belong to the reader.
367. **The three horizons** (R174). Tom's words:
     - 04:27: "if h = 0, that is a mathmatical horizon, but because of the readers grain, the actual horizon is much before h = 0. so we have mathmatical horizon (impossible), grain horizon (possible), so grain horizon is the far horizon, and near horizon is the corner. working horizon is same as near horizon. octaves are recursive, so they have their own horizons."
     - 04:29: "maybe the far "grain' horizon is the recursion, so you fall into this recursive situation."
     - 04:37: "if horizon is not specified, than its the common term that is used generically."
     - 04:41: "when possible specifiy which horizon you are referring to. the other unamed horizons should explain the situation without leaning on the word horizon."

     What follows:
     - The **mathematical horizon** is h = 0, never reached (R77's horizon).
     - The **far horizon** is reached, and there the reader carries into the next octave and counts octaves. Its wording, "where the grain runs out", is provisional (item 371).
     - The **near horizon** is the corner, and the working horizon is the same thing.
     - The octave edges are described without the word: where an octave's own reading starts at 0 and runs without end.
     - SPN was gone through accordingly.
368. ⚠ **Oct 5: every sweep still has its own corner, but under R183 each doubling is a whole sweep with its corner at its middle, not a corner at every 4ⁿh (item 381).** **Every octave has its own corner** (R175; Oct 4, 05:01: "recursion is the same thing all the way down, so each sweep would have its own corner by definition.").
     - This settles R167 against R172 in favour of R172.
     - Past the near horizon there is a corner at every 4ⁿh, with a front half (v in terms of the corner) and a back half (the corner in terms of v). The facing turns at every corner, which is every second doubling. For example, v = 3h reads v/(4h) = ¾, facing forward.
     - The doublings keep their places and counts.
     - R158 still holds for the far side as a whole: the octave has to be counted first, and then each half is a share of a known multiple of h (SPN §3.3's new table).
369. ⚠ **Oct 4, 05:43: resolved by R176 (item 372).** **R149 is ongoing research** (Oct 4, 04:52: "r149 is ongoing research, seem to me the near horizon is propertional and far horizon is recursive octave. readers grain is confusing to so i'm not ready to rule on that."). Not ruled.
370. **The ruling check.** Tom, Oct 4, 04:15: "this have been moving quickly, i don't know if those rulings are still valid."; 05:04: "ok , keep the 7 rulings."
     - **Confirmed:** R88 (its horizon is the mathematical one), R127, R137, R146, R148, R162, R169, R170.
     - **Scoped or revised:** R37 by R173, R77 by R174, R167 by R175.
     - **Ongoing research:** R149.
     - **This file** (as of Oct 1) is not to be cited as a source of rulings without checking SPN's Appendix A.
371. ⚠ **Oct 4, 05:43: resolved by R176; "grain" is retired (item 372).** **PRIORITY: what "grain" is** (Oct 4, 04:57: "flag this is a priority that needs to be resolved."). Not ruled.
     - "Grain" has carried three senses:
       - the **step**: the gap between neighbouring addresses;
       - the **ratio between rungs**: the base of the count;
       - the **count of addresses**, N.
     - **Candidate:** the far horizon is where a reader's step becomes as large as the reading itself. For N addresses laid evenly in turn, the relative step is smallest at the corner (about π/N) and reaches the size of the reading near v/h = 4N/π, which is the last address. By the flip, the same holds near π/(4N).
     - **Proposal:** retire "grain", as "density" was retired.
     - **Waiting on it:** R174's far-horizon wording, R149, and the carry lab (`wander/plans/carry-plan.md`).


## 8l. Oct 4–5: the lay, the gift of geometry, depth as the recursion, h is the reader

*Gathered from SPN's rulings R176–R183 (Appendix A and its new central assumption), the depth working paper (`depth-as-recursion.md`), and the plans and runs of 4–5 October: DBR, SRF, NST, DTL (`plans/`). Quotes are Tom's, with the time. Items above that these change carry ⚠ **Oct 5**.*

372. **The lay, and "grain" retired** (R176; Oct 4, 05:42: "the approach that mirrors the floating point standard is the most likely.").
     - A reader lays its addresses in plain proportion from home to the corner, one step h/N. Past the corner it lays octaves, each laid like the last, carrying at every octave edge and counting octaves.
     - **"Grain" is retired**, as "density" was. Its three senses are named apart, as a float names them: **the step**, the gap between neighbouring addresses; **the ratio between rungs**, the base of the count; **the count**, how many octaves a reader can hold.
     - The **far horizon** is where the count of octaves runs out. Toward home the limit is the step.
     - This resolves R149 (item 369) and the priority item (item 371). CAR measured it: R176's lay held every prediction (SPN §9.9).
373. **Two degrees of freedom, breadth and depth: the gift of geometry** (R177; Oct 4, 06:45–06:51: "two degrees of freedom, breadth and depth." / "dimensions imply orthogonality and access by an index. this is a sweep, arctan is not permiteed." / "i don't think the reader does much of anything, this breadth and depth degreess of freedom is the gift of geometry.").
     - They are not dimensions: neither is at right angles to the other, and neither is reached by an index.
     - Each is a sweep from home: proportion to its corner, then octaves.
     - The reader holds its unit and a count, and reads. It calculates no addresses.
374. **The chord and the sweep: the semicircle** (R178; Oct 4, 07:08–07:16: "a straight line is both 0 and 1, its fully expressed" / "the chord and the sweep both connect two points. its the semicircle." / "the reader is the pivot point of the sweep.").
     - The chord joins two points directly, fully expressed and with no turn. That is the view from nowhere.
     - The sweep joins the same two points along the arc, about the reader as the pivot, at one end of the chord. h and v meet at a right angle (Thales) and share out the chord, h² + v² = 1.
     - Even steps of the bar are even steps along the arc, so nothing is calculated.
375. **The view from somewhere sweeps; the view from nowhere indexes** (R179; Oct 4, 10:14).
     - The view from nowhere is the x, y grid: breadth known, every place reached by an index, no home, corner or horizon. The uniform unit circle is the same view held as h relative to v.
     - The view from somewhere is the breadth–depth sweep, swept from home.
     - LIN measured one shape held both ways; OCC measured where they part, where depth folds.
376. **Depth is the recursion** (R180; Oct 4, 20:07: "this is depth. its the recursion. and this demo shows how.", over *Launch from Somewhere*).
     - Depth is how many sweeps sit inside one another: the count of nested sweeps between a reader and a thing. It is not a further axis.
     - The Tangent Bridge Theorem is the sweep of one octave ("this theorem draws lines for one active, not recursively for all octaves").
     - 20:32: "tvf is for breadth, as it stands, packing leaves tighter is not depth, its breadth." The theorem's "depth d" is breadth.
     - Measured (`depth-as-recursion.md` §2): one sweep of N leaves reaches about log₂N octaves each side before its leaves run out. Nesting a sweep in every octave round-trips to machine precision.
377. **"The depth is g"** (R181, Oct 4, 20:35). Withdrawn by R182 (item 378). DBR's two runs bridged g across its depth leaves and stand as measured. They concern the expression of breadth, not depth.
378. **g is how much breadth is expressed; with depth, the format holds a surface** (R182; Oct 4, 21:02: "I don't think g has anything to do with the recursion or depth. g is how much the breadth is expressed." / 20:58: "defining the surface, not the line but complete surface if we include depth in the format.").
     - The surface is breadth across every level of the nesting at once. Each nested sweep is a line of breadth, depth is how far down a point is reached, and g on top says how much shows.
379. **The central assumption: h is the reader** (R183; Oct 5).
     - 03:43: "h is the invariant that builds the readers geometry. it is specific to that reader … the corner splits the octive in half, precisly. so h could just be the front half, the proportional side of the octive. from that, you double it, to know the size of that readers octive."
     - 03:46: "Lets promote this as the central hypothesis."
     - 03:56: "i mean, h by definition is the reader. we could say, assuming h is the reader, than the following happens."

     So h is not something to find or test (it is the known side by definition, R146). The consequences can fail, and those are listed at the top of SPN: the octave is 2h, every reader builds the complete geometry from its own h, nested octaves are the same object, no reader can read its own h.
380. **Readers do not live in octaves; reality is what the addresses meet; it is all free** (Oct 5, 04:02: "each reader will create their own geometry from this. they don't live in octaves. each reader will have the complete geometry show by this demo, so they will live in their own octive 0, and build outward from that. reality is the rings that are placed on the fingers of the addresses that are created by this geomtery. the crazy thing is that its all free, reader does not have to calcuate any of it.").
     - A dweller that one reader sees in its octave k is, to itself, in its own octave 0, with its own corner.
     - The geometry creates the addresses, and the world's magnitudes sit on them (the rings on the fingers, a metaphor only).
     - The reader calculates none of it (R177).
381. **The octave under R183** (SPN §3.3, written 5 Oct on Tom's "yes", 04:10).
     - **The reader's octave is its whole sweep, 2h.** It is a front half of one h (v as a share of h, home to the corner) and a back half of one h (h as a share of v, the corner toward the mathematical horizon): R169's "[0,1] before you and [0,1] behind you".
     - **Its children are the doublings**, 2ᵏ⁻¹h to 2ᵏh. Each is a whole sweep in its own right (`nest.py`): place in proportion in the octave's own reading, its own front half counting its own h's from its lower wall, its back half against its far wall.
     - **Each child's corner is its middle**: ⅜h, ¾h below the reader's corner; 4/3·h, 8/3·h above it. The reading is smooth through it, with the same slope from both sides to 10⁻⁶.
     - **The reader's own corner is a wall of its children**: the far wall of ½h–h and the home of h–2h. It is "the start of the next octave" (R163).
     - **The flip pairs child k with child 1 − k** and sends their readings ρ to 1/ρ (to 10⁻¹²). ½h–h and h–2h are such a pair, which is R167's "one octave from either facing".
     - **The facing turns only at the reader's own corner.** Doubling the world changes nothing on either side of it, exactly; across it, a child laid in v/h becomes one laid in h/v (SRF run 1: 0.017 for a row).
     - **Supersedes:** R172's unit and map (item 364) and R175's corners at 4ⁿh (item 368). Tables counted by doublings stand.
382. **Each octave is the 0 to π/2 sweep, and the fisheye is the sweep** (Oct 5, 04:18: "think about it. each octive is the 0 to pi/2 sweep."; 04:25: "the fisheye is just another term for the sweep. the fisheye has perfect clarity at home, gently compression until the corner and aggressive accelerationg compression after the corner.").
     - Every reader, at every level, reads its own world through its own whole sweep, its own fisheye.
     - The fisheye is the sweep, not the world's fill. The Cauchy shares (0.2048, 0.1392, 0.0768 per doubling) are what an evenly spread world looks like through it.
383. **Inside one doubling, proportion again: R163 measured** (NST, `plans/nested-same-plan.md`; five predictions, all held).
     - Read through the addresses of one doubling of a reader's sweep, an evenly spread world fills the doubling in proportion. It is half before the doubling's middle, within 0.01 from the second pair of doublings out and to four places from the fourth, and it halves from the first step, 0.25, 0.125, 0.0625.
     - Next to the reader's corner, where the fisheye's gentle compression gives way to the accelerating one within one doubling, the bend shows: 0.559 and 0.441.
     - **Withdrawn reading** (04:18): "the Cauchy belongs to the top level." It took one reader's world, seen inside a child's addresses, for the child's own reading.
384. **Each octave its own world, adding detail** (Oct 5, 03:03: "the octave ladder offer increasing levels of detail. rather then zooming, it each octave can be its own world in a sense, adding more detail"; DTL, `plans/dtl-plan.md`).
     - Each entered octave is another reader's whole sweep, holding what the levels above did not.
     - Measured, on a thing with the same shape at every level at half the amplitude: the miss inside the octave each level lives in halves per level, 0.82, 0.41, 0.20, 0.10, 0.05 (units of the first level's amplitude). Each world holds its own detail and leaves the next level's over.
     - The two chains, back and front, gave identical numbers with home and horizon swapped: they are flips of each other.
     - D1 and D4 were killed. D1 measured every level inside the deepest octave, where the upper levels cannot resolve what has not been entered. The comparison with zoom was not at equal leaves.
     - **At equal leaves** (DVZ, `plans/zoom-plan.md`): depth's miss inside the octaves it entered is about even with zoom's at level 1, about 1.7× lower at level 3 and 10–13× lower at level 7. Zoom's is lower in the reader's own octave pair. Depth spends its leaves where it has looked; zoom spends them around where it stands.
385. **The nesting is not a store for breadth** (SRF, `plans/surface-plan.md`; Tom, 03:01: "I don't think this is for surfaces, its for depth.").
     - Laying one line across nested rows, in competition with one sweep of the same leaves, measured breadth twice over. One sweep won almost everywhere.
     - The rows' error sat at their walls, where each row thins toward its own home and horizon: nesting used as storage puts a far horizon at every wall.
     - Kernel forms: the closed kernel reflected about a row's home (period π) is the cosine (chord) form, to 10⁻¹¹. Wrapping a row's home onto its horizon fails at the seam.
386. **Detail at a wall: the seam is the detail not yet entered** (WNZ, `plans/wnz-plan.md`; Tom, 04:46: "3"). A level shape that is +1 at every home and mathematical horizon, held through two five-level chains.
     - The entered octave is right at its own wall (within about a third of its inner miss); the step there is what the unentered side lacks.
     - The step never exceeded 1.22× the octave's inner miss, which still halves per level (2.00).
     - Entering the neighbour across the wall cut the step to about 0.03 of a level's amplitude (m = 2–5).
     - The two walls are not alike: the levels above already hold most of the wall value at an octave's far wall, and little at the near wall, which is the parent's own corner (steps about 0.12 against 1.3). Why is open.
     - WN2 (`plans/wn2-plan.md`, four predictions, all held): entering the octave across either wall closes it. At the parent's corner the step falls from about 1.3 to 0.003, because child 0 and child 1 are each other's flip there and their errors cancel; at the far wall, to 0.034; at level 1, to 0.07 and 0.10.
     - JMP (`plans/jmp-plan.md`): a thing that itself steps up 2 level units at every wall. One side entered holds about half the step; both sides entered hold it to about 1% (far wall) and 5% (parent's corner). At the parent's corner, flip-even detail cancels in the step and flip-odd detail adds.
     - NFW (`plans/nfw-plan.md`): the distance to the parent's nearest leaf does not explain why the levels above miss the parent's corner (killed). The miss is not monotone in W at either wall; tentatively, aliasing.
387. **Seen from its parent, a nested fisheye is symmetric about its corner** (NST run 2, `plans/nested-same-plan.md` §5; Tom, 05:04: "proceed").
     - Across the parent's doubling, the child's v/h is exactly proportional on its front half and its h/v on its back half: R183's front [0,1] and back [0,1].
     - Its clarity, from the parent, is 1 at home, ½ at its corner and 1 again at its mathematical horizon wall. The accelerating compression past the child's corner is the h/v side read in v/h.
     - Every child lies the same way, at every level.
388. **A shape is held as deep as it has detail** (SHP, `plans/shp-plan.md`; Tom, 05:25).
     - A reader standing on a unit circle reads it as the evenly spread world: s = v/h = tan(θ/2). The circle holds no depth.
     - A shape's departure from its unit circle, held per address, is what depth holds: detail at the reader's own scale in its own sweep, finer detail one or two levels down.
     - Past the level that holds a feature, each level cuts the miss 11–250×. Unlike DTL's self-similar thing, a shape runs out of detail.
     - Scale is free: the shape at twice the size reads identically.

## 8m. Oct 6: holding by octaves in the 3d bench (DEP-D, DEP-G)

*Measured in the 3d studio's depth bench (`3d/bench/depth/`, reportbase/3d#31), on content not built on any ratio: two scenes made by the studio and by Claude (a harbour village, a valley), each read along 32 sight lines as a depth reading in eye heights, and the studio's seven surface patterns read up a part's height. Each run's plan, with predictions and kills, was committed before its code, and the code before its one counted run (`D-PLAN.md`, `D-RESULTS.md`, `G-PLAN.md`, `G-RESULTS.md`). Not ruled. These test how well readings are **held**, at a fixed ratio between rungs. They are not the run of SPN's central hypothesis, readers taking their h from their parent's record.*

389. **Depth earns its leaves on scenes** (DEP-D D3, held). The bench's holder lays a sweep in every octave of the reading, then enters nested sweeps by content: an octave, an octave of that octave's own sweep, and one more level. Against the same leaves spent on whole octaves only (breadth), it was better in all 32 views, by about 7× (harbour) and 10× (valley) at the median. This is item 384's DVZ on content not built for it.
390. **The 2 is not singled out by holding** (DEP-D D1, killed; DEP-G, both predictions held).
     - The same holder with the ratio between rungs at √2, φ, 2, 3 and 4, at equal leaves. 2 was best in 7 of 32 views (√2 in 13, φ in 9). The ratio matters: the worst was at least 1.5× the best in 29 views.
     - DEP-G held the base share fixed (every ratio 14 nodes and a base of 96 to 128 leaves), since a smaller ratio had also meant a larger base. √2's lead on the harbour stayed (its median 0.21 of 2's); on the valley 2 became best (9 of 16 views).
     - So the best ratio follows the scene, not the reader: √2 for a village on a slope with its content in many octaves near the reader, 2 for a long valley floor. 3 and 4 were worst on both.
     - What it does not test: whether a reader's h settles at 2 when taken from its parent's record. The 2 of R183 comes from the corner sitting at the middle of the octave under the swap; this bench fixes that and varies only the ratio between nested octaves.
391. **On a part's height, depth does not pay where detail is even** (DEP-D D4 and D6, killed; D5, held). A part's height axis held at 48 leaves: one flattened sweep (the studio's `'flat'` kernel, a sweep with its corner at the middle), or a sweep of 16 plus four nested sweeps of 8 entered by content.
     - On all seven patterns the nested holder was worse than one sweep (1.04× to 5.8×): a pattern's detail is spread evenly up a wall, so there is nowhere deeper to go. Item 388's "a shape is held as deep as it has detail", read the other way.
     - On a smooth profile with a steep middle (tanh), the base of 16 left 10⁻⁶ where one sweep of 48 is at roundoff.
     - But where leaves were spent in regions, the sweep's children (doublings crowding toward both walls, each with its corner at its middle, item 381) beat plain halves on every pattern (0.44 to 0.82 of the error).

## 9. Open

- ~~**The sign**: are v and h magnitudes or signed?~~ **Ruled (RGA R37): magnitudes**, each on [0, 1]; direction comes from which freedom is read and which facing the reader stands in, never from a negative v or h. (The earlier note here, "an unmade ruling", *The Boundary* B324–B325, predates R37.)
- ~~**The order**~~ **Ruled: v/h.** (`freedom.js` already uses v/h; the receipts that use h/v, B328, need changing.)
- ~~**Three landmarks or two?**~~ **Ruled (R88, Sep 29): three, of two kinds**: the corner, left where it is by the facing; home and the horizon, a pair the facing exchanges (along the forward reading: the reader's own point and the vanishing point, with g read from the far end).
- ~~**Breadth: the world's range, or N?**~~ **Ruled: breadth means extent, not quantity.**
- ~~**Readers build, or only read?**~~ **Ruled: readers read; building is the view from everywhere.**
- ~~**Depth**~~ **Ruled: depth is z** (Sep 24); **refined Sep 27 (R79): depth is the forward sweep, the parallel reader's**; Tom proposes g is the depth (item 131).
- ~~**Chosen or suffered**~~ **Ruled: chosen.**
- ~~**The word "facing"**~~ **Ruled: one facing per freedom.**
- ~~**g**: hangers evaluated or placed?~~ **Ruled (R80): the share of hangers placed.** **g is just [0, 1]: no horizon, so no corner** (21:34). ⚠ Oct 1: in situation 3, g splits at the corner (item 361). Which hangers it places is open: not by the reading's landmarks; B.43 (receding) gives placement by facing.
- **g and the forward magnitude**: g is proposed as the depth (item 131); R79 makes depth the forward magnitude along each address. Is g the depth of the representation (how many forward sweeps have run), over the depths of the things (each hanger's own forward magnitude)?
- **3d sweeps**: R79 was stated for the plane; in 3d do the across sweeps run left–right and up–down (the sphere was left open Sep 25, item 46a¹⁵)? *Evidence toward yes (Sep 28, items 153–154): in* Blind, *removing the up-and-down sweep removes exactly outlines, stacking and in-plane turning, and hides more.* *And (items 155–156): the two across sweeps must be crossed at each address; laid side by side, as* Two Strips *does, only 13–14% of their pairings land on the piece both strips named.* *And (items 187–188): laid as two turns crossed, the front half gets poles and a preferred up; laid evenly over the front half (the 2d Cauchy), no direction is preferred and tilting changes nothing. Ruled (R84): in 3d the across sweeps are one even sweep over the front half.*
- **The undivided relation's [0, 1]** (item 131): the pair itself, or one number?
- **The parallel reader's visual name** (was "the outside reader's").
- ~~**g saturates near the reader**~~ **Answered (items 175–176): near order comes from the serial reader's motion (the sway, a unit-free ratio), not from g; the first border decides only where two things meet.**
- ~~**An empty reading of something there**~~ **Answered (items 171–172): a reader holds nothing there itself; a second reader's relations can place the thing on its addresses (as well as things it does read), but it cannot touch what it does not read.**
- ~~**Touch from behind**~~ **Answered (items 173–174): read by turning, paid in warning; unread only what arrives faster than the turn comes round.**
- ~~**Whose frame the world moves in**~~ **Ruled (R83, item 167): the game loop is the world, a free choice in demos; reality has no game loop.** Open in its place: the demos give every reader the same "now" (one update, read by all). Without a loop, what do readers share of *when*? (Cf. *The Common Hour*.) *Partly answered (item 168): without a shared now, relations to still things are exact and moving things smear by their motion between readings; what readers can share of* when *(items 169–170): a beat read off a shared moving landmark, the same for all at the same moment; carrying readings to it corrects straight motion, not curved.*
- ~~**What a report must carry to survive a change of standpoint**~~ **Answered for points on a row (items 159–160): the cross ratio of the target with three shared landmarks, from turns alone.** **And for points on a plane (items 161–162): two numbers from four shared landmarks, with both sweeps crossed and the plane seen from above, not at a graze.** **And off every plane (items 163–164): a reader passes a line (four numbers, from two frames of four landmarks); two readers' lines cross at the point, if the readers are spread up and down as well as across.** **And g (items 165–166): it does not cross standpoints; between readers in one place, dividing by a shared landmark's g makes it agree exactly.**
- ~~**The anamorphic Christof**~~ **Answered (items 185–186): only g and contact survive.**
- ~~**g on the ladder**~~ **Answered (items 193–194): one reader with its own g stands at the similarity rung (shape up to a scale), as do two readers passing their own turns; the unit comes only from a shared length.**
- **Is "somewhere" a place?** (R146, item 354): the definition of the view from somewhere needs no place; whether the reader's place is what makes v's breadth unknown is unruled.
- **g swept, not computed** (R148–R150, items 358–361): on the front side g is the count of steps over the steps to the corner, n/C; on the back side, what g is as a swept count toward an unknown end is not yet stated.
- **The unit rule's register on the front side** (item 350): logarithmic on both sides of the corner, where R149 has plain proportion before it; and how the register reaches home.
- **Situation 5's bounds** (R151): the form "the rung offset known within bounds" is Claude's.
- **Formal names for the sides** (item 363): unruled.
- **The turn, demoted and needed**: the Cauchy needs counting laid on the turn (*One Reader* §9, item 11). *Evidence (items 193–194): the turn is what carries the affine and similarity rungs; readers passing only cross ratios, which do not depend on the turn, stop at the projective rung.*
- ~~**PRIORITY: what "grain" is, and where the far horizon is**~~ **Ruled (R176): "grain" retired; the step, the ratio between rungs and the count; the far horizon is where the count of octaves runs out** (item 372).
- ~~**One corner past the near horizon, or many**~~ **Ruled (R175): many; every octave has its own corner** (item 368).
- ~~**R149, the front side**~~ **Resolved (R176): proportion to the corner, octaves past it** (item 372).
- ~~**A bounded sweep against carrying**~~ **Run (CAR): R176's lay held every prediction** (item 372).
- ~~**Consequence 3 of the central assumption**~~ **Tested (C3): within its own sweep a reader needs nothing from above; to place its reading in its parent's world it needs its home and one bit, its facing (R161). A wrong bit costs up to 0.17 of the octave** (item 379).
- ~~**Figures computed on R172's map**~~ **Recomputed (C3): 0.111 (was 0.053), 0.045 (was 0.011), 1.37h = (1; 1, −1, 0)** (items 364, 381).
- ~~**Depth against zoom at equal leaves**~~ **Run (DVZ)** (item 384).
- ~~**A shape on the nested structure** (R180's next step)~~ **Run (SHP, item 388).**
- ~~**Walls where the detail is not zero**~~ **Run (WNZ): no new rule needed; one entered octave leaves a seam no larger than its inner miss, and entering the neighbour closes it** (item 386). (The near wall with its neighbour entered: closed, WN2. A thing that itself steps at the walls: held when both sides are entered, JMP.) Still open: why the levels above usually miss the parent's corner (NFW killed the leaf-distance candidate).
- ~~**Which octaves a reader enters**~~ **Ruled: Tom, 5 Oct 05:04: "it should not matter."** Every octave is the same sweep, so the choice of which to enter is not part of the geometry. (Was: item 384: Tom answered "no" to both rules offered (every octave to a fixed level; only where the parent misses); DTL used the chain holding a point looked at, flagged as Claude's choice.)
- ~~**"depth must only be a 3d concept"** (Tom, Oct 4, 12:14), against R177 and R180~~ **Set aside.** Tom, 5 Oct 05:02: "3d is normally how people talk about these things, but observation is not dimensional, it is sweeps. don't worry about the 3d comments, they are not helpful." Depth is the recursion of the sweep (R180).

---

## Appendix: the names

*The same table as One Reader §1.7.*

*Tom: "a lot of the confusion is the various names we give the items in this list." The names are the corpus's own, gathered from its papers and records; which
to keep is Tom's to rule.*

| the item | in v and h | names in use across the corpus |
|---|---|---|
| the source | one push, two parts | v and h; V and H (older papers); writer and reader (*The Relation Space* since Sep 1: H reads, V is written); relatee and relator (its earlier drafts) |
| the pair held without its size | v² + h² = 1 | the hold; the census; the unit circle (demoted, §1.2) |
| the passage from home to horizon | the bar of 1 turning | the sweep; the passage; the asymmetric relation space, [0, ∞) or the share chart [0, 1); the quarter turn (its drawing, the symmetric space); the atom (retired) |
| the reader's number | s = v/h | the reading; the address; the ratio; r (*The Unit Rule*); celerity (SR). ⚠ *The Relation Space* uses s for the share v/(v + h) |
| the same, from the other seat | h/v | the swap; the other facing |
| the angle | θ = arctan(v/h) | the turn; the angle register; the blue (the dial); times 2/π, the share; ⚠ Oct 1: the shortcut, not the sweep (R148) |
| the log of the reading | z = ln(v/h) | the log register; the rungs; the ladder; octaves |
| the relation before division | v_A·h_B − v_B·h_A | the disagreement; the relation raw |
| home | h = 1, v = 0 | home, (0, 1), where nothing is written (*The Relation Space*); the keystone (Boyd's arch, §6.1b) |
| the corner | v = h; one h | the corner (the name to use); the fulcrum (older name, *The Relation Space*, *The Unit Rule*); the agreement point; the one mark; s = ½ in the share chart; 45° and 1/√2 (on the page only; the circle has no corner, R139); the edge of the working horizon (R149) |
| the horizon | h → 0 | the horizon, (1, 0), where nothing is read, at the reader's sides (R77); the rim and the limb (outside); the leg (*The Serial Cauchy*); the far end |
| the vanishing point | the far ahead, reading → 0 | the vanishing point, at home (R77, Sep 27); not the horizon |
| the reader that divides | holds v/h; sweeps left and right (R79); ⚠ Oct 5: "the fisheye is just another term for the sweep" | serial (the name to use, R45); inside (dropped); serial; Cauchy; fisheye; the unit rule; the flashlight; ~~pointing~~ (withdrawn Sep 25: a reader can't point) |
| the reader that does not | keeps magnitudes; sweeps forward, the depth (R79) | parallel (the name to use, R45); outside (dropped); parallel; arcsine; Chebyshev; reverse fisheye (provisional); the kept frame; counting |
| the reader with no limits | holds v and h with size | the view from nowhere (Sep 25; formerly the view from everywhere, god's eye); classical; the writer |
| the depth | ⚠ Oct 5: the count of nested sweeps (R180); g is how much breadth is expressed (R182), not the depth | g (proposed Sep 27; withdrawn as depth by R182); the expression; ~~the density~~ (dropped, R124); the grain (proposed Sep 19); the forward sweep (R79) |
| the situations | what the reader knows of h's and v's breadths | situations 1–5 (R137, R151): the circle, every other shape, the fisheye, impossible, v approximately known |
| the front side | home to the corner, v/h < 1 | the front side; **the working horizon** (R149); the proportional side; proper fractions (item 363) |
| the back side | past the corner, v/h > 1 | the back side; the logarithmic side; the doublings; improper fractions (item 363); ⚠ *The Unit Rule* §7 uses "back side" for the directions behind the reader |

**Collisions: one name, two things.**
1. **"Corner."** v = h in *One Reader*. *The Relation Space* and *The Unit Rule* (both since Sep 1) call v = h the
   **fulcrum**, and in *The Unit Rule* "corner" is the register's crossing (arrival = hold).
2. **"Grain."** ⚠ **Retired (R176, Oct 4): say the step, the ratio between rungs, or the count.** d = ln(b/a), the register's step (*Degrees of Freedom*; §6.4); and g, the share of hangers placed, which
   Tom proposed calling the grain on Sep 19 (rename outstanding). ⚠ **Oct 4: a priority open item** (item 371): the step, the ratio between rungs, and the count of addresses, with a proposal to retire the word. ⚠ Sep 27: Tom also calls g "the density", which collides
   with the lens's density (items 43–44, 62); if g is the depth, one of the two needs another name.
3. **"Freedom."** The half-line [0, ∞) the reading lives on (§1.2); "a freedom", the complete set of ways v and h can
   relate (*The Serial Cauchy* §2); "the facing is your freedom" (Sep 19); a degree of freedom (*Degrees of Freedom*).
4. **"Turn."** The quarter turn (the angle's range); and *the turn* as a landmark, a reversal of the magnitudes' direction
   (*The Unit Rule* §7).
5. **"v" and "h" in the dictionaries.** v is β, the speed as a share of c, while Varićak's "v = c·tanh u" (§4.1) is a
   physical velocity; on the freedom, h is the unit (§6.2).
6. **"Pair."** The relational pair (the bar's attitude) and the register pair (the held and arriving slots) are two
   objects, not two readings of one. Conflating them is the corpus's most frequent error (*The Unit Observer*, record).
7. **"Hold."** v² + h² = 1 here; in *The Unit Rule* §4, the held level, the register's stand-in for the reader ("Call
   the level in hand **the hold**, and the sample being read **the arrival**"); in *Degrees of Freedom* §1, **h** itself,
   the denominator. *The Six Things* uses none of these for the census.
8. **"g" and "grain," three ways.** *The Unit Rule* §5: g is the register's step ratio, "the grain is the generator";
   *The Six Things* §16: the grain is "the smallest ratio a reader tells apart", the base of its logarithm; and g, the
   share of hangers placed (Sep 19), which *Degrees of Freedom* calls the **expression** (renamed from the unit radius
   Sep 18). *Degrees of Freedom* is itself split on the grain: "a step in TURN, not in address", and also ln(b/a).
12. **"Breadth."** ~~The world's range of magnitudes or the array's length N~~ **Ruled (Tom, Sep 24): breadth means
   extent, not quantity.** N is the count.
9. **"Corner," a third sense.** *The Six Things* §14 uses it for the unit square's vertex: "the fulcrum sits on the arc,
   never at a vertex of the box". *The Unit Rule* also stretches it to every rung (§5) and every rule's hold match (§14a).
10. **"Facing."** *The Six Things* §4: the facing is a constituent, the turn as against the extent, accumulated over a
   traversal; §15, "the reversed facing". *One Reader* §1.3 numbers facings 1–4 as quarter rotations. Close, but the
   paper counts passages, not facings. (§6.4's outside table now says "the foreshortening" for h, to avoid a fourth
   sense.)
11. **"r."** *The Unit Rule* uses r for arrival/hold (a ratio, §7 lens) and for x − y in rungs (a log difference, §7
   proof).

13. **"Asymmetric."** *The Relation Space* and *The Six Things*: the reader's one-directional relation, which is now situation 3; R137: situation 2, a shape whose breadth varies, a view from nowhere.
14. **"Horizon" and "working horizon."** The horizon is v/h → ∞, never reached (R77); the working horizon is the front side, home to the corner, all of it reached (R149). *The Relation Space* §20's "interior horizon" (the corner as a reading) is a third use.
15. **"Corner," settled.** Collision 1 is resolved on Oct 1: the fulcrum is the older name for the corner.

**Collisions: one thing, many names.** The reader that divides has seven names and the corner six (the corner, the fulcrum, the agreement point, the one mark, the ratio at one, 45°). Each came from a
different paper, and each is right in its register (Cauchy formal, fisheye visual, semicircle mathematical, Sep 2). What
confuses is using two in one passage without saying they are one thing.
